#6822. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

#6816. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6811. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6807. The Best Black Nail Polishes for a Deep, Moody Manicure

From glossy jet black to shimmering charcoal, these shades deliver instant drama.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Black nail polish has never really been a trend—it’s a constant. It shows up on runways in Paris, backstage at rock shows, and at the desk of the most polished person in the office. It’s equal parts rebellion and refinement, and the right shade can make a manicure look like a chic, sophisticated statement or a mood.

The catch is that black polish is not one color. If you hold three bottles side by side, you’ll notice: one is a flat, inky void, another leans toward blue or red-black, and a third glitters with a faint shimmer. So you see, undertones matter. And formula changes everything. While a glossy cream gives you high-shine drama, a matte finish reads modern and architectural.

So whether you want the classic jet black that goes with everything, a vampy near-black with a hint of purple or blue, or a metallic charcoal for people who want a little sparkle with their shadow, there’s a perfect shade out there.

Ahead, we break down the best black polishes, from the one-coat wonders to the formulas that last through a full week without chipping.

A true black with zero undertone, no shimmer, and no hint of navy or brown. This shade is deep, high-gloss darkness that looks equally chic short and sharp or long and unapologetic.

This dark, almost-black navy is hard to pinpoint unless you get real close. Up close, you’ll notice the faint blue undertones that set it apart from a true black.

This creamy, smooth black gives nails rich, saturated color with a single coat. The formula glides on evenly with no streaks or patchiness, leaving a high-shine finish that looks professionally done.

Not only will this Gucci nail polish look chic on your vanity, it will also give you an undeniable edge. Luxury in a bottle, this plant-based formula delivers rich, high-impact black with a glossy, gel-like finish.

Described as a near-black raisin purple, this full-coverage cream polish is rich, opaque, and remarkably durable and will last you for nearly 10 days with Dazzle Dry’s award-winning system.

This tonal hue looks like shimmery black dusted with a veil of gray shimmer. It’s a charcoal alternative to classic black.

Short on time but not on sparkle? This Olive & June black is flecked with muted rainbow glitter and dries in about one minute.

Another speedy option, this matte polish dries in just under two minutes and leaves behind a demure, velvety finish. It’s the perfect manicure for life on the go.

A black-red is a wardrobe staple for the nails. Deep and dramatic, it reads almost black at first glance, then reveals a smoldering burgundy undertone as it catches the light.

#6803. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

#6799. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6793. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

#6791. When Does Flirting Become 'Microcheating'?

We often think of cheating as this big act of infidelity that involves sex with someone that isn’t your partner. But sometimes, even things that don’t feel like that big of a deal—liking someone’s Instagram posts consistently, sending a flirty text and then deleting the message off your phone, getting a phone number while you’re out—can break trust between a couple. This is because betrayal and infidelity can only really be defined by two people in a relationship. And sometimes, your partner would call what you’re doing secretly “microcheating.”

Therapist Debra Feinberg, LCSW explains that microcheating consists of “small, often secretive behaviors that cross an emotional line without being a full affair.” Depending on the couple, this can look like anything.

“There is no universal definition and no checklist of behaviors that automatically tell you someone is cheating and what to do next,” clinical psychologist Dr. Paulette Didia tells Cosmopolitan.

Microcheating can lead to difficult discussions and sometimes even a breakup. It essentially becomes up to you and your partner to figure out what boundaries exist in the relationship, what loyalty looks like to you, and how you move forward (or don’t) when trust is broken—no matter what the scale.

Flirting is fun! Exchanging a hot glance with a stranger or acting coy with a coworker—fun, fun, fun. But there’s a point where—if you’re in a relationship—flirting can cross the line into a form of microcheating. And oftentimes, you’re very aware of where that line is.

“The line isn’t the act, it’s secrecy and impact,” therapist and matchmaker Kristen Anthis says. “A useful test is whether you’d do it if your partner were watching, and whether it’s moving emotional energy out of the relationship.”

You may not think what you’re doing is a big deal, but if you know that it’s something your partner would feel betrayed by, that’s a pretty good indicator that they might consider it microcheating. Regardless, the best thing to do is to have a conversation about what you and your partner expect of each other when it comes to interacting with people outside of your relationship.

Licensed Marriage and Family Therapist Cheryl Groskopf explains that couples often face roadblocks around discussions of infidelity because they just assume that “loyalty” means the same thing to them as it does to their partner. The reality is that we’re all very different and think differently about what cheating is.

“Maybe you grew up watching your dad flirt with every waitress while your mom laughed it off, so to you flirting is just being friendly,” she says. “But your partner grew up watching a parent have an affair, so those same interactions feel threatening.”

This is why talking about what “respect” looks like in the relationship is important, even if you swear it’s obvious—because the reality is that it’s not.

Anthis suggests making the boundaries you draw specific and mutual—not guesses. What does microcheating look like to each of you? Now, what your partner thinks is microcheating will likely look a little (or a lot) different from your definition. You might personally think liking certain photos of other people is harmless. But once you hear your partner out and why they feel disrespected, what do you gain by continuing the action? If it really won’t affect your life and it will make your partner feel secure, there is a simple solution there.

But sometimes, the request may not be reasonable at all. Your partner may think texting a friend or interacting with someone outside of the relationship is totally unacceptable. It’s fair to disagree with this. A situation like this is when you should aim to understand whether you and your partner can see eye to eye or not.

“I think people get so focused on what their partner should or shouldn't be allowed to do that they forget to ask themselves what they actually need in a relationship,” Groskopf says. Maybe you're perfectly comfortable with certain interactions but draw the line somewhere else. The key is to just say it straightforward and address what feelings come up for both of you.

“Your partner still gets to make their own choices, and you get to decide whether those choices work for the kind of relationship you want.”

Sometimes you’ll find you can move forward—and other times you’ll learn that you and your partner aren’t really compatible.

The “micro” in microcheating doesn’t necessarily mean that you can just easily move on once a betrayal has taken place. Like with anything important in a relationship, it’s going to take a lot of communication.

If you feel like your partner’s actions reflect microcheating, Groskopf suggests understanding the behaviors before jumping into accusations. “Are they enjoying being desired? Are they sharing things with this person that they're having trouble sharing with you?” she asks.

“I think it's just as important to talk about what that behavior brought up for the hurt partner because sometimes it's activating something much deeper around trust, rejection, or feeling replaceable.”

When the person microcheating approaches the conversation with curiosity about their own behavior instead of jumping to just defend themselves, repair is definitely possible. As long as they understand how their behavior affected their partner and how to change it, the relationship can move forward with trust and respect.

“I'd pay very close attention to how someone responds when you tell them they've hurt you,” Groskopf says. A healthy relationship is one where both people take responsibility for their actions—and especially how they affect the other person. But if there is a pattern of disregard and disrespect, that might be an indicator that the microcheating is just the beginning of your problems.

Don’t let the name fool you: Nothing about “micro” cheating is small or irrelevant. Even the most seemingly harmless acts can cause huge rifts in a relationship. And at the end of the day “cheating” is still part of an equation—and no relationship where someone is betraying the other is in a good place. So you can either discuss (and uphold) boundaries, or watch your microcheating become a major problem.

#6790. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley Goes Completely Backless in a Sculptural Gucci Dress

She made a statement at the 2026 LACMA Gala

At the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala, Rosie Huntington-Whiteley was all business in the front, party in the back. Showing up to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art’s annual black-tie event—where everyone from philanthropists to actors to musicians to athletes came together for a glamorous evening of fundraising—the model was seen wearing a sleek black dress with a surprise.

Courtesy of Gucci, the evening’s sponsor, the piece appeared simple and modest at first glance—with a front defined by a sculptural, exaggerated high neckline and a slim-fit long-sleeve, floor-length silhouette. When Huntington-Whiteley turned, however, the dress was revealed to be (almost) entirely open, with a singular piece of silver hardware creating separation between a low scoop back and a high leg slit.

And, if that wasn’t enough, the whole garment was lined with dark-green sequins, which shimmered subtly in the light. She matched the shade to her diamond-and emerald earrings, which were on full display thanks to her slicked-back ponytail. She slipped into pointed-toe black pumps and stopped for photos in front of Chris Burden’s famed public art installation, Urban Light, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening.

The Art+Film Gala celebrated its 15th year tonight, supporting the museum’s ongoing efforts to expand its film programming. It honored the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic work exploring the complexity of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for a musical performance, and, like every year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

Ever wonder how much Alfonso Ribeiro and Julianne Hough make for hosting Dancing With the Stars every season? Well, some interesting information just dropped, and by that I mean we now know their exact (alleged!) salaries, including a pretty big pay discrepancy.

An insider tells The Sun that Alfonso makes $1.75 million per season for hosting the show. Meanwhile, Julianne makes $1.25 million. If you’re wondering why Alfonso is earning $500,000 more than Julianne, apparently it’s because he’s been hosting for longer, is the “head host,” and has presumably had more time for salary negotiations and raises.

“Alfonso has also been hosting since 2022 and brings his own history with the show, having won Season 19,” the source says. “His experience in Hollywood, his admiration from viewers and his established relationship with the franchise support the substantial hosting fee.”

The source adds that Julianne “brings a different kind of value to the show than Alfonso. Her value comes from her being a two-time DWTS champion and a former judge. She also has a lot of Hollywood credits under her belt. But Alfonso is considered the ‘main host’ so he gets more than Julianne.”

Meanwhile, Alfonso and Julianne’s predecessor Tom Bergeron was reportedly earning $150,000 per episode according to Celebrity Net Worth, which is about $1.8 million per season.

Alfonso and Julianne haven’t confirmed their salary details (obviously) and Dancing With the Stars keeps these things under wraps, so take all this with a grain of salt but...they’re clearly earning a lot!

#6783. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

As threat actors adopt more targeted and convincing tactics, education leaders need practical ways to reduce risk without undermining the open, connected environments their institutions depend on.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6778. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

Today's middle school students' future careers will be transformed by artificial intelligence, automation, robotics, advanced manufacturing, biotechnology, cybersecurity, and technologies that may not yet exist. Our career education policies need to prepare them for that reality.

As threat actors adopt more targeted and convincing tactics, education leaders need practical ways to reduce risk without undermining the open, connected environments their institutions depend on.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

#6777. Kylie Jenner’s Deer-Print Ashi Studio Gown Is an Unexpected (and Welcome) Surprise

From leopard-print coats to zebra bikinis, Kylie Jenner has never shied away from an animal print. But at the Los Angeles County Museum (LACMA) Art+Film Gala, she made the case for a something a little unexpected.

Joined by the likes of Alex Consani, Paris Hilton, and Nicole Kidman, Jenner arrived at the museum and stopped for photos in front of Chris Burden’s famed public art installation, Urban Light, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening. There, among the oversize street lights, she was seen wearing a low-back Ashi Studio gown in a brown-and-silver deer print.

The piece, pulled from the house’s Fall/Winter 2026 couture collection and styled by Alexandra Rose and Mackenzie Grandquist, was stitched from precisely-cut panels of fabric to fit snug around the body. The skintight number featured a halter neckline and an open back, and from the knees, it relaxed into a long, floor-sweeping skirt. A dramatic tassel detail finished things off.

The evening marked Jenner’s first time attending the LACMA Art+Film Gala, which held its inaugural edition back in 2011. The event was founded to support the museum’s ongoing efforts to expand its programming, making film more central to its exhibitions and education. Like always, this year’s version welcomed everyone from models to actors to philanthropists to musicians to athletes, bringing together the biggest names in the worlds of art, film, fashion, and entertainment. Once again, Gucci signed on as the sponsor, and Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

#6774. Merlot Nails Are the Most Luxurious Way to Wear a Red Manicure This Fall

Cozy up with a glass of wine and start reading all about this expensive-looking trend.

I’ve never been a big fan of a classic red manicure on myself, but when it comes to deeper shades, I’m all in. Burgundy nails, black cherry nails, spiced cherry nails... these are all my speed. So, when a new expensive-looking crimson shade came along this fall, I was automatically on board. Meet merlot red nails, the rich, wine-inspired shade that’s giving my usual fall manicure colors some very chic competition. (I love a good glass of red wine almost as much as I love a good fall manicure, so this trend has been especially intriguing.)

These high-gloss, juicy nails combine red with subtle purple and berry undertones for a rich, full-bodied color (much like a full-bodied bottle of merlot). Ahead, I’ll break down everything you need to know about trending merlot nails, including inspo pics and how to tips from nail artist Vikki Swan.

As the name suggests, merlot nails take inspiration from the deep, reddish-purple color of a glass of merlot wine. These manicures use burgundy and dark cherry red polishes that have subtle berry or plum undertones for a rich, multi-dimensional finish. Unlike a bright cherry red manicure, merlot is deeper and more muted, making it particularly great for fall and winter, and also making it super versatile and easy to wear. “Merlot red is a classic in its own way, just like traditional red nails, but with a deeper, more seasonal feel,” says Swan. “The darker wine tone gives the manicure a fall mood and makes it look richer and more elegant. It’s expressive, but still very versatile and easy to incorporate into different fall looks.”

The easiest way to get merlot nails is to find a polish that resembles your favorite glass of vino tinto. Look for deep wine and red-burgundy shades, recommends Swan, rather than a traditional bright red or nearly-black oxblood.

Prep is key for any manicure, but “this is especially important with dark and red shades,” says Swan. “If the cuticle and nail plate are not prepared properly, the final result will not look as clean and polished.” Dark colors emphasize imperfections on the nail and cuticle line, meaning you want to ensure dead skin is removed from the cuticle area and the nail is totally clean. “The cuticle area needs to be cleaned thoroughly so the color can be applied neatly and evenly without flooding the skin,” says Swan.

Once your nails are prepped, apply two thin coats of your favorite merlot polish and finish with a top coat of your choice. A super glossy one works well, but “I also like a matte finish with small gold accents,” says Swan. She also advocates for a merlot French manicure or micro French if you wanna incorporate a little minimalist nail art. Now, top off that glass and check out these merlot red nail ideas that will make your fingertips look absolutely regal.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6771. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6764. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6758. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6755. ‘Love Is Blind’ Star Rob Feeley Releases Lengthy Statement, Claims Fans Saw “a Small Part of a Much Bigger Experience”

Love Is Blind Season 11 star Rob Feeley is breaking his silence after a truly messy season. Quick recap: Rob got engaged to Michaella Giorgio in the Pods, but things went south during a cast-wide bar hang, where Rob told everyone he didn’t care about their relationship. Obviously, Michaella ended things, and Rob has some things to say now that he’s watched the season.

“Now that the reunion has aired and this chapter comes to a close, I’ve been reflecting on everything this experience has taught me,” Rob wrote on Instagram. “I’m truly grateful for the people I’ve met, the memories made, and the personal growth that came with both the incredible moments and the challenges. Looking back, there are moments I wish I had handled differently. There were times when I felt overwhelmed and let my emotions get the best of me. While those moments don’t reflect the person I strive to be, I recognize that they happened, and I take responsibility for how I reacted and where I could have communicated better.”

Rob went on to “acknowledge the hurt I caused Michaella,” saying “she deserved to feel heard, respected, and understood. I’m genuinely sorry for the times I fell short and for how I reacted in certain situations. My intentions don’t change the fact that my actions affected her, and I take responsibility for that.”

Rob also confirmed that he and Michaella have been in touch since the show filmed two (two!) years ago, and that they have moved on with “mutual respect.” He then reminded fans that they only saw “a small part of a much bigger experience, and not every conversation or situation can be fully understood from what makes it onto the screen.”

The burgeoning reality star ended his musings by teasing some “projects and opportunities” he’s been working on, and noted that “There’s a lot to look forward to in the coming weeks, and I’m excited to share what’s next.”

So...guess we’ll see what happens (next season of Perfect Match, perhaps?).

#6753. There Are No Small Roles for Penélope Cruz

The veteran star commands our attention in every film she appears in.

Penélope Cruz is lying down on an airport lounge sofa. She’s on her side, cheek against her hand on a striped pillow. Her hair swoops under her chin. She looks so cozy that, from this angle, her gray blazer could be a blanket. “Sorry that I’m like this…” she says softly. Her eyeglass lenses are flecked with the reflection of her screen. “I’m very jet-lagged because I was here for only a day and a half, and yesterday we shot for 16 hours.” By “here,” she means Los Angeles, and by “we,” she means the cast and crew of Nancy Meyers’s hotly awaited first feature-length film in over a decade, out in late 2027. The next day, Cruz will be on a red carpet in London celebrating La Bola Negra and Bunker, two of her four movies out this year. You can’t blame her for getting some rest while she can.

In 2026 alone, Cruz has appeared onscreen as a noir detective in Maggie Gyllenhaal’s Frankenstein reimagining, The Bride!; a sex therapist who hosts orgies and forces her neighbors to have hard conversations about marriage during an awkward dinner party in Olivia Wilde’s The Invite; a book editor in Bunker, whose husband takes on a questionable job for a billionaire (opposite her real-life spouse, Javier Bardem); and an entertainer for Spanish Civil War troops in La Bola Negra.

When the Spanish directing duo Los Javis (Javier Ambrossi and Javier Calvo) approached Cruz for the role of Nené in La Bola Negra, they worried she would turn it down because it wasn’t big enough for an actor of her caliber. The character only appears in a couple of scenes, but Cruz connected with the script and its bold portrayal of queer love across generations. “It’s incredible we still have to fight for rights that should be as normal as breathing,” she tells ELLE. She spent months preparing: She studied cuplé, a cabaret style of late 19th-century Spanish theater; practiced dance choreography; and saw a voice teacher to overcome her fear of singing onscreen. (She hasn’t done it since her role in Nine 17 years ago.) “I’m always scared of singing,” she tells me. “I think it’s the most vulnerable thing.” Her resulting performance as Nené—with 1930s skinny brows, a platinum wig, and flashy musical numbers—leaves a lasting impression. No role is too small for Cruz.

This is the woman who won an Academy Award for playing a fierce, unpredictable artist in Vicky Cristina Barcelona (making her the first and only Spanish actress to win an Oscar), but who also appears in Sex and the City 2 for literally one minute. She’s been a furiously grieving mother in Ferrari and the girl of Tom Cruise’s dreams in Vanilla Sky; she’s played many mothers (even a pregnant nun) in Pedro Almodóvar’s oeuvre, and Jack Sparrow’s ex in the Pirates of the Caribbean franchise. She’s in a unique position as one of the most revered actresses in Hollywood—with three additional Academy Award nominations under her belt, plus an Emmy nod for playing Donatella Versace—but she can just as easily slip into a jumpsuit to play a secret agent in Zoolander 2. “I’ve always felt invited to be a part of the industry, and I feel very grateful for that,” Cruz says. Some of her best-known roles might be the firecrackers and bombshells, but her sensitive, maternal, even silly characters are just as memorable. She can’t be put in a box. She proved that this year.

Cruz is still discerning with her roles, especially the ones opposite Bardem, whom she married in 2010. They have two teenagers, a son and a daughter. “We don’t want to do it too much, but once in a while it’s great,” she says of working together. “It’s just not something we want to do all the time. It would be too risky.” Bunker is the latest of their collaborations, which also include 1992’s Jamón Jamón, her first film and where they first met, and Vicky Cristina Barcelona. “We have 33 years of friendship and then a lot of years of relationship as a couple. Of course, a lot of things are the same, but you both have changed and grown together,” she says. “And that’s going to be present in the work…you can see and understand each other with one look.” Bunker’s director Florian Zeller charmed them, she says, and it helped that the shoot was in their home city, Madrid. After showing off her range this year, Cruz is already gearing up for a new kind of role: director. For the past four years, she’s been working on her first film, a documentary with a subject matter she isn’t yet ready to share. By helming the doc, which she expects to finish next year, she joins a small but growing number of female filmmakers, and she looks forward to a time when it’s no longer newsworthy. “It’s sad that it still has to be a conversation,” she says. But at the same time, “we cannot ignore it and look the other way and pretend like there is any kind of equality in those terms.”

As Cruz heads into the new year, there’s the Nancy Meyers film and other new characters on the horizon. But for now, in an airport lounge—now sitting upright, munching on snacks—Cruz is looking forward to being home with her family. And then getting back to work.

Beauty Tip: Wake up with soft, moisturized skin with Chanel Sublimage L’Extrait De Nuit.

I feel it was through a lot of characters that I discovered in all the films that I watched, but especially all the films from [Pedro] Almodóvar. I would watch his films and feel like, See, I’m not strange. I’m not strange because I say I want to act. I’m not strange because in class I’m thinking about a million things…daydreaming and fantasizing. He made me feel normal. He made me feel that it was okay to be like that, but my parents did as well. They never judged me in those terms, because I think they’re artists in their hearts.

I think more and more younger people are going to the cinema, and I cannot lose hope. And with Netflix giving La Bola Negra a record amount of time in a theatrical release [47 days to play in theaters before streaming, the company’s longest window ever], I mean, everything helps. If one does it, the other ones will do it, and then more people will do it. And then [viewers] will have more options to go and see a film you want to see in a theater. And we won’t have to close them all, because that would be such a heartbreaking thing.

[It’s good] for young generations to have the opportunity to experience that, to experience what it is to go and see a film in a dark room full of strangers where you’re not looking at your phone, you don’t have interruptions, you are just there.

Salma [Hayek Pinault], because she’s one of my best friends. And she basically took me to her house when I arrived in L.A. for my first movie. I was going to a hotel alone. And she said, “I’m going to pick you up.” She didn’t let me go to the hotel. She took me to her house. It’s like family: Even if we don’t see each other all the time, we’re really, really close.

I had this vision: I just saw the scene in my head of me singing Sade to Seth. I arrived to the set and said, “Olivia, I think at that moment before anything happens, the way that we get to connect with each other is with me singing Sade to him, and she would know the lyrics and everything.” And she said, “Oh, let’s listen to it together.” And then I played her the song, which she already knew, and she completely understood what I was talking about, and we did it. And we just felt that it was working because it was really funny and absurd. We all brought things like that.

I don’t like giving advice, but if they press me, I always say the same thing. “You are a young person beginning in your career and you really want my honest advice? Stay away from drugs.”

The fact that when you have a writer-director who is a woman, it is commented on in the interview or is talked about as something extraordinary, something that is not just normal. You don’t say that about women being in other departments in film, but you get asked about it when it’s about your director or writer-director. So that is unfortunate, that it’s evolving at such a slow pace.

I love her as a director and as a woman. She’s the coolest and she’s brilliant. She has an amazing eye, [a] great brain. She has humor. Just look at what she did, the way she shot this movie all in one apartment, the way she edited, the way she had space for all of us. What she did in the editing room was protect every single one of us. With her being the lead in the movie. She protected us and also her character, but always [based on] what is the best for the film. She always moved from that place, honestly, of what is best for the film in each one of her decisions, and I really respect her for that. Right now she’s one of my favorite directors.

I’m sure there are benefits, for example, for medicine, for finding ways and tools to protect our planet [amid] climate change. For things like that, it can be incredible. But the problem is that at the speed it’s going, there’s not enough time to regulate things so it doesn’t become something that can become a real threat.

That’s where it gets so tricky: Who’s creating that balance? Who’s creating a boundary and the limits of what we all can handle? They try to sell the benefits of it, saying, “People will have all this free time on their hands.” That always freaks me out, because I’ve been happy in my life because I’ve had purpose. Imagine if young people a few years from now won’t have to get prepared for something, or dream about something and work hard to achieve their dreams, because they would be treated like robots in a cycle of: You wake up, you eat. But what do you do with your time? What is your purpose? What do you have to invest your time and your passion into?

I worked a lot since I was a teenager, especially in my twenties and my thirties. And then I knew if I had children, that was going to be my total priority, and I always wanted to raise them myself. I always felt that was the biggest reward from having worked so hard all those years: to have the opportunity to work less after I had them, make one movie per year, or two if they’re not too long, and do it in a way where I haven’t had to be away from them.

That has been the biggest blessing, because I’ve worked mainly in the summer, where we have traveled together. Or if it’s something short, like The Invite, it was during Easter and adding one week in the beginning, one week at the end. I’m very lucky that I’ve been able to work, but also that I put all those seeds there for that moment when I became a mother, not to have to separate [from my kids] more than very short trips.

Hair by Dimitris Giannetos for L’Oréal Paris; makeup by Ariel Tejada at PRTNRS; manicure by Yoko Sakakura for OPI; produced by Alexey Galetskiy at AGPNYC.

#6751. Another ‘Secret Lives of Mormon Wives’ Star Has Quietly Filed for Divorce, Per Court Documents

Just days after Jen Affleck filed for divorce from Zac Affleck, it looks like another Secret Lives of Mormon Wives cast member is officially pulling the plug on her marriage.

The Sun reports that Miranda Hope has filed for divorce from her estranged husband Chase McWhorter, news that comes a full two years after their separation. Per the outlet, which accessed court docs, she “opted to keep the filing and other documents related to the case private” but “a child support worksheet and a notice of required classes were also filed.”

Miranda and Chase got married in 2017, and they’ve had a lot of ups and downs navigating their split and coparenting their kids. It’s unclear what triggered Miranda to formally file for divorce after two years (note: she’s been referring to herself as “divorced” for some time now), but she’s moved on and is dating Kaleb Winterburn.

Back in July, Miranda spoke about her relationship with Chase to Page Six, saying, “It’s best case scenario for being divorced and coparenting. It’s not always seamless, we still argue and there’s ebbs and flows. I think that at the end of the day, something that Chase and I both pride ourselves on is the fact that we always put our kids first. So even when there are those ebbs and flows and those lows, our number one priority is the kids. Being able to have this type of relationship where we still have a family dynamic, we still consider ourselves and refer to ourselves as a family. We’re still a family, it just looks different. You have two homes.”

Miranda also added that she doesn’t have expectations for Chase as a partner, she just expects him to be a teammate: “Our relationship took a different form and we’re both so much better for it.”

#6747. Sabrina Carpenter Would Love to Star in a 9 to 5 Movie Musical

The star talks Dolly Parton, the French New Wave, and her Johnnie Walker short with Damien Chazelle.

For Sabrina Carpenter, a song and a role each offer a different way into a story. “Acting and music have always gone hand in hand for me,” she tells ELLE exclusively. “With music, I get to tell stories through my own perspective, and with acting I get to step into someone else’s world and bring their story to life, and that is always an exhilarating feeling for me.”

Carpenter gets to move between those perspectives in a new Johnnie Walker film directed by Damien Chazelle, the Oscar-winning visionary behind Whiplash and La La Land. With the Short n’ Sweet star at its center, the clip nods to French New Wave cinema through the director’s signature motion-driven style.

The short begins with Carpenter, in a mod embellished minidress, stepping into an unusually quiet party. As someone pours her a Go Go Highball, her signature Johnnie Walker cocktail, the scene comes to life: the guests spring into motion, and Carpenter gets swept up in the revelry while a meta quartet of directors looks on. Here, she opens up to ELLE about working with Chazelle, her favorite New Wave films, and the movie musical she’d most want to star in (hint: it’s a certain blonde’s workplace comedy).

This past year has been pretty surreal. Coachella will always be a standout for me, probably for the rest of my life. I had so much fun creating that show. Also getting to perform onstage this year with Madonna and Stevie Nicks. [I’m] still processing that.

Sometimes the best moments are just spending time with the people you love, and this summer spending some downtime with my close friends and my sister, we loved our Go Go Highballs for sure.

Getting ready for my Met Gala after-party comes to mind. But I can’t always reveal all the details to you guys; there’s no fun in that.

Working with Damien was a dream! I’ve admired his work for so long, and he has a brilliant way of making even the smallest moments feel larger than life. I love how he was able to capture the humor and playfulness that come with life’s unexpected moments.

I love La La Land! I think there’s something really special about creating a short moment or scene that feels almost like a whole movie, so I’m always trying to approach these projects from a cinematic viewpoint.

Two of my favorites are The Umbrellas of Cherbourg and Cleo from 5 to 7.

Well, I just rewatched 9 to 5 and you know I’m the biggest Dolly [Parton] fan. So that would be a fun one.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.
