#6744. Florence Pugh Won’t Let Fear Hold Her Back

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The daring East of Eden star on her banner year, speaking out, and not standing for bullshit.

Florence Pugh says she “tries to be brave at least once a day.” Whether she’s raising her voice, taking on complex roles, wearing a sheer dress and an I-don’t-give-a-fuck attitude on the red carpet, or even—gasp!—showing cellulite onscreen, she is consistent in her courageousness. When we meet in late September, it has been two days since she made a powerful statement on Instagram about the lawsuit alleging gang rape at Cornell University, and then posted again when she learned her original post had been blocked by Meta. “No one fucking censors me,” she tells me. “Don’t even try. That’s just going to add fuel to the fire.”

It’s not so much that Pugh is fearless (she is still going through life as a woman, after all), it’s that she was raised with steel in her spine. “My dad has always hated bullies, and he’s always told me that if you ever see anything, you have to do the right thing. Otherwise you are just the same as them,” she says. “And I think that’s where that instinct of ‘I must speak out on this injustice’ comes from. It’s always important to stand up for people who don’t have the voice that I have. I have the ability, and I have to use it. I don’t know who I would be if I didn’t.”

Pugh is in New York City in the days ahead of the premiere of East of Eden, her latest project in perhaps the biggest year of her career to date, between the Netflix limited series and roles in three blockbusters: Spider-Man: Brand New Day, Dune: Part Three, and Avengers: Doomsday.

When she arrives for our lunch at the Greenwich Hotel, her aura precedes her. Before she’s in sight, I hear her thanking waiters, busboys, and a random guy who had the fortune of holding the door for her, purring “darling” and “love” at them as she floats by, her charming, deep British drawl on full display. When she steps into the courtyard, she waves at me from the other side as if we’re old friends.

As we tuck into some bigeye tuna and a green Caesar (she is happy to share), the conversation turns serious quickly. Pugh is arguably the highest-profile celebrity to post about the heinous accusations at Cornell, and I ask her why she felt compelled to do so. “Because I’m a woman who cares about women. I care about women I know, and I care about women I don’t know. And if we allow for stories like that to become in any way normal, then we are allowing ourselves to continue toward that destiny of abuse and rape being accepted as a norm,” she says. “Rape can never ever be a conversation that we move on from. It has to be something that we look at, face-on, and we demand justice, accountability, and for the rapist to see what they have done to someone’s life. Because if we don’t, we’re accepting it. And I don’t ever, ever, ever want to live in a world where we move on from it easily.”

I tell her I know it might seem odd to talk about Cornell in an interview that’s meant to be about her acting career, but she breaks into a smile and says, “It’s actually perfect.” You see, to her it’s all related, because she tries to be just as brave onscreen as she is off it. “I’m terrified of every single role that I’ve played. Like, ‘How am I going to do that? I don’t know if I can.’ But that’s also the thing that really thrills me, because it means that I get to come face-to-face with that feeling of fear again and override it. And the same can be said of things in life that are intimidating or are scary or require me to be brave. It’s like, ‘If I stay in this, I’m going to live in this fear. And if I punch through it, then I can overcome it.’”

Her characters demand a lot of her. They are complicated, nuanced, and often find themselves clawing out of their circumstances by tooth, nail, and, more than once, poison. At just 19 years old, and without any formal acting training, Pugh’s award-winning breakout role in Lady Macbeth (not that one) required her to embody a bride in 1865, so miserable in her cruel marriage that she murdered her way to freedom. In 2019, she played both a grieving twentysomething-turned-cult “May Queen” in Midsommar and the youngest sister in Greta Gerwig’s Little Women, managing to imbue the latter character with such humanity that she had audiences feeling empathy for Amy March (of all people), and also received her first Oscar nomination. And since her debut as Yelena Belova in 2021’s Black Widow, she has done many of her own stunts in the Marvel films, once jumping off a 2,227-foot-tall building nine times to get the shot.

Beauty Tip: For a warm vanilla scent that wraps you like a coat, try Max Mara Le Parfum Eau de Parfum.

Her range and repertoire are why Zoe Kazan, writer, executive producer, and co-showrunner of this year’s buzzy East of Eden adaptation, has said Pugh was the “only person” she envisioned for the role of the notorious Cathy Ames. “I thought it was the most gripping, sensational script, and I immediately started saying the lines out loud, so I was like, ‘Oh fuck, well, I’m Cathy,’” Pugh tells me. “I loved how she was a full human who does awful things, and has awful things done to her, and makes mistakes. I loved the question marks and the complexities to her, because that’s real to me.”

In John Steinbeck’s telling, Cathy is pure evil, and it’s unclear what made her that way (he himself seems not to know). Leave it to a woman to add depth to a female character. Kazan’s Cathy is just as ruthless, but she has a backstory that is used to add context, but never to justify her heinous acts. She’s fully formed, messy—okay, yes, likely sociopathic—and exactly the type of woman Pugh loves to take on. “I don’t know how to describe it other than I play characters that ask a lot of audience members,” Pugh says. “I think there’s something that excites, and also troubles, people about the way that I find humanity in even some of the most evil characters. I think it fascinates people.” As we take our final bites, I say people should just let women be, both in real life and onscreen. “And let us be big and beautiful,” Pugh adds. “I know that’s intimidating, but please let us, because it only makes great things.”

There’s so much scrutiny of how we look, how we dress, and how we age. And then when we do those things, they’re like, “Oh my God, she looks like this, or she’s done too much of that, or she’s gained weight, or she’s lost too much weight.” You can never get any footing right. I feel like the best work that I’ve ever done is when I truly feel ugly. When I’ve got shit makeup on and I’m either sick or ugly or having a hangover or a breakdown. I love that type of makeup because it allows me to be free.

What I love about acting is, I don’t have the typical self-consciousness of just being a woman. When I’m acting, I get to be comfortable in the body that I’m in. If the camera picks up that I have a stomach roll when I’m sitting down, then I have a stomach roll when I’m sitting down. And if the camera picks up that I’ve got cellulite on my thighs, then yeah, I’ve got cellulite on my thighs. I just give it over to the art. So performing is very freeing for me, and it always has been.

It is so crucial to be okay with how you are and who you are, because people are going to attack you regardless. They’re going to say really shitty things, no matter if you’re too small, too big, too wrinkly, too spotty, too loud, too quiet, too opinionated. They’re going to find issue with you, because that’s just the nature of the internet and people and opinions. So it’s really important to find peace with what you want to represent and who you want to be. Because then you can just be like, “Ugh, those dickheads.”

The moment I got that nomination, it’s like the pressure just vanished—there was nothing breathing down my neck anymore. I felt the energy of how people wanted to work with me change. It was not only just a pat on the back that I could feel, but it was a pat on the back that I felt like everybody could see. But it also made me even harder on myself to get another one. It’s like, I know that I’ve achieved this before. I can achieve this again.

I learned how to drive on the Warner Brothers lot when I was 19, for this pilot that I was doing called Studio City. And so I learned with the transpo captain, going around the Warner Brothers lot in a car, and I remember at the time thinking, This is so hysterical, this is so movie world. I just thought it was hysterical that I was constantly learning how to drive all of these amazing cars for so many projects, but I never had my license. [She has one now.]

The thing that I’m getting better and better at is protecting myself. Every time I work on a movie, I get more confident in the way that I can protect myself and the way that I can lead a film set, and just not stand for bullshit anymore. I’m not doing it. I’m not standing for bullshit behavior. I’m not standing for bullshit attitude. I’m not allowing for people to be rude to the crew. You just get bolder and bigger and more confident with time, and you want to be around and work with the right people. And the moment someone is making that vibe feel off, everybody feels it. I don’t want to deal with messy. It’s just about surrounding yourself with professionals who have the same way of working in mind: Show up on time, don’t be rude, don’t be a misogynist.

There’s something so magical about doing those big, stonkingly large movies with budgets and costumes and sets, because those are the films that I remember watching when I was a kid. But I’m not one thing. I also love doing really small things. I love how they both feed my soul.

I would like to have lunch with Cathy. No, actually I’d like to have lunch with Kate [what Cathy calls herself in later episodes]. But I wouldn’t be trying to fuss on her. I’d love to have dinner with Kate just because I’d be really interested to see how she talks to people outside of me. And then I’d love to have dinner with Amy March, because she’d take me to some fabulous place in Paris and I know she’d be wearing fantastic clothing. And then there’s poor Dani [in Midsommar]. I’d need to have a whole weekend with her. She’s so broken. I’d need to feed her soul food. I’d need to cook for her. I’d need to put her in front of the fire and wrap a blanket around her and just have hugs.

Lead Image: Dress, Dior. Earrings, ring, Bulgari high jewelry. Bracelet, Bulgari.

#6736. Kate Middleton's “Cinnamon Bun” Chignon Is Her Most Regal Style Yet

The royal’s updo for the National Service of Remembrance ceremony was timeless and sophisticated

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s no surprise that many beauty lovers look to the royal family for quiet-luxury and glam inspiration. Yesterday, during the United Kingdom’s National Service of Remembrance ceremony at the Cenotaph in London, Princess Kate provided another reference point worth bookmarking: an oversized, off-center chignon.

Kate’s voluminous updo peeked out from under a wide-brimmed hat, gathered at the nape of her neck and pinned on the left-hand side. Paired with minimal makeup, including rosy cheeks, a coral lip, and her signature subtle smoky eye, Kate’s beauty styling exuded pure understated elegance. To get the look at home and minimize frizz, a few generous spritzes of hairspray, like L'Oréal Paris’ Elnett Extra Strong Hold, are key in locking the chignon in place.

While the royal is often touted for her loose, beachy waves, this distinguished look is a masterclass in traditional polished princess style. Arranged in layers, the princess’s sun-kissed highlights shone through even under the hat, complemented by her structured black coat by Catherine Walker, adorned with a red poppy brooch. And as evidenced by the tone of her still-bronde hair since she debuted the color courtside at the 2026 Commonwealth Games in Glasgow last August, Kate doesn’t seem to be planning to transition to a darker shade any time soon.

#6728. How Often Should You Be Cleaning Your Leather Furniture? It's More Often Than You Think

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: A leather seat in a space designed by Jill Tran of Tran + Thomas Design Studio.

No one invests in a piece of leather furniture with plans to replace it again in just a few years. After all, a full-grain leather sofa can go decades without cracking, and minor scuffs simply add to the patina.

But the key lies in how well you care for your leather furniture. Leather may be lower maintenance than fabric upholstery, but it still needs occasional TLC to last.

We reached out to textile experts for tips on how to extend the life of leather furniture. Here's how often you should clean and condition these pieces, and how to tell the difference between normal patina and damage that needs attention.

Lisa Beach is the director of operations at BenchMade Modern, a furniture company that specializes in leather. According to her, cleaning your leather furniture regularly protects it from damage.

She recommends vacuuming your leather furniture and wiping it down with a dry cloth any time you notice dust, pet hair and dander, or crumbs starting to accumulate. This can range from every one to two weeks, depending on how often you use the furniture. The goal is to prevent these particles from settling into the furniture and possibly scratching the surface.

In general, she warns against using a damp cloth during these quick and frequent cleanings. "For semi-aniline or protected leathers, you may wipe down your furniture every few months with a damp cloth, but be sure to follow with a dry cloth to remove any excess moisture," she says. "Natural full-grain leathers are more absorbent, so we don't recommend using damp cloths or moisture unless you need to clean a spill or stain."

Leather furniture also benefits from a deeper clean with a specialized pH-balanced product every few months.

Shawn McGowen is the CEO of Leather Honey, a family-owned company that has been producing leather care products since 1968. He suggests blocking off time for a deep clean every three to six months, and following it up with a leather conditioner.

Leather naturally ages and develops a patina over time. McGowen says patinaed leather shows off a slightly darker color, softer sheen and smoother feel. You'll first notice this starting to develop in the places you sit and rest your arms most often.

"This is where your natural body oils slowly burnish the leather’s surface, giving that lived-in look that people seek after," he says. "On the flip side, damage often occurs due to neglect and looks more like cracking, flaking, stiffness, or wrinkling that doesn’t go away with cleaning. Patina comes from properly using your leather while damage comes from neglect."

Note that leather furniture can fade instead of darken with age if it sits in a particularly sunny room. However, this doesn't mean you need to keep the curtains drawn or move your leather furniture to a darker corner. McGowen says that at-home recoloring balms or leather dyes are very easy to apply to small areas.

Even if you're a very determined DIYer, it's important to know when it's best to reach out to a leather care professional to restore your furniture. Otherwise, you may end up doing more harm than good.

"When leather becomes too far gone and starts to show signs of neglect or wear such as tears, holes, large areas of discoloration, or stains, that is when the DIY becomes more difficult and potentially a waste of time," McGowen says. "These should often be handled by professional leather care specialists because they have years of experience in color matching and upholstery, and have a wide range of tools needed for larger restoration projects."

#6722. Can Rian Johnson’s New Camera App Make Your iPhone Photos Look Like His Movies?

Shooting beautiful photos with an iPhone isn’t always the simplest task. Though the cameras are serviceable for a phone, Apple’s complex, built-in processing usually spits out vibrant but flatly lit images. Enter Knives Out and Star Wars director Rian Johnson and his longtime cinematographer Steve Yedlin. With the help of programmer Eric Johnson, the filmmakers recently introduced Chromagraph, a new camera app for iOS built on the same color science Yedlin developed over many years for their movies.

Open up the app, and you’re met with a relatively simple interface—in some ways, even simpler than Apple’s stock camera app—that serves an even simpler purpose: taking great pictures. Snap a photo, and the app captures an image in RAW format, containing the unprocessed data directly from the camera’s sensor. Then it applies Yedlin’s color science, creating a very “filmic” look with more contrast, grain, halation, and an overall color curve resembling classic film stocks. The user can then adjust the look of their pictures any which way they want using the provided sliders.

Chromagraph is hardly the first replacement camera app for the iPhone—and yes, it’s iPhone-only for now. Apps like Halide and Adobe’s Project Indigo offer a lot more options and might be more fit for professional use. But to Johnson and Yedlin, Chromagraph is built to reflect the more point-and-shoot quality of a smartphone camera, keeping its UI simple and its feature set relatively small, giving the average user an easy new tool for taking beautiful photos with the device they carry around every day.

A week after it went live, Johnson and Yedlin sat down to talk about what inspired them to build Chromagraph, reverse-engineering Apple’s own processing techniques, the often misguided debate between film and digital, and the magic of taking a beautiful picture.

Rian Johnson: With love. [Laughs]. I take a lot of pictures with my phone. I'm an amateur photographer, and I have a film camera that I use. I have a few other cameras, but the reality is, this is what I walk around with all day long. I had gone through different versions of taking regular pictures and pulling them into photo editors and trying to get them to look the way I wanted them. And then I started messing around with third-party apps to take RAW photos and then taking those into editors, and that gave me a little more control. But I realized what I really wanted was to be able to take a picture with my iPhone and have it, at least to start, look the way that our movies look, which are done by taking digital cinema files and Steve applying color science to them. I asked Steve, "Could we do this on an iPhone?" And I think Steve was a little hesitant, just because the iPhone sensor, while it's very good for a phone sensor, it's still a phone-sized sensor. It's still pretty noisy. There's a lot of limitations. But come on, let's just try.

Steve Yedlin: The reason that you can get better quality images out of the phone—even though, as Rian said, the sensor is great for a phone—is this burst-merge methodology, where you're actually taking several shots at different exposures and merging them to reduce noise in the shadows and extend latitude in the highlights. And while it's computational, it's real photographic data. It's not generated or artificial. It's photo data. Since Rian wanted to make it into a camera app instead of just processing. As Eric, my coding partner, and I dug into it, we realized on the Pro iPhones, as a developer with the API, you can request Apple's ProRAW that's already done that burst-merge thing. So with the Pro phones, it was a little bit easier, but with the non-Pro phones you can’t, as a developer, request that from the camera. It just won't do it. So we made our own burst-merge for non-Pro phones. We knew that if we were actually gonna give this to other people, and it's not just for us, we know more people have non-Pro than Pro. It would be too bad if every non-Pro phone has reduced latitude and higher noise.

Yedlin: That was about nine or 10 weeks. We got 20 years of color science and then nine or 10 weeks to bundle it for iOS.

Johnson: Well, I guess we use the word "look" kind of liberally. One of the fun things about the app, I think, is that it starts out just looking good. It does have a perspective on what looking good means, and starts out like that. What I love is you're starting out with an image that doesn't look like an iPhone picture. It doesn't have the flattened HDR dynamics. If there's some kind of garish light source in the frame, it handles that the way, to my eye, a film camera would. It doesn't do any kind of digital freaking out with it. It just looks like a beautiful, mellow, very filmic image. And then, it's not even necessarily that it looks like our movies. What I've been enjoying recently is seeing how far I can push it using the different controls in the app and how many different quote-unquote looks I can actually achieve starting from the base.

Johnson: Well, I mean, again, there's a little bit of a thing right now in movies and in visual culture where “filmic look” has come to mean a very specific thing.

Johnson: The thing is, when we say “filmic look,” the reality is, movies that for nearly a century existed on film had so many different looks to them. I guess just the notion that film looks a certain way is part of a bigger thing right now that I enjoy pushing back against. But I'll say, with the app, it looks like you started with a medium like film, not an iPhone sensor.

Yedlin: If you think about the pre-digital days where there were only a couple of film stocks that were barely different from each other, and maybe one or two print stocks that were also barely different from each other, but you got all these movies look totally different ways. You're never going to say that a Gordon Willis movie looks like a Vittorio Storaro movie. There's so much craftsmanship that goes into it. And this thing isn't the photographer; it's just the photo processing.

It’s just like, we’re not going to use a different recipe if Wake Up Dead Man is more dark and gothic with horror movie elements, and Glass Onion is more sun-drenched Greece. That's in the content. That's the artist, the craftsmanship of shooting it, so they look totally different. The whole thing about a processing system is that it looks artful no matter what that content is. It's not making the “look,” or the craftsmanship, or the content, but it's always going to look artful. The app does very much use the model of how film dyes work.

Johnson: And the reality is we both grew up watching movies shot on film. We came up together shooting stuff on film. We love that look. So that's a personal subjective thing.

Johnson: On the most zoomed-out level, what you just described, “you shoot it and it looks good,” it describes when I just shoot with my Leica M6, when I shoot with a film camera. If you get the exposure right then it's kind of dialed in. It also, though, describes how I've gotten used to shooting our movies when we shoot them digitally. We don't have a digital technician on set tweaking all the stuff. What Steve does is, he's created his color science and a LUT that basically interprets what you shoot, that's preloaded on the camera. So we can really just shoot with the Alexa [digital camera] as if we're shooting with a film camera. We can just meter it, and we can go, and we'll know that what comes out is gonna be in the pocket of what we want.

The other thing about shooting with your phone: very often, you just see something, you pull your phone out of your pocket, you want to be snapping as soon as you can. So the simplicity is really reflective of what you use it for. Honestly, one of the things that we kept coming back to was how smart Apple's UI design was in their camera app, which makes sense. They're great designers. We would try to do something clever, and then it would be funky, and then we’d go back to the camera app and say, "Well, how did Apple do it?" We're also not UI designers. We're still tweaking this. We're still taking any suggestions anyone out there has. This is very much still a first pass at it.

Johnson: For me, I open it up and it works just fine. For me, it's literally just my thumb naturally tends to be here when I do this, so I want it to be there when I press this. It's things like that, until you really start spending hours using the app, or put it out there and have people using it, that you know what you need to fix.

Johnson: First of all, it's been extraordinary and really emotional for both of us to see the beautiful photos that people are taking with the app. We really made this just so we would have it. It was almost an afterthought, putting it out there. What I love, day to day, is I'm taking more photos with my phone and it makes me think about light differently.

Johnson: It's not just capturing everything. Suddenly, when I see a scene and I'm taking out my phone to shoot it, I'm thinking about the contrast of the sunlight versus the dark areas of the frame because I know there's going to be more disparity between them in this finished image. I'm thinking more filmically and photographically, which is fun.

Johnson: It’s a point-and-shoot camera. The thing is, even though we want it to look good, there's a very specific use for your phone in taking pictures, which is exactly what you describe. You don't want to be sitting there fiddling with a bunch of settings. You want to pull your phone out and snap what's right in front of your face. We weren't aiming to make a tool on the level of a SLR camera, a DSLR, or even a good mirrorless full-frame camera.

Yedlin: I think that simplicity—we don't have a million looks, the simple UI, the point-and-shoot—it's all part of the same thing, which is the joy of photography. It’s what's so fun about seeing everybody enjoying it and snapping these pictures. It's not a professional camera, and it also admits what the iPhone can and can't do. We've had some people ask for more professional, manual controls over exposure and stuff like that, but the bottom line is that at the core of it, there's only certain things that camera can do. There's both the physical restrictions, but also the restrictions of what Apple even allows you, as a third party, to request from the camera. So part of it is just stripping all that out and not pretending that it's this pro camera.

Johnson: No, we haven't really considered that. But I'm all for anything that's fun. Somebody else asked me a question, phrased in a different way: could you have a “Wes Anderson” look? Or noir, black-and-white film, or this or that? You can achieve any of those looks with just a little bit of messing the sliders around. The idea of thinking of it as this kind of magic filter you put on that instantly makes it look like that is… I don't know, that seems less fun to me.

Yedlin: Also, I think it's a misattribution. I mean, Wes Anderson shoots on the same film stocks that everybody else shoots on. It's not like when you snap the camera, it's going to make everybody stand square to the wall and make Luke Wilson be in your shot.

Johnson: That's one of the things I enjoy about it. And actually the same way that I do with film, I love the little gap between [looking through] the viewfinder and then opening up the photo. I develop my own film, and it’s a little bit like that magic moment when you pull it out of the final rinse and go, “What did we get here?” That's a kind of a feature, not a bug.

Johnson: Yeah, but also I would say there's a difference between how I think about it when we're shooting our films professionally—we want the entire film to have a consistent look, and we talk about what that look is—and as an amateur, just shooting pictures for my own pleasure, where I much more enjoy the thing I just described. I don't want to sit there in a DI suite and tune it. The notion of having it be kind of, snap it, and then, “Ooh, what did I get?” And then, “Ooh, what can I do with it?” It’s a bit more like opening a Christmas present as opposed to engineering a toy from the ground up.

Yedlin: And the other thing is, the fact that it's possible doesn't mean that you know how to do it. The reason we have all of our own complicated math is because we can't do this with any of the available sliders that would be in a color corrector or a photo editing app, or anything like that. When we shoot our films, we're seeing it live. We don't have to wait for the process. To me that's really satisfying. There's a lot of movies where the color grade is such a heavy lift, where they're inventing every shot from scratch. We save a lot of time because the overall look starts with what we want, and then we can use that limited time to really finesse it and for the colorist to do his magic.

Johnson: Yeah, it's not a moral stance. The first Knives Out was the movie where we really switched over to shooting full digital, and it was a long, long, long discussion to the point where Steve and I only made the decision because our line producer came into the office and was like, "You have to make this this decision because we have to order a camera package."

Johnson: That's a big part of it. Steve said during that discussion, "It's honestly easier for me to make digital look like film than to make film look like film.” Because it has to go through a digital backend process regardless, and all you're doing is adding steps in terms of the scan and the limitations of all that. With The Last Jedi, where we shot a combo of film and digital, we had some frustrations with both the physical film stocks and also the cameras themselves. Work was being put in jeopardy because this is literally antiquated pieces of equipment that you're working with.

I don't want to just shit-talk film or the process. It would be fun to accept some limitations and say, “No, for this one we're going to shoot on film again.” But I guess the other thing that I really fundamentally agree with Steve about, an argument that he made that really landed with me, is just to let go of the notion of giving up your control and your artistry, in terms of what you want it to look like, to a brand name. “It’ll look that way because I'm shooting on X or Y or Z.” To kind of take control back. “I want it to feel like this, I want it to look like this. How do we achieve that?” People who shot on film for years and years and years, for decades, for the past century, did that. They would bend it to their will. And I think today there's a little bit of a magic thinking that started to permeate visual culture, which is you shoot on film to get the “film look.”

Johnson: I’ve taken beautiful shots with the stock app, absolutely. As somebody who loves cameras, who loves tech, who loves amateur photography, that just goes with the territory. There's a thousand different ways to do it. There are people taking vibrant, large format digital shots that I think are astounding, and there are people taking glass plate, eight-by-ten photos with a camera from the 1800s that I think look absolutely amazing. It’s just enjoying the process and getting something you think is beautiful.

#6717. Bob Dylan Just Dropped a 39-Disc Box Set. I Listened to Every Single One

It showed up in my inbox on a Tuesday afternoon without any particular fanfare, just an email from Legacy Recordings with a link to a stream. Artist: Bob Dylan. Title: Rolling Thunder Revue — The 1976 Live Recordings. Total Tracks: 381.

The link brought me to a bare-bones Sony Music web page, a couple light grey boxes arranged against a dark grey background. A small window in the middle of the page occupied maybe 10% of the available space. Inside the window were lines of text with lengthy, baroque file names, one on top of another. You could see about five at a time, starting with the first:

Bob Dylan_The Rolling Thunder Revue The 1976 Live Recordings_G0100060414779_01_01_Visions of Johanna_USSM12603067_CT122.

Bob Dylan_The Rolling Thunder Revue The 1976 Live Recordings_G0100060414779_01_02_If You See Her, Say Hello_USSM12603068_CT122.

This, it hardly needs to be said, is not the ideal way to familiarize yourself with the 31 hours and 18 minutes of new music included in the latest box set from Bob Dylan Industries. Like previous installments of the Live Recordings series, 1976 is an exhaustive accounting of (nearly) every second of (nearly) every show and concert given by Bob Dylan on a legendary tour. If a document of a performance from this tour exists, it is here, whether you want it or not.

There’s a certain thrill to a release like this, especially for the Dylan mujahideen, those of us willing to engage in the struggle. The 1976 Live Recordings is best thought of as a choose-your-own-adventure set, allowing you the opportunity to ramble along with the Rolling Thunder Revue at whatever pace you find comfortable. You can dip in on St. Petersburg (4/23/76) for the first recorded edition of “Idiot Wind,” then mosey on to Gainesville (4/25/76) for the only-ever performance of “Dink’s Song,” then settle in for a long date with Oklahoma City (5/18/76), one of the strongest complete shows. And if you want to summit the whole mountain—well, hit “play” on Lakeland (4/18/76) and let it rip. Just remember to stay hydrated.

As exciting as the sheer volume of new Bob Dylan music is, these ultra-complete tour retrospectives can be an intimidating listening experience. This is especially true in the case of The 1976 Live Recordings, which captures not the crackerjack bonhomie of the inaugural Rolling Thunder Revue, but the second, flabbier incarnation—halitosis and head scarves rather than whiteface and roses.

Dylan caught lightning in a bottle with the first Rolling Thunder, trucking around northeastern college towns with his ramshackle rock carnival while simultaneously 1) shooting an autofictional art film, and 2) trying to piece his broken marriage back together (partially by forcing his wife to act in said art film). The looks were immaculate, the vibes heady, the performances electric. Just peep a few minutes of a 1975 “Isis”—talk about the ghost of electricity howling in the bones of a face. The music press tailed the show from town to town throughout, breathlessly reporting on the latest feats of derring-do from Bob and co-conspirators. Rolling Thunder became a viral sensation, a meme, an object of FOMO decades before the term was invented.

For Dylan, it was the culmination of a thrilling 24-month period in which he’d returned to touring, recorded three brilliant albums, and reclaimed his reputation as the most vital force in rock songs. Taken together, Planet Waves, Blood On The Tracks, and Desire, along with the Before The Flood tour in ‘74 and Rolling Thunder in ‘75, seemed a clear statement of intent. You can’t repeat the past? Whaddya mean you can’t, of course you can.

Riding high on good will and good press, Bob got the band back together the following year. It was only natural. The first Rolling Thunder had been an experiment of sorts, a burst of creativity fueled in large part by spontaneity and esprit de corps. There was a real “let’s put on a show!” energy to the proceedings. Tour dates were announced only a few days in advance, the itinerary unfolding according to the whims of the man in the wide-brimmed hat, and concerts were given not in theaters, but in New England’s stuffiest gymnasia and rotary halls.

The mercurial, DIY approach was a big part of the magic, but it also limited the tour’s reach. The sequel would be a professional operation: fully produced, well-rehearsed, hosted in cavernous coliseums across the American South. Better, boozier, browner.

Those latter two descriptors certainly applied to Rolling Thunder ‘76. As for the former, it depends on who you ask. The energy of the ‘75 shows was dialed back considerably, the wiry charge dissipating somewhere between the action on stage and the tens of thousands in the stands. It would have been impossible to reproduce the energy of a room like Plymouth’s War Memorial Auditorium (capacity 1,500) in a place like Fort Collins’ Hughes Stadium (capacity 30,000), and Bob didn’t even try. Instead, he and the band dialed up the cream quotient, turning the sound into something thicker, a little less wild. If Rolling Thunder ‘75 was a bantamweight, dodging and dancing around the ring, ‘76 was a heavyweight—slower, steadier, with a brutal left hook.

Some of the best Desire cuts (“Hurricane,” “Sara”) were stripped from the ‘76 setlists, replaced with Blood On The Tracks material that neatly mapped the emotional geography of Dylan’s crumbling marriage. Most shows included some, if not all, of the following: “Tangled Up In Blue,” a tale of missed connections and cruel fate; “You’re A Big Girl Now,” a bitter, disillusioned sequel to “Positively 4th Street;” “Idiot Wind,” the breakup song to end all breakup songs, a kiss-off to both a former lover and a former self; and “You’re Gonna Make Me Lonesome When You Go,” a sweet little number written, not coincidentally, about the young A&R rep Bob had been seeing on the side. To the surprise of no one, Sara Dylan would file for divorce early the following year.

Listening now, it’s clear that the live Blood material is the lifeblood of Rolling Thunder ‘76. Bob is invested in these songs like they’re written in his soul: performances of “Idiot Wind” and “Shelter From The Storm” highlight every show in which they appear, and “Tangled” is always interesting, even if it’s occasionally more tuneless than it ought to be. The other stuff is more hit-and-miss.

Take the 4/22/76 taping for the Midnight Special television program, which notably lacks any Tracks tracks. Instead, Bob and company meander through an abbreviated selection of greatest hits in a vain attempt at pleasing the crowd (and, presumably, network execs hoping to sell ad spots against the programming). It’s the Dylan pupu platter: a few “protest” songs, a few mid-Sixties rockers, and a couple contemporary cuts as a sop to the actual fans present. Several Rolling Thunder cast members make appearances—Joan Baez, Bobby Neuwirth, Roger McGuinn—but no one’s heart seems to be in it. Dylan was dissatisfied with the results at the time, and he refused to allow the performance to air. Now here it is a half-century later, available for all of us to judge ourselves. Turns out Bob made the right call.

I can’t help but compare The 1976 Live Recordings to last year’s salvo from BD Industries, Through The Open Window: The Bootleg Series Vol. 18 1956-1963. That volume covered plenty of well-trod ground, tracing Bob’s earliest days in the Greenwich Village folk scene. Still, producers Sean Wilentz and Steve Berkowitz managed to string the collection’s hundred-odd tracks into something coherent and compelling. The result is a release that is intentional, engaging, and, despite its marathon length, essential.

The same cannot be said about this latest set. On a personal level, I find 1976 Bob much more compelling than I do 1962 Bob—I like my Dylan aged, like a fine Cabernet. But the experience of listening to The 1976 Live Recordings is somewhat less satisfying than that of Through The Open Window. Any time I return to Sony Music’s cursed promo portal, I feel like I’m pulling the lever of a slot machine. Sometimes I hit a jackpot like “Going Going Gone,” with its revised lyrics about “diamonds and rust,” a nod—or a shot?—at Baez. Other times I get bupkis—yet another by-the-numbers edition of “Oh Sister.”

It’s worth noting that The 1976 Live Recordings, like previous releases of the 1966, 1974, and 1975 tours, isn’t being marketed as part of the Bootleg Series. The first Bootleg volume—released all the way back in 1991, now closer to the beginning of Bob’s career than it is to the present—set a high standard for classic rock box sets. Deep, voluminous, and impeccably sequenced, the inaugural release presented an extraordinary alternate history of The Great American Artist, one nearly as strong as the actual history. Even if selections were weighted toward Dylan’s first decade, a number of brilliant recordings from Bob’s wilderness years were also included. See “Lord Protect My Child,” a powerfully vulnerable ballad from the Infidels sessions. See also the alternate take of “When The Night Comes Falling From The Sky,” featuring the hot-rod stylings of Roy Bittan and Little Steven Van Zandt. “Blind Willie McTell,” “Foot of Pride,” “Series of Dreams”—the list goes on and on. My favorite Bob record shifts with the seasons; there are simply too many colors in the man’s mind to settle on one. Sometimes, however, the answer to that question is the last disc of the first volume of the Bootleg Series. It is that good.

Whatever its merits, I won’t ever say the same about The 1976 Live Recordings. Rather than present a considered set of highlights from the ‘76 run, Bob Dylan Industries has simply thrown open the gates of the vault. The words of William Mulholland upon the opening of the Los Angeles Aqueduct come to mind: “There it is, take it.” Those of us familiar with the Expecting Rain message boards have had it for years—most of it, anyway. I had my fingers crossed that someone would have rustled up a tape of Salt Lake City (5/25/76), which featured the only-ever performance of “Lily, Rosemary, and the Jack of Hearts,” but no such luck. If a tape of that show does exist, it’s still sitting in a tomb somewhere, waiting for someone to ride through the devilish cold and find it.

That, more than anything, is the central disappointment of The 1976 Live Recordings: there’s very little here that hasn’t already been in circulation for some time. Of course, bringing new and heretofore unknown delights to light was never the primary purpose. Neither was telling a story in the manner of Open Window. A release like this will surely boost the cultural footprint of Rolling Thunder ‘76, particularly amongst lower echelons of Dylan fans—those who do most of their listening through YouTube and have no idea what a FLAC is—but that’s more a happy byproduct than a raison d’etre.

The real aim here is purely legal: the maintenance and extension of copyright. EU law dictates that recordings published within 50 years of their creation are protected for 70 years following their publication. Miss that deadline and the recording enters public domain, allowing any interested parties to do with it what they will. This simple fact explains the last decade-plus of releases from Bob’s archives: The 1966 Live Recordings in 2016, The 1974 Live Recordings in 2024, and now The 1976 Live Recordings in 2026. There’s a lot of juice left to squeeze in these lemons, and Sony Music is intent on squeezing them.

The general attitude of Bob Dylan Industries seems to be that any scrap of music ever made by Dylan is worth preserving and exercising ownership over. There is a cold, calculating logic behind these box sets, one which prioritizes financial motives over the music itself. The 1976 material would benefit greatly from the deft hand of a curator, someone who had dedicated the time to separate the wheat from the chaff (releasing the complete Fort Worth show on streaming services is a poor substitute). This careful editorial approach is what makes the inaugural Bootleg Series release or Through The Open Window such powerful collections. In this case, however, it would yield an incomplete copyright arrangement. So here we are instead.

Fine by me. Even if The 1976 Live Recordings ends up being too much of a good thing—seriously, if I ever hear another “Vincent Van Gogh,” it’ll be too soon—I can’t say I’m dissatisfied with the finished product. If anything, it makes me excited for the years to come, as later Dylan tours approach their semicentennial.

I’ve long been obsessed with the 1978 shows, for instance. That year’s incarnation of Dylan featured radically glitzy rearrangements, powerhouse Street-Legal cuts, and a plethora of ruffled shirts. The At Budokan live album documented a specimen from the early days of the tour, but the best performances came at the end, as Bob refined the schmaltzy big band sound into something soulful and desperate. I’ll be first in line for a hypothetical 50-disc The 1978 Live Recordings set. Then there’s the gospel tours of ‘79 and ‘80, and the “Bob Is Back” run from ‘81, and the butt rock and eyeliner era from ‘84… It goes on and on.

The important thing is that this music was made, and now it is being made available. Copyright holders and Dylan obsessives can agree on that much, even if we’re getting different things out of a release like The 1976 Live Recordings. We always did feel the same, we just saw it from a different point of view.

#6712. The Real-Life Wardrobe of Jamie Campbell Bower, Who Only Wears Suits If They’re McQueen

“I don’t know, I suppose I’m an artist,” Jamie Campbell Bower says with a laugh, after I ask him to describe himself. He’s correct, in more ways than one. If you’ve been consuming pop culture for the last decade and change, it’s less a matter of if you’ve seen the 37-year-old Londoner before, but where. He’s acted in some of the biggest franchises in Hollywood—including Twilight, Harry Potter, The Mortal Instruments, and Stranger Things—while also nurturing a multifaceted music career. As of this month, he’ll be heading to Middle-earth as Celeborn in the third season of Amazon Prime’s epic Lord of the Rings series, The Rings of Power.

During a hectic week of Rings of Power in New York City, Bower and I spent a quiet early morning talking about the bands that helped him find his style as a teenager, the difference between performing as an actor and a musician, and why he loves playing the bad guy.

All clothing and accessories, subject’s own. Coat by MM6 Maison Margiela. Sweater by John Lawrence Sullivan. Vintage skirt. Shoes by John Lawrence Sullivan. Sunglasses by Persol (throughout). Necklace (bottom) by Kinraden. Other jewelry by Pianegonda.

Jamie Campbell Bower: It’s complexity. With any character, there has to be a level of complexity. Playing in the shadow self is really illuminating, to be Jungian. You have to know it in order for it to not take over. I found it particularly interesting to rationale and reason, even though this character was doing terrible things. I have always found it really interesting to dig away and find the why. So much of that character was really about isolation and pain and not feeling like they had a sense of belonging in the space for them. Obviously, they find this power that gives them this sense of safety. It becomes the wall, and it gives them purpose, and it hides them from the world and from being hurt. What draws me to any character is complexity and understanding the balance. It’s almost anthropological in a way.

It was amazing. I was so terrified on day one. I’m always nervous about joining any show, doing any gig. But I remember day one of actually shooting with other cast members, turning around to Nick Müller, our A-camera operator, and I just went, “Look, Nick, you’ve been here way longer than I have. If I’m in your way, just tell me to fucking move.”

I’d been a part of Twilight as well—joined that on the second movie after watching a cultural moment explode—and I was in that position again with Stranger Things. It was so big. I was joining this new world, but everyone was wicked and super kind. I made some amazing friends. I love Millie [Bobby Brown]. She’s just the best. She’s like my little sister, and also like my big sister at times as well. She’s got so much sage advice and is so brilliant at what she does and so driven. I am in awe of her, and I was from the moment I met her.

I’ve got the hand of Vecna. I took it out on Fallon [the other] night. I told this story, but I got my bag searched in Mexico City on the way in over the weekend. We pulled up and one of the people there looked at me—her eyes got big. She said, “Vecna.” I’ve got three massive fuck-off suitcases. They’re going through them and I’m like, “Look, I’ve got the hand of Vecna in here. Do you want to see it?” And then it ended up being the quickest bag search ever. I got some elf ears with me as well, both made by the same guy, Barrie Gower.

It was the summer when I was 15. I spent it wearing a visor with spiky hair, because I used to skate growing up in super baggy jeans. I remember turning up being like, “That’s not you.” Skating is a part of who I am, but I was looking to represent myself in some way, shape, or form.

Then we started this indie band when I was 16, 17. Devonté Hynes had this band called Test Icicles. I always used to look at those guys with their skinny jeans and their big hair, and I was just like, “That’s a bit of me, that.” How skinny can my jeans get? How drain pipe-y am I? That’s where I really started to find my own identity.

I’ve been fortunate enough throughout my career to meet and work with such beautiful creative people in fashion and find myself more within that, too. I was talking about it to somebody yesterday: When you try a fit on that is not you, it’s like having a bad haircut. You don’t want to do that. I’m even the same if I’m doing a photoshoot. I always go through the rail and I’ll say, “That’s not my vibe. I can’t say that I’m going to wear that.” Of course, I’m always willing to try, I’m always willing to push. But you know yourself better as you get older.

It was from Alexander McQueen. I loved the identity of that brand, but I couldn’t afford a jacket, or a suit, or even a scarf. But the smaller pieces, the rings and the wallets, were like 100 bucks. Those were the things that I would gravitate towards and buy.

More recently, within the last 10 years, I remember coming here to New York for a show that I did called Will, going to Bergdorf, and buying a couple of pairs of Comme des Garçons trousers. As they were being rung up, I was like, “All right.” It wasn’t loads—we’re not talking thousands—but it was still a lot of money. That was probably the first big clothing purchase that I ever made. And I’ve still got the trousers. I used to wear them on stage in the band as well, which is foolish.

Got a bunch of stuff from H. Lorenzo the other day—some great shirts, some great jackets. I really like how they curate the store. My most recent purchase is a lot of stuff from the new Ann Demeulemeester collection. I actually just bought a sick leather trench from it—brown leather, which is fire.

Jacket by Thom Browne. Shirt, jeans, shoes by Ann Demeulemeester.

Going back to when we were kids: Test Icicles, the Libertines, the Rakes, Franz Ferdinand, Editors. Got to meet a few of those guys throughout my travels, which was always pretty sick. Nick Cave. Warren Ellis. Nick has got such a sharp style, as does Warren. Warren’s a bit more unbuttoned. I met Warren in Venice five years ago. He was stood at the end of the pier and I was so excited to meet him, I just bounded up. I was like, “Hi, Mr. Ellis. My name’s Jamie.” And I just blurted out to him for ages, and then I was like, “Thank you so much.” He said, "Good to meet you, mate. Bye.”

I think fluidity and being myself is always the main thing. I’ve never felt wholly comfortable in a regular suit. I remember we were invited to do a show in Venice, and the band that we were in was pretty heavy. We wore suits in an environment that for us felt almost quite subversive. The music that we played didn’t really fit that. I quite enjoyed that.

But on the whole, I don’t love suits too much. I like to keep it more interesting and fluid. I’m not saying that they can’t be interesting for other people, but I’ve never been interested [in them myself]. Apart from McQueen, but that’s because something like the McQueen shoulder is so identifiable and creates such an incredible silhouette. That’s probably the only time I’ve ever really been like, “I can wear this suit for a long time.” But yeah, I avoid anything that doesn’t feel right for me.

It’s hard to say. I do feel like so often they can blend. I’ve been so fortunate in my career, being drawn to things that feel like me and getting to explore different parts of myself on film. That does have an effect on the music that I’m making at the time.

With a film or a television series, you’re saying someone else’s words and building a character, but also at times injecting your own personal experience into that. In music, they are your words. You can make a choice to be like, “I’m going to build a world in this song to express something.” Or I can just be like, “This is me.” So, they can scratch different itches, but I think that they work in symbiosis sometimes.

The original movies came out when I was 12, so they were pretty seminal. I remember being in Hong Kong on a trip when The Hobbit was coming out. I was on my own, and I went to a cinema in a place called Pacific Place to watch it. That made a really big impact on me—it felt like a fantastical world that I could see myself in.

I started auditioning for it in 2024. Walking into it, of course, I was elated. I felt so lucky and so happy, like I was a little kid again. Like, I was 11 or 12 on stage in some provincial town outside of London, and now I get to fuckin’ walk into this Lord of the Rings world and be an elf? Dude, that’s sick.

Three non-negotiable albums that everyone… Oh, bloody Nora!

I discovered Dylan when I was like 16, when we were reading [J. D.] Salinger and Allen Ginsberg and books like On the Road. Let’s go with a classic. Freewheelin’ is old-school enough. It’s cool, pre-him going electric, which I adore as well. Highway 61 Revisited is a great record too. Take your pick between those two. Chopin’s Nocturnes are amazing. That’s a beautiful, beautiful piece to get into. Then there’s Nick Cave. I did an interview before and I chose Murder Ballads, which is a great record. But I also love some of the more recent work, like Ghosteen or Skeleton Tree. Ghosteen is amazing.

Oh, Ghosteen’s wicked. Let’s go with that. I referenced the artwork for Ghosteen on my last release. My album cover was by an artist called Thomas Cole, who did a collection of paintings about the journey from birth to death. He also did paintings of the Garden of Eden. From what I have now seen, those paintings heavily inspired the artist that did the paintings for Ghosteen as well, which is kind of insane.

So, he’s from the East. It’s the swearing.

The Boots, Shoes, and Sneakers You Should Be Wearing This Fall

REI and Beams Revived Your Dad’s Built-Tough Parka From 1986

#6707. 11 Best Cowboy Boots in 2026, According to GQ Editors

You might think that cowboy boots are a privilege reserved for ranch hands and folks who can actually ride a horse. But with a little chutzpah, the best cowboy boots are just as adept on a saddle as they are on the subway. Is that so surprising? Before they blossomed into their fashionable forms, much of menswear’s most riffed on garments were rooted in function: cargo pants, work jackets, even suits. With enough time, they were adopted by civilians and adapted to their second lives as symbols of self-expression and creativity, not just job uniforms. Cowboy boots are no different.

In terms of styling, the classic move for western boots is to lean into their rougher side. Toss them on with some well-fitting jeans (preferably vintage boot cuts) and with a snap shirt. And that's still a great approach. But if you open up your mind and step outside of the saloon, the possibilities are practically endless. A slick pair of exotic leather cowboy boots matched with a ‘70s-inspired suit screams filthy rich oil tycoon if not Alessandro Michele-era Gucci. Or, why not mix up a preppy pair of chinos with a grungy graphic tee and some roughed-up suede ropers? The point is, you don’t have to pretend to be a cowboy to wear their boots. So treat them like any other pair of shoes in your closet and wear them however you want. Hell, we’d go so far as to call them an essential pair of boots for every wardrobe.

And if you’re having trouble, well, that’s why you’re here. We’ve wrangled together the best cowboy boots west—and east—of the Mississippi, from the budget-friendly to the ostentatious and every vibe in-between.

The good folks at Tecovas are living, breathing proof that direct-to-consumer brands aren’t all operated by algorithms tasked with solving imaginary problems. The DTC upstart might be the youngest of the bootmakers on this list, but it's quickly endeared itself to budget-strapped swashbucklers with a hankering for democratically-priced kicks.

Take, for example, the Cartwright, a handsome riff on a classic western silhouette made in Leon, Mexico, a bona fide cobbling capital of the world with over a century of expertise to draw on. It boasts hand-laid cording at the shaft, a resoleable Goodyear-welt construction, and a slew of colors and leathers to choose from, including genuine cowhide and goat leather. They’re not the cheapest boots on this list, but the relatively approachable price belies the craftsmanship involved. For $375, they’re one small step up from our favorite budget cowboy boots—but one giant leap in overall quality.

You can think of roper boots as the chilled-out version of the average western boot. Originally designed for calf roping, roper boots feature a shorter shaft and a shorter, squared off heel. These details made it easier for cowboys to dismount their horse and run after the calves they roped. Coincidentally, they also make roper boots easier to walk in which is why some people prefer them over other cowboy boots for everyday wear.

Our pick for the best roper boots currently goes to Red Wing. (Perhaps no surprise, given the brand also makes our favorite trend-proof boots.) Part of Red Wing's Heritage line, they're crafted with premium S.B. Foot Tanning Company leather, and feature Goodyear welt construction so you can have them resoled and keep ‘em going for a long time. Plus, if you’re just dipping your toes into the cowboy boot world, the minimalist uppers forgo the decorative western stitching which makes them easier to style.

If you’re after that western look but need a pair of boots that can handle the demands of a jobsite, a lot of guys will tell you to look to Ariat. That’s because they manage to blend classic cowboy style with modern-day features, and a palatable price point in perfect proportion. While they do offer a range of work boots in lace-up styles, their Groundbreaker boots are a standout with classic cowboy boot looks.

They’re made of full-grain leather, include a supportive and comfortable insole, and meet ASTM standards for slip resistance and electrical safety. Because it’s among Ariat’s most popular styles, it’s also available in a wide range of colorways, toe shapes, and even steel toe versions. Some more traditional cowboys will poo-poo the fact that these aren’t made stateside, but if you’re on a budget, you’ll get more than what you pay for.

Cowboy boots aren’t all rough-and-tumble affairs. In fact, the right pair can easily strut the red carpet or waltz into a fancy restaurant with panache. You’ll want a pair with leather that shines up well and in a darker colorway. For date nights and more formal affairs, these Tony Lamas will impress thanks to the exotic lizard skin uppers and low-key embroidery. They’re the kind of boots that can play well for the in-laws with a tailored suit or play a rock-’n’-roll star with some vintage jeans and a tight white tee.

These days, most boot brands walk in the shadows of one brand: Lucchese. They’ve been outfitting American presidents, high-profile celebs, and the Dallas Cowboy Cheerleaders for decades and it’s all thanks to their commitment to high-quality craftsmanship. Though any pair of Lucchese’s would earn you more than a few approving head nods, the most coveted are the boots that they make in their Texas workshop.

Using a range of full-grain leathers and exotic leathers like caiman alligator and pirarucu fish skin, each pair is crafted by master bootmakers using time-honored techniques. The Gavin, in their iconic Black Cherry, is a picture-perfect style featuring smooth Santini goat leather uppers with intricate embellishments, welted construction, and lemonwood pegs—made the old-fashioned way, basically. They’ll cost you a pretty penny, of course, but they'll last pretty much forever, and the drip you’ll get in return is priceless.

From Ralph Lauren’s western-inspired sub-label, these RRL boots meld high-end materials with old-world craftsmanship and feature American-tanned leather uppers, Goodyear-welted construction, and vintage-style rubber soles—all crafted in a multi-generational Texas bootmaking factory.

Frye is another American brand with proven boot quality chops. (In fact, it claims to be America's oldest continually-operating boot company.) Its Roper boot has all the same strengths as Red Wing's, and comes in a non-roughout leather.

We don't typically include the same designers twice on one list, but Tecovas are that good. The Johnny is for anyone who wants a softer, slightly more charming boot than the eternal Cartwright up top.

For fellas whose style skews a little more regular-guy than rodeo, these lower-profile, lower-priced Ropers have a Goodyear welt and Vibram outsole. Bang for your, ahem, buck.

Wythe's playing-card-inspired boots feature all the bells and whistles, including premium leather inside and out, hand-applied details, lemonwood pegs, and second-to-none construction all-around. While some brands tap the expert bootmakers in Léon, Mexico to pump out high-quality mass-appeal boots, Wythe tapped these artisans to really let them cook.

All of Yuketen's shoes are perfect, which is a big part of the reason Drake's now carries them. And while they're best known for their moccasin styles, the brand makes an exceptionally handsome cowboy boot too. Really, they're just showing off now.

The classic move for western boots is to lean into their rougher side. Toss them on with well-fitting jeans (preferably vintage bootcuts) and a snap-button shirt. But open up your mind and step outside of the saloon and the possibilities are practically endless. A slick pair of exotic leather cowboy boots matched with a '70s-inspired suit screams filthy-rich oil tycoon—if not Alessandro Michele-era Gucci. Or why not mix up a preppy pair of chinos with a grungy graphic tee and some roughed-up suede ropers? The point is, you don’t have to pretend to be a cowboy to wear their boots (though you certainly can). So treat them like any other pair of shoes in your closet and wear them however you want. Hell, we’d go so far as to call them an essential pair of boots for every wardrobe.

Peter Middleton knows a thing or two about the lifestyle having cut his teeth at Ralph Lauren and used it as a springboard for his own western-inspired brand, Wythe. “To me the beauty and shape of the Cowboy Boot (and a lot of the original design intent) lies in its curves and that’s what makes a great boot SEXY. There isn't a lot of men's footwear that has these dramatic curves, leather, and a high heel but the right cowboy boot has them all.”

He says that good cowboy boots should be made with natural leathers that age well with wear, mould to the wearer’s feet, and can be recrafted repeatedly. His non-negotiables include full leather construction from the insole to the outsole, linings, uppers, and heels. Look for Goodyear welted construction which will allow you to have the boots resoled and extend their life. The toe bug (the fancy stitching at the toe), should sit right where your foot flexes to help cover up the crease that comes with each step. Preferably, this would be corded by hand, of course.

Price points will vary, and you can get some well-made boots for around $300. The really great stuff will often be twice that much. Middleton says, “the best thing to do is wait and save up until you can buy the version you actually want because its impossible to cut corners and still deliver the product you want.” So if you can be patient, save up. If you can’t, try buying secondhand. But if you simply can’t wait, you’ll do well enough with plenty of the mid-tier brands until you’re ready to upgrade.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you.

#6700. 18 Best Raincoats for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

Decades ago, your only options for rainy weather coverage were some kind of trench coat, a coarse wool overcoat, or, if you really needed to be out in it, a thick rubber sack that kept you dry but didn’t breathe. Thankfully, material science has advanced.

There are, of course, umbrellas? And we won’t stop you looking into those. But ask anyone from the Pacific Northwest, and they’ll tell you their rain jacket does all that work with ease. That’s because raincoats aren’t an accessory to your outfit: they’re the main focus. A good raincoat can turn your sad ensemble into head-turning Paparazzi fodder, or at the very least, keep you dry and stylish when it’s wet out.

Still not convinced? We figured—so we tested the best rain jackets and coats for men on the market right now, from the tried-and-true natural-fabric mainstays to the buzzy, newfangled riffs, and everything in between. Here they are.

Arc'teryx's Beta LT jacket is the pinnacle of no-BS rain defense, beloved by wannabe Everest climbers, GQ Recommends editors, and meme-savvy TikTokers alike. Its custom Gore-Tex fabrication will keep you from getting soaked during the harshest of downpours, while its breathable pores work overtime to let water vapor (a.k.a. sweat) escape. That sleek, streamlined silhouette also lends itself naturally to a range of pairings, from straight-fit jeans and sneakers to a full-on “Scaling Kilimanjaro” kit—cargos, backpack, hiking boots—perfect for all of your wildcard outdoor activities.

It’s an investment, but hundreds of guys were showering in theirs for a reason: If you need top-tier weather protection and long-term durability—and are willing to invest—this is still the best rain jacket for serious outdoor use. That said, most dudes chasing the clout that Arc’teryx affords won’t need to max out its capabilities. Still, it’s good to know that if you’re ever caught in a downpour that won’t let up, the Beta’s the one waterproof jacket that won’t leave you high and, uh, wet.

When it comes to outdoor gear that keeps up with Mother Nature, Patagonia’s among the first to come to mind. The OG gorpcore label has more than its fair share of bangers for every climate from the iconic Baggies shorts to its range of plush fleece jackets, and when it comes to rainy weather, there’s the Torrentshell jacket. It’s a canonical piece of kit for Patagonia and has kept customers dry and fly for years.

The latest version features a three-layer shell system that maximizes water-repellency and breathability with a 100% recycled nylon face fabric. It features toasty microfleece-lined handwarmer pockets, welted zippers for water protection (plus pit zips for ventilation), and the entire thing can pack down into itself. So pack it up, throw it in your bag, and bust it out whenever the weather turns out to be worse than what the weatherman says. With a feature set like that, you’d expect to pay through the nose, but we’re thrilled to say that it doesn’t even break $200.

The advancements in rain-wicking technology tickles our lizard brains as much as watching a rock-crushing machine turn a boulder into pebbles. But as much as we appreciate multi-layered DWR-coated materials and heat-sealed seams give us joy, we also have a great reverence and appreciation for rain coats of yore. Enter the waxed jacket, a time-tested fabric used by sailors off the British coast since the 1800s.

If you’re gonna get a waxed jacket, you’d best get a Barbour, the reigning champ of waxed jackets. Barbour has more options and collaborations than ever, so it can be tricky to get your head around what’s what but for our money, the classic Beaufort is the Goldilocks of wax jackets. Where the Bedale is shorter and styles like the Border run down to the mid-thigh, the Beaufort is just right but still long enough to layer over your sport coats. Whichever you choose, don’t forget to re-wax at home after a few seasons. Guys seem to be nervous to do it, but it’s easy, and everytime I’ve re-waxed mine, people compliment me on how great it looks. The reward for your grunt work is a jacket you'll keep for decades to come.

This season, J.Press has unearthed a Rare Barbour: the Burghley. It hasn’t been in the brand’s regular rotation for a number of years, earning it a following among collectors in places like Japan. But now it’s back in all its sweeping, full-length glory.

Originally designed to extend past the knees (and over the saddle and tops of riding boots) the Burghley protects the rider from rain and thorns. It’s a dramatic silhouette that will set you apart from all the cropped Transport and Bedales out there. Sure, it has equestrian heritage, but you needn’t be keeping a stable to wear one. It’s a great alternative to a trench, at home in the city as well as the country. And yes, it’s still made in England.

The perfect trench coat does exist, and Burberry, inventor of the style's signature gabardine fabric in 1879, cracked the code on it long ago. In the nearly 150 years since, surprisingly little has changed about the silhouette, and for good reason. The classic fit means it's roomy enough to layer over your slouchiest Armani-inspired suiting, while the belted design gives it instant sophistication. Plus, the lining and under-collar check pattern is unmistakably Burberry—an investment-worthy flex since the coat was invented.

Its WWI heritage is well-documented—British officers wore it in the trenches, using details like the shoulder flap for rifle recoil and belt rings for canteens and grenades serving real military functions. Today, it’s less about battlefield utility and more about how that water-resistant cotton gabardine will keep you dry in light city rain. I like the black, but the classic tan is always an option for you too.

Another entry from Vancouver—what can we say, they know a few things about rain up there. Indie label James Coward sources fabric from mills known for their quality and considered production practices, then makes its garments locally in its workshop. This coat is cut from the brand’s durable, waterproof ‘Mastershield cloth, a dense weave that offers protection from the elements. A generous silhouette and roomy raglan sleeves give it natural gravitas in gloomy weather, all while keeping the look relaxed and easy to layer.

Whereas the Arc’teryx Beta is the brand’s versatile all-mountain shell, the Alpha SV is built for technical climbing and severe weather. A helmet-compatible hood, harness-ready chest pockets and pit zips make it the top dog of storm shells.

HAVEN puts a GORPcore spin on the refined, city-ready trench made from three-layer GORE-TEX PRO, a more rugged fabric typically found in shells for climbing and backcountry skiing. It’s serious storm gear with a city sensibility, ready to make you feel invincible during a rainy commute.

The wrap coat is Lemaire’s runaway hit. But this Ventile-cotton style is a rainy-day flex: densely woven, breathable cotton that swells when wet to help keep wind and rain out, without synthetic fibres or coatings. Chic.

Menswear can get a little obsessive about fabric specs and provenance. But when a jacket is this cool, you get why. 100 percent Giza cotton, woven at maximum density in Saitama, Japan and originally developed for the British Air Force and later recommissioned by Japan’s Coast Guard. Yeah, that’s the good stuff.

Prep never left, but J.Press is certainly on its way back. The label’s updated take on a classic hooded parka keeps the familiar military shape sans PFAS with natural Ventile cotton. Choose classic army green, or eye-popping orange.

Haider Ackermann's take on Canada Goose is coming into focus. The brand’s Snow Goose sub-label is more Milan than Manitoba, trading logo-heavy designs for cool colours and modern silhouettes that go well beyond down parkas.

Making a good rain jacket might not take a degree in nuclear science, but Outdoor Research’s founder was actually a nuclear physicist turned avid mountaineer. This stripped-back shell sheds weight and packs small without skimping on protection. Stash it and forget it.

Marmot, meanwhile didn’t pull back on features to shave weight. This soft, quiet waterproof shell is built for movement, with an “Angel-Wing” design that lets you reach overhead without the hem riding up and putting your backside on display.

Reigning Champ has also gone well beyond sweats and tees. This workhorse of a rain jacket is more downtown than summit in style but still meets the moment with GORE-TEX construction and a very digestible price.

Italian fabric. Fifth-generation Portuguese construction. New York City design. That’s the mix behind Knickerbocker’s full-length trench. Its tightly woven, water-repellent fabric helps keep rain out all with the kind of easy, downtown cool Knickerbocker does so well.

Uniqlo’s even more affordable sister brand, GU, delivers a lot of jacket for the money The throwback piping and punchy colours nod to the ’90s, while the water- and wind-resistant fabric makes it practical, too.

A waxed Harrington probably isn’t for a downpour unless your trousers are waxed, too, but it’s ideal for breezy, drizzly, and sunny fall days alike. And, aye, the waxed fabric comes straight from Scotland. No messing about.

Who wants to drag a sopping wet umbrella around only to forget it at the restaurant or in the back of a cab? When you’ve got a trench coat with your cell and headphones in the pockets, it’s almost impossible to lose. Through light mists, heavy rain, and even torrential downpours, the best rain coats are, ironically, the drippiest too. They’ll prevent you from showing up to the function like a poor wet dog and instead make your arrival a warm, dry welcome.

Trench coats that aren’t the only coats vying to shield you from the rain, though. Gorpcore’s popularity has given us waves of tech-y jackets, parkas, and anoraks all wrought from futuristic multi-layered materials that balance breathability with waterproof ability. The result is a cementing of this 21st century sportswear into must-have menswear. And let’s not forget the workwear-adjacent waxed jackets born from sailing the Scottish seas in 15th-century Scotland. With their rugged good looks and a lust for patina, they’ve become stylish staples when the weather’s all doom and gloom.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)
