#6665. RIP to LIV, the Golf League No One Ever Really Cared About

The most remarkable thing about the era of LIV Golf, a five-year run characterized by near-parodic blunders and missteps, is that leadership and its public-facing talent don’t appear to have learned any lessons from their many failures.

From the beginning, the upstart league, established in 2021 as a rival to the dominant PGA Tour, was plagued by its own ineptitude. It’s only fitting—after LIV’s newfound lifeline, private investment firm BC Partners, reneged on a $300 million investment—that the brand’s figureheads are now celebrating the end by taking a victory lap. Tone-deaf doesn’t begin to describe it.

LIV CEO Scott O’Neil—who took over for original boss and Major winner Greg Norman in January of 2025 and was faced with finding new cash flow after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund announced in April that it would no longer finance the operation—clearly exists in a different reality than our own. “Together, our team delivered world-class individual and team competitions across five continents, built a global business, secured pathways to The Open and U.S. Open… and built a league capable of attracting both elite and rising talent from around the world,” he said in a statement late last week. It’s unclear if the farewell tour will continue, though, an even newer report suggests that BC is looking to purchase a bankruptcy loan from LIV’s current owners. If this Hail Mary works, LIV’s future would look much different than the one O’Neil has promised.

Another way to look at it? One of LIV’s biggest accomplishments was that the league, despite its intentions to diversify the game and bring fun back into a sport that had come to personify privilege and exclusivity, inadvertently consolidated the PGA Tour’s power as a monopolistic behemoth now primed to solidify its status as the only league in town. They helped usher in a new era of leadership and investment for the Tour, including the introduction of CEO Brian Rolapp, who is by all accounts the most well-liked and accomplished boss it’s had in quite some time.

LIV also managed to tarnish the reputations of its biggest stars. Jon Rahm, who was vehemently opposed to the dog and pony show when it was announced, only to sheepishly take a bag of blood money so big his great grandchildren will never have to work a day in their lives, is now one of the game’s great “What If?” stories. Prior to the US Open at Brookline Country Club in June 2022, the defending champion was asked about his thoughts on the upstart league. “Money is great,” he said. But Rahm and his wife thought about it further. “Will our lifestyle change if I got $400 million? No, it will not change one bit.” Just over a year later, he defected to LIV.

Then there’s Phil Mickelson, who was poised to become a fixture of the American Ryder Cup team and widely considered a shoo-in to hold a cushy job as an analyst for golf’s biggest broadcasting events. Upon joining the league, he admitted in a conversation with Alan Shipnuck that Saudi Arabia was a terrible bedfellow. (Shipnuck and Mickelson have disagreed over whether the comments were on or off the record.) “They killed Khashoggi and have a horrible record on human rights. They execute people over there for being gay,” he explained. But there was a caveat: LIV was simply a “once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to reshape how the PGA Tour operates.” Miraculously, he got that part right.

Prize money on the PGA Tour is greater than ever, the Tour’s main events have recalibrated, and its season-ending championship series has been completely reimagined. Mickelson won’t be allowed within 100 miles of it.

The league’s biggest attraction, Bryson DeChambeau, is theoretically the worst-situated of the game’s many turncoats, though he’s probably more excited about growing his YouTube channel than he is about playing golf anyway. No one’s put their reputation on the line for this gaggle of buffoons quite like DeChambeau, who spoke proudly about how LIV was “growing the game” and “bringing team golf to the world.” This past weekend, he shared a statement on the dissolution of LIV. “I still believe in team golf,” he wrote. “It may look different in the future, but I still believe it’s essential to growing the game.” A loyal foot soldier, even from the grave.

It was always about the money. Nothing more, nothing less. This was a cash grab of epic proportions, and golfers not good enough to earn their keep on the PGA Tour took a life raft and briefly found themselves in possession of luxury superyachts. Then, low tide left them holding bags of cash and not much else. Was it worth it? For most of these golfers, probably. They were past their primes when LIV began; they’re still past the primes now, and just a helluva lot richer.

The league positioned itself as a disruptor, and disrupted so poorly that they’ve turned the PGA Tour into the strongest version of itself we’ve seen since Tiger Woods was at his peak. All that bloviating about the good of LIV Golf from its loudest talking heads is a bunch of nonsense.

It’s hard to tell if these statements are one last line of defense or more genuine delusion from LIV’s last advocates. They came in with the idea of sticking it to the PGA Tour, which at the time was facing bleak circumstances, bogged down in nonsense before redirecting its energy towards batting this gnat into extinction. Ultimately, LIV helped the PGA Tour save itself.

Now it’s gone for good, and five billion dollars later, we’re not gonna remember a damn thing about it. Not the players, not the leadership, and surely not all the ways they supposedly grew the game while merely growing their own bank accounts.

#6663. Inside Designer Carla Fernández’s Vibrant Mexico City Home, Full of Indigenous Textiles and Art

When the Mexico City–based writer and curator Su Wu started working on her new design book, a theme began to emerge: She kept noticing the presence of pre-Hispanic architectural and design traditions in modern Mexican interiors.

It was from this realization that Mesoamerican Modern: Ancient Ideas in Contemporary Mexican Homes was born. The book features 16 homes across Mexico—those of artists, architects, designers, and more—all shot by photographer Luis Garvan.

Wrought iron doors designed by Carla Fernández.

“When I began this project, I thought that we would be documenting a history, like the aura surrounding the artifacts in a museum, the apparitions of the past,” Wu writes in the book’s introduction. “Instead, what I’ve found is the vibrant, living presence of Indigenous ideas in Mexico.”

“In the guest bedroom, pink silk curtains dyed by Fernández cast a glow in the room. The bedspread is from her personal collection of Mesoamerican textiles, made of undyed wool in Teotitlán del Valle, Oaxaca.”—Su Wu

One space that exemplifies those ideas is Casa Flora, the colorful brutalist Mérida home and studio of the Mexican fashion designer Carla Fernández. As a designer, Fernández seeks to preserve the textile traditions of Indigenous and Mestizo communities through collaborations with artisans, bringing ancient practices to a contemporary audience. Fittingly, the home is full of Mesoamerican art and textiles.

Fernández led the renovation of the house, which dates back more than a century and now hosts workshops for Taller Flora, her design lab, which focuses on traditional Indigenous clothing techniques. (You can also stay in the home; the designer lists it for stays on Airbnb.)

Fernández’s husband, the artist Pedro Reyes, created several elements of the space, including a concrete outdoor shower, as well as some of the tiles throughout the space. His sculptures—including a prototype of Mitla Sillas, chairs inspired by the pyramids of the Mitla archaeological site in Oaxaca—are found throughout the home.

The outdoor shower designed by Pedro Reyes.

Reflecting on Fernández’s home, Wu tells Vogue: “I was very moved when writing the book with the idea of the ‘void’ in Mesoamerican architecture, and how so many architects, going back to the writings of Teodoro González de León, described the patio as a central, defining feature of the Mesoamerican home, a space that is at once enclosed and open to the elements. And in Carla’s home, I think there are many moments that exemplify this idea of the boundary between interior and exterior being not a physical one, but rather a marker of movement across psychological states.”

“A latticed window, known in Mexico as a celosia, is a central part of Mesoamerican architecture, used for cross-ventilation and to create a permeable sense of separation and shadow.”—Su Wu

Prototypes of Reyes’s Mitla Sillas chairs.

For Wu, Casa Flora is also a prime example of an idea that chef Elena Reygadas wrote about in her foreword to the book, “which is that handmade things are meant to be lived with no matter how precious, or especially if precious.”

“An equipale chair made of bent wood by the Wixárika people in Nayarit sits in front of a backstrap-loom tapestry of the Torre Latinoamericana, the iconic skyscraper in Mexico City designed by architect Augusto H. Álvarez. Made by artisans from San Juan Cancuc, Chiapas, the tapestry echoes a collection of dresses from Carla Fernández that featured historic buildings in Mexico City.”—Su Wu

All images © Luis GarvanCasa Flora, Mérida, Yucatán (pages 244-263). Mesoamerican Modern: Ancient Ideas in Contemporary Mexican Homes by Su Wu. Published by Phaidon Press.

#6662. How Martine Rose Became Menswear’s Secret Weapon

After much speculation, the fashion rumors were true: Martine Rose is officially Balenciaga’s next creative director. It’s undoubtedly her biggest job yet—and a historic appointment: she is the first woman creative director to lead the French house. Rose succeeds Valentino vet Pierpaolo Piccioli, whose departure after just three seasons was announced yesterday. This is a major moment for the British-Jamaican designer, who has spent nearly two decades building a namesake label that wields outsize influence.

Rose has long occupied a curious position in menswear: a designer’s designer with a devoted following of both high-fashion heads and the streetwear obsessed. Since launching her eponymous brand in 2007, she’s developed an unmistakable visual language of playful styling, graphic-driven sportswear, offbeat tailoring, and covetable sneaker collabs. Her clothes take the familiar and make it strange, infusing everyday garments with humor, a touch of romance, and occasionally an ugly-chic sensibility: think square-toed loafers, chap-covered suit trousers, and soccer jerseys with lace-up fastenings. The results can be loud, wildly stylish, and routinely fun.

“I’ve always been drawn to the way men are told to dress,” the designer told GQ in 2023. She’s been on a mission to subvert those conventions ever since.

On the Martine Rose runway, sportswear and tailoring regularly share the spotlight. Much of that sensibility comes from her ability to borrow from and fuse London’s historic subcultures: ravers and club kids, football fanatics and nattily dressed mods. She tends to embrace the unusual and eccentric rather than focusing on making everything read as conventionally stylish. One look from a recent collection featured a swishy track jacket tucked into straight-leg blue jeans, and another paired a perfectly tailored coat with athletic shorts and knee-high socks. In Rose’s universe, there is no wrong way to mix and match. That’s part of her charm.

That sensibility eventually caught the eye of Demna during his decade-long tenure as Balenciaga’s creative director. The firebrand Georgian designer reached out to Rose in 2015, and she spent the next three years as a menswear consultant for the house during a high-profile era defined by supersized proportions and outlandish accessories. The blazers were big and the boots were bigger, but the star power of the celebrities who wore them was the biggest of all.

Rose is no stranger to dressing famous people. Her clothes have landed on some of the most photographed men in the world: Drake, Timothée Chalamet, Bad Bunny, A$AP Rocky. Perhaps her most visible endorsement came at the 2025 Super Bowl, when Kendrick Lamar strutted around the halftime show in a custom red, white, and blue leather varsity jacket designed by Rose. (In 2023, the rapper had boasted about how he wears “Martine when I board jets” on the Baby Keem track “The Hillbillies”; a year later, he sported several of her designs in his “Not Like Us” video.) Over the years, Rose has become one of the rare designers whose collections are covered by both Vogue and Hypebeast, a testament to her appeal across fashion’s various tribes. She’s been a fixture at London Fashion Week, and made her Milan debut in 2024. She has two hit Supreme collaborations under her belt. When Nike came calling, Rose produced remixed versions of the Shox (which Lamar wore while he accepted three Grammys in 2023) and Monarch sneakers—turning two of the Swoosh’s most divisive silhouettes into lustworthy objects. Tommy Hilfiger enlisted her for a collection centered on the streetwise style of Tommy Jeans in the ’90s. Brands know she can deliver the goods.

Which brings us back to the Balenciaga appointment. Rose obviously knows the house, or at least Demna’s vision for it—a vision that brought meme-worthy fashion but also massive creative and commercial success. A statement from Luca de Meo, CEO of Kering (the French luxury group that owns Balenciaga), included all the boilerplate press language about innovation, creativity, and culture. More crucially, Rose has proven she can make familiar clothes feel fresh, taking what guys are already wearing and turning them into something surprising and covetable. The question is now what Rose will be able to do with the resources of a global luxury powerhouse at her disposal. She’s spent much of her career building her own idiosyncratic world at Martine Rose. At Balenciaga, she’ll find herself on a much bigger stage with a vast audience and a completely new rulebook.

In 2020, when discussing her reputation as something of an underground darling, Rose told GQ: “I’ve never really wanted to be part of a big thing. Anything that is mainstream—since I was small—hasn’t really appealed to me.” Six years later, she’s earned one of the biggest jobs in fashion. There’s great fun in evolution, too.

#6660. My Fall Outfits Were Feeling Stale—12 November Outfit Ideas I’m Trying Instead

Now that the weather is cooling off, leaving the house involves grappling with the internal struggle: will I be cute or will I be comfortable today? On chilly days, the heaviest coats or most weather-proof shoes are all-too-appealing, but they don’t always show off your style in the way you might hope. Luckily, this month there are some clever ways you can meet in the sartorial middle: ensuring you don’t freeze, but still putting your outfit first.

With darker evenings here, football season in full swing, and Thanksgiving around the corner, think of these 12 November outfits as the puzzle pieces to help you check the boxes for everything coming up on your calendar. Playing with bold colors, contrasting textures, and even sneakily comfy silhouettes are some of the simple ways you can make November your best-dressed month of the entire year. Check out some of my favorite looks below.

An on-trend striped rugby sweater is the kind of dress-up-or-down staple you might usually style with jeans and sneakers. But for any dressy events on the calendar, try playing with texture and structure by styling it with a full, sequin skirt. It’s a look that reads dressy without trying too hard.

One way to make comfy pieces like oversized knits and track pants feel instantly dressy? By going bold with color. For days when you want to look polished but will mostly be relaxing on the couch, this is a great place to start.

It’s prime football season, which might mean your Sunday afternoon is devoted to keeping up with your favorite team. A pair of relaxed jeans and a suede jacket is a combination that’s cute enough to wear to brunch with your girlfriends, but won’t feel out of place at a sporty party.

If you’re the type to dress up for Thanksgiving (or any occasion really), a trim satin suit is a dressy look in stark contrast to the usual oversized staples. The shiny fabric gives the look a festive touch, while styled back to a simple black top and shoes, the overall effect is very ‘90s Prada—who can complain about that?

Odds are you’ll be headed out of town to spend some time with family. Your travel day look should always put comfort first, but a few small styling choices can make the outfit feel purposeful instead of schlubby. A crew neck tee paired with a V-neck sweater, a trench coat, and chunky Dad sneakers are what make Zoë Kravitz’ outfit look so good.

As winter approaches, there’s something enduringly chic about a white-on-white look. Even if your outfit is as simple as jeans and an oversized sweater, the matchiness pulls everything together. Black accessories are an easy pick, but you could also add a pop of contrast by styling the outfit with red shoes.

Call it genius, call it sneaky; there’s no need to wear full-on sweats to the Thanksgiving table. A dressy matching set can be just as comfortable. Look for styles with a soft, stretchy plissé fabric, which has a dressed-up, flowy air but feels as easy as your coziest loungewear.

This time of year, fashion is all about rich, sumptuous textures and hues. Gigi Hadid’s party look puts a soft, check flannel skirt in contrast with leather boots and a matching jacket, and the effect is greater than the sum of its parts. Cozy yet chic.

When you don’t want to think too hard about your look, an all-black outfit is an easy fallback with polish. Try opting for an oversized knit, styled with tailored trousers and loafers for a look you could wear to the office, but would feel just as comfortable in for a Saturday spent shopping with your most fashionable friends.

Breaking up a tailored look with a cummerbund or even a scarf can be an easy way to repurpose pieces you might usually relegate to the office. This dressy yet comfortable look is versatile, so throw it on for post-work martinis or even a night at the orchestra.

Fringe is a trend to know this season, and Nara Smith’s Kallmeyer dress is the blueprint for a festive Thanksgiving party or wedding when you want to dress to impress. The fringe detail adds movement, which makes it ideal for a situation with a dance floor. Layer a leather jacket or a black trench coat on top for your time in transit.

A great pair of jeans should always be in heavy rotation, but how you style them is an easy way to make them feel fresh. Here, mixing feminine pieces with more classic menswear details gives this a casual-yet-effortful effect. Try a blazer and little heels with socks for a playful spin on pieces you likely already have sitting in your closet.

Aemilia Madden is a freelance writer, editor, and consultant with over 10 years of experience in the fashion industry. She’s written for publications including Vogue, Harper’s Bazaar, Elle,The Cut, and The Wall Street Journal.

She also writes a newsletter called Taeste Bud, that covers stuff we buy, places we go, and the people we want to be. Previously, she held roles at The Zoe Report, WhoWhatWear and Popsuagar. Aemilia attended the University of California, Berkeley where she majored in Political Economics. In her time away from the internet, Aemilia is probably out on a hike.

#6659. All About Anthony De La Torre, Lana Condor’s Actor/Musician Husband

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In the To All the Boys films, Lana Condor’s character is often consumed by relationship drama. In any given movie, Lara Jean may be torn between her current boyfriend Peter Kavinsky (Noah Centineo) and childhood crush John Ambrose McClaren (Jordan Fisher), or struggling with whether to follow Peter to college. But in real life, Condor faces no such dilemma. She’s been in a relationship with actor/musician Anthony De La Torre since 2015. In January 2022, the two got engaged, and in October 2024, they got married. Below, all you need to know about De La Torre and his romance with Condor.

Like Condor, De La Torre is an actor who most famously played a young Jack Sparrow in 2017’s Pirates of the Caribbean: Dead Men Tell No Tales. He’s also appeared in episodes of the Nickelodeon series 100 Things to Do Before High School and the Starz show Vida.

The couple actually met at an entertainment industry function. During her February 2019 cover story with Cosmopolitan, Condor spoke about meeting De La Torre at an Emmy nominees reception in 2015. His pickup line? “Hi, I’m Anthony. I thought I would make a friend.” She described their initial meeting to the outlet as being “like a ­Taylor Swift song.”

Ever since their storybook meeting, De La Torre and Condor have stayed by each other’s side. The actress told Cosmopolitan UK that her beau is quite the romantic. “He writes me love letters all the time,” she told the outlet. “He’s the freaking best. He’ll leave me little messages taped to my bathroom mirror on days I have to wake up early for work. He once picked up my favorite takeout food after work and served it up properly on our kitchen table, with little sticky notes attached to each plate saying how proud of me he is.”

Both Condor and De La Torre wrote romantic Instagram posts to celebrate their fifth anniversary as a couple. In August 2020, she shared a photo of the pair cuddled up on the grass with the caption, “5 years with the man of my dreams. You are still my most favorite person in the world. You are my best friend. You have taught me how to love and to be loved, to forgive and to grow, to never give up, to be each others teammate and cheerleader. You make me laugh like no other and it doesn’t matter where we are in the world, as long as I’m with you, I’m home. I love you @anthonydltorre happy anniversary. Let’s cuddle. 🐶”

De La Torre’s post, also shared on August 31, includes a sunset picture. He wrote, “5 years ago we went on our first date and saw a movie called The Gift. While the plot of the film in no way represents our life, the name of the movie couldn’t be more fitting to what you are. The greatest gift I've ever been blessed and entrusted with. From our tiny ass apartment with a blow up twin mattress to riding horses in Kenya, it has always been an adventure 🤪 Thank you for changing my life, inspiring me to grow, and making everything worth it. I love you, my tiny burrito 🌯♥️”

Condor shared the news that De La Torre popped the question on Jan. 28, 2022. She explained the details behind the ring and shared engagement photos, which you can view here on her Instagram.

She wrote, of the proposal, “Saying YES was the easiest decision I’ve ever made. I consider myself the absolute luckiest woman alive to live in your sphere. Aside from my father, without a doubt, you are the greatest man in the world. Emmy & Timmy said it’s about time mommy & daddy got engaged!!!”

Condor added of the ring, “💍: Anthony worked with the INCREDIBLE Vietnamese female owned @parisjewellerscanada to design the most stunning piece I’ve ever seen! The fact he knew how important it was to me that he’d collaborate w/ a Vietnamese company to help design my ring is a testament to just how truly thoughtful he always is. I cannot wait to be your wife, baby. I love you a million times over.”

The couple got married in Malibu at the Serra Franciscan Retreat Center, according to Vogue. Condor told the publication that she and her late mother Mary went to a spiritual retreat there when she was in high school.

“My mother Mary recently passed away, leaving my entire family devastated,” the actress shared. “But she would not have wanted us to not celebrate our love. We really wanted to get married at the Serra Retreat because being there was such a core memory that I had with my mom, and I felt like we would be able to feel her presence on our wedding night.”

The bride wore a strapless Vera Wang dress she picked out with her mother as well.

“My mom and I absolutely fell in love with my wedding gown the moment I tried it on, and I am so grateful I got to share that experience with her,” she said. “That was the only time she was able to see me in my dress, and I will hold that memory in my heart forever.”

She added, “The entire weekend could not have gone better. We are so filled with love and gratitude for each other and also for all of our incredible guests who came from far and wide to join us in the celebrations. This weekend will forever go down as the happiest weekend of our lives.”

The couple kicked off the year at Vanity Fair and Amazon MGM Studios’ awards season celebration in Los Angeles on January 10.

Condor wore a black Sebastian Gunawan Signature off-the-shoulder gown, while De La Torre opted for a copper-colored suit.

On March 15, they attended the Elton John AIDS Foundation’s annual Academy Awards viewing party in West Hollywood. Condor chose an ivory Alberta Ferretti halter-neck gown, while De La Torre wore a light blue suit.

They stepped out again on July 28 for a Los Angeles screening of The Devil’s Mouth, with Condor wearing a red gown featuring a plunging, draped neckline and De La Torre dressed in a black suit.

The pair also attended the US Open in New York City on September 8. Condor wore a navy top layered over a plaid collared shirt, accessorized with pink-tinted sunglasses, while De La Torre wore a blue blazer over a checked shirt.

In a Nov. 10, 2026 interview with People, Condor and De La Torre revealed that they spent their second wedding anniversary filming The Receipt, an upcoming romantic comedy that lets them share a love story on-screen, too.

“It was so cute. We got to celebrate it on set together with Fetch,” Condor said. “I don’t think we really told anyone. I think they just kind of knew and they were so, so sweet and everyone on set was just the most adorable. It was the cutest love-filled day. It was delightful.”

While they’ve worked together on music before, playing a couple on-screen was a new experience for Condor.

“I’ve fallen in love on camera quite a few times, but I think it’s a lot different when you can actually do it with someone that you share your life with and that you’ve loved for a very long time,” she said.

In addition to being an up-and-coming actor, De La Torre is also a musician. He’s previously been in bands including DE LA TORRE and The Fell. In October 2019, he released a new song titled “Know Me.” According to Seventeen, the track was written about Condor.

De La Torre and Condor teased the release of a joint musical project on Jan. 7, 2020. She shared a photo of the couple snuggled near a laptop on Instagram and captioned it, “We’ve been working on a very special project for months now for you guys. It’s been blood, sweat, and tears. It’s been highs & lows & sobs & laughs but we’ve done it together, and that’s what counts 💪🏽. Building dreams together. Love your loved ones, love your strangers. We can’t wait to share with you so soon x.”

Speculation that the couple was working on music together grew after De La Torre’s cryptic Instagram post. “Feb 19,” he captioned a shot of the pair.

The answer to questions about Condor and De La Torre’s first collaboration arrived with the dreamy duet, “Raining in London.” Condor announced the release of their song (and De La Torre’s EP) on Instagram. “This is our heart,” she wrote. “Our song 'Raining In London' (ft. me) is FINALLY OUT, and so is my dear @anthonydltorre whole EP FIND ME. I couldn’t be more proud of him. Against so many odds, he’s made this EP come to life for you all to hear. It’s definitely playing on repeat all day for me 💕! I love you golden boy, thru and thru.”

De La Torre also has his own channel, which includes a video of fun facts about the actor. In it, he opens up about learning to speak Spanish, his close bond with his siblings, and why he once dyed his hair black.

In the months since their first duet, the couple recorded a cover of Lauv’s “I Like Me Better” and released another original song, “No Way.” They even choreographed a TikTok dance for the latter song.

As for Condor’s musical catalog, she has also drawn inspiration from De La Torre. Last August, she released the song “For Real,” a track about how much she misses her longtime boyfriend while traveling for work.

“I’ve been in a pretty serious relationship for five years now, and whenever I travel I have to leave him,” she explained to People. “I always have a lot of anxiety about going off and leaving my home. I had just written down this journal entry about my feelings of going back to work as things start to shoot again, and my anxieties about leaving my boyfriend and about starting life up again, especially in this insane year that we've been having.”

Condor went on to address her new musical collaborations with De La Torre: “I mean, it seems so random, but I’ve always loved music. My boyfriend is a musician, and I’ve watched him navigate the music industry for years now, and I have always been very fascinated with it. I just felt like during quarantine, why not try something new if you love it?”

Condor told Cosmopolitan that when To All the Boys I’ve Loved Before was released in 2018, rumors about her and Centineo’s offscreen relationship persisted. “Noah and I definitely encouraged the speculation,” she admitted. “But it’s because we believe in the story and the characters, and we genuinely love each other. You can truly love someone in a very platonic way.”

But Condor said Instagram trolls would say “horrible things” about her real-life romance with De La Torre. “It was so hurtful for both of us,” she explained. “This is supposed to be a good thing. Why are you ­coming for someone who has no part of this story? If you say you support me, why would you hurt someone I love?”

For a while, De La Torre disabled his Instagram comments. But now, the couple seems to be handling social media pressure in stride. In August 2019, De La Torre even jokingly shared a photo with himself and Centineo, followed by a pic of Condor and co-star Madelaine Arthur. He captioned the post, “PK and LJ are even cuter in person.”

When De La Torre isn’t writing Condor love letters or sonnets, he’s providing another public service. Namely, he’s sharing photos with his and Condor’s dog Emmy. Yes, she has an Instagram:

Each couple experienced isolation a little differently during the COVID-19 pandemic. For Condor and De La Torre, they tried out TikTok trends and wore matching tie-dye outfits. Last March, both of the performers shared videos of themselves experimenting on the app to Instagram.

#6658. 50 Marathons in 24 Days. Men Are Inventing Extreme Physical Challenges for the Hell of It

For more than 18 days straight this past May, Mason Wright woke up every morning at 5 a.m. and ran laps around a track at a high school in Layton, Utah. The early-spring air was typically crisp, and the smell of dew-covered grass reminded him of childhood. By the time students would arrive for class, he’d have run 10 miles. At lunch, he kept running. And when student athletes came out for after-school track practice he would still be there, running alongside them. When the sun finally set over the mountains, he’d crawl into a van parked beside the field, sleep as much as he could, and do it all over again. He ran through freezing mornings that left frost on the ground and rainstorms that flooded the track, leaving his feet so waterlogged that his skin split open.

When Wright finished his extended sprint, he became the third person in the world to run 1,000 fairly consecutive miles around a track, a project he embarked upon because, as he puts it, “I wanted something that genuinely sounded impossible.”

Mason Wright camped nearby a high school track to complete a self-assigned challenge of running 1,000 consecutive miles in a row.

When Wright finished his extended sprint, he became the third person in the world to run 1,000 fairly consecutive miles around a track.

“I wanted something that genuinely sounded impossible,” Wright says of the project.

Wright isn’t the only one with an appetite for the absurd. There’s now a never-ending slew of guys inventing and completing preposterous physical challenges, often with elaborate, self-imposed rules meant to make them as miserable as humanly possible. Many have teams chronicling every grueling mile and setback online. If they manage to pull it off the challenge, they emerge as semi-viral niche sports heroes in their corner of the fitness internet. In 2020, ultramarathoner and Navy Seal David Goggins launched his annual 4x4x48 challenge, inviting his followers to run four miles every four hours for 48 hours. In December 2025, UK-based athlete Travis Kerry set the world record for longest nonstop bear crawl after bear crawling 50 kilometers (31 miles) across the streets of London, and nine months later Kevin Ocipeka from Germany burpee broad-jumped his way 26.2 miles along a track in 155 hours, 44 minutes, and 51 seconds—longer and faster than anyone had burpeed before.

“There’s this flywheel effect of extreme one-upmanship going on on social media,” says Michael Ceely, a psychotherapist and high-performance coach who works with athletes.

But beyond bragging rights, why are men motivated to push themselves to such dire limits? And what, exactly, are they getting out of it?

Many of the men I spoke to for this piece are drawn to the unknown. Once they have an idea, they hyperfixate on it until they accomplish it. Professional mountain biker Matt Jones says the concepts come from just about anywhere. “That’s how my brain works,” he says. He can’t drive past a rooftop or construction crane without wondering whether he could ride it or jump off it. “It doesn’t take long before you start going down a rabbit hole of crazy ideas, and then actually becoming obsessed with them enough to wanna do them,” he says. For the stunt he pulled off in November 2025, he spent months calculating the precise timing needed to jump through the open trailers of two trucks traveling in opposite directions, with only a split-second margin for error.

Matt Jones in Sweden strategizing for his 2025 ‘Through’ project, in which he jumped through the open trailers of two trucks traveling in opposite directions.

Matt Jones performed a world first jumping between two moving trucks.

William Goodge, an ultrarunner who embarked on the arduous task of running 50 marathons in 50 states in 24 days this October, is similarly attracted to the uncertainty that comes with each challenge. “If I wasn’t scared by it, I wouldn’t be enthusiastic,” he says. “That’s the whole point in this journey for me.” His quest, which culminated at this year’s NYC marathon, was nearly as logistically challenging as it was physically demanding. A flight delay or bad weather could have set him back a few hours, potentially derailing his entire schedule. On four of the days, he needed to run three marathons with only short stretches of rest or travel between them.

The thrill of chasing a near-impossible goal can seem grim once you’re in the thick of it, though, and that misery is equally part of the appeal. Kerry had dreamed of earning a Guinness World Record since childhood, and when he saw someone bear-crawl a marathon, he decided to take the record even further: 31 miles, compared to 26. He tested the movement on a treadmill, taking 20 minutes to cover a single kilometer. It was, he says, “the hardest fucking thing I’ve ever done.” But he figured if he could do one kilometer, he could do 50. “It’s just one 50 times, and I just need to get better at it,” he says.

Yet halfway through his bear crawl across the cobbled streets of London, rain was coming down, his hands were cold, and he was wearing a pair of garden gloves he had cut the fingertips off to prevent his palms from getting torn on the pavement. “One of my best mate’s jobs the whole time was squeezing water like a literal udder out the end of my gloves,” he says. Kerry thought about calling it quits. “Even though I physically wasn’t at the end of my limit, mentally I was like, I just can’t do this anymore,” he says.

UK-based athlete Travis Kerry completed a 50-kilometer bear crawl across the cobbled streets of London in 2025.

Kerry still hasn't regained feeling in his fingertips after completing the challenge. “I don’t know if that’s ever gonna come back,” he says. “A small price to pay.”

His coach could tell he was losing steam, so she told him to stand up and scream. “I went to the edge of Tower Bridge and screamed the word ‘fuck’ as loud as I possibly could,” he says. The release felt incredible. He got back down on his hands and feet and kept crawling.

Despite the sense of accomplishment they represent, these feats can come at a serious physical cost. Both Wright and Kerry say they may have suffered permanent nerve damage after their massive efforts. Wright, who ran 400 miles on stress fractures, says the pain that would take over his body was “unbearable.” Kerry hasn’t regained feeling in his fingertips. “I don’t know if that’s ever gonna come back,” he says. “A small price to pay.” Jones acknowledges that he’s spent most of his most epic moments in the last 10 years in physical pain. “I think there’s a limit to the force you can take and the number of years you can take it for,” he says.

The men who do these challenges put their bodies through hell in the search for personal growth and self-discovery. Take Goodge. He began running after losing his mom to cancer. “I wanted to feel physical pain to deal with the mental and spiritual pain of what I was going through,” he says. Compared with what she endured, he says, his own discomfort seems trivial. “I’m doing it in her honor. I’m doing it for Stand Up to Cancer. I can carry on. I’ve got no excuse not to take another step.”

Wright also found himself confronting the grief of losing his father when he was younger during his 1,000-mile run. “When you lose someone close to you, your world stops, but the world around you continues,” he says. “You almost feel like you’re stuck in time.” Running endless laps around the same track while the students’ lives continued brought that feeling back. “It forces you to let go of these things or else you’ll go more crazy,” he says.

Matt Valaoras, who holds hours-long planks at music festivals and sporting events, started planking as a way to manage his anxiety. As a college student, he could only plank for 45 seconds, but he added on time every day until he could hold it for several hours. As he’s held his core steady through rough conditions (the worst of which was a swarm of bugs biting him while he planked the duration of a Savannah Bananas baseball game), he’s “stacked the evidence” that he can handle anything that’s thrown at him—physical or otherwise. “I can confidently say, ‘Okay, I can do this because I, I did that, and it was worse than I expected,’” he says.

Ceely says it’s common that physical suffering can sometimes be a socially acceptable way for men to process emotional suffering. “Men have a hard time expressing weakness or vulnerability or saying ‘I just feel bad, period,’” he says. “They want to say, ‘Here’s what I did to fix it.’ Which, in a way, is a healthy coping mechanism. It’s better than being depressed.”

Valaoras acknowledges that this is part of the equation for him. “For a lot of guys, myself included, it’s easier to exhaust yourself physically than explain what’s exhausting you mentally. I’ve definitely used exercise as a way to avoid confronting things emotionally.” Nevertheless, he says that following some kind of physical exertion, he feels more comfortable opening up.

Meanwhile, Kerry says that he uses his emotional suffering as a way of fueling his physical feats. “One who chooses suffering is choosing to use their brain’s deflection mechanism to channel that emotional suffering into physical capabilities,” he says. He references the so-called pain cave—a term that many distance runners have used to describe the most miserable moments of an event, when the brain and body start to wear thin. “By focusing on that emotional trigger,” he says, that “becomes the person’s ‘why’.”

This form of emotional release can create a powerful cycle of anticipation and reward—encouraging more and more challenges. “You get adrenaline, endorphins, dopamine,” Ceely says. “There’s a physiological kind of addiction that can start just because you’re human and you’re wired that way.”

And it becomes a lot harder to stop if you have an audience expecting an encore. While most of the athletes I spoke to insisted they would still be doing these challenges if no one was watching, social media introduces pressure and expectations; it “moves the goalposts because the wilder the idea is, the more it sets the standards and moves the bar,” says Jones. Wright acknowledges that you can’t “run these miles and post about it on social media without some ego involved.” As he planned his track run, he was set on doubling the stakes of his last challenge, which was a 421-mile run across Utah. “800 miles isn’t cool, so it had to be 1,000,” he says.

Jones says he wouldn’t attempt his most outlandish stunts without the pressure to hold onto his brand sponsorships or please the fans in his comment sections. He, unlike most men who we spoke to for this piece, is a professional Red Bull athlete, whose livelihood in part depends on pulling off these spectacles. “Would I jump through two moving trucks just for a laugh? Probably not,” he says. “No, I wouldn’t at all.” He nevertheless enjoys his audience’s admiration. “I guess that’s vanity in a way, isn’t it?” he says. “But it’s also the currency of what we do.”

None of these men seem particularly interested in finding a comfortable stopping point. Wright says he has yet to find his limit. Valaoras has world records attempts in mind. Goodge doesn’t plan on slowing down.

“I want to be on the edge. How close can we get without falling off?” Goodge says. “How close to the sun can we fly, like Icarus, and not get burnt?”

#6657. The Fall Winter 2026 Fashion Trends to Know and Shop Now

If recent seasons have been defined by understated dressing, the fall-winter 2026 trends mark a shift toward a more expressive approach to style. However, there’s still a piece of the cake for every aesthetic to cut into. Across the runways, designers embraced the art of contrast. By pairing clean, minimalist silhouettes with rich textures—and reimagining classic wardrobe staples with unexpected details like a peplum or a voluminous shape—they proved that practicality and personality can coexist. The result is a season that feels both wearable and exciting.

Leading the conversation this season are six standout trends that capture fashion’s evolving mood. The Updated Lady introduces a fresh take on suiting for the modern woman, complete with feminine touches and softened structures. (Take a look at Chanel, Balenciaga, and Jil Sander for fresh inspiration, for starters.) Elsewhere, collections at Khaite, Kallmeyer, and Conner Ives nodded to the golden age of the 1920s, incorporating elegant Art Deco touches throughout. An unexpected take on proportions has entered the chat, too, with Tory Burch and Celine reconfiguring preconceived notions of size and shape. Designers, from Prada to Loewe, leaned on the power of styling, too, showcasing how layering and a close attention to detail can transform a wardrobe. For a more alluring take on the season, Alaïa and Valentino celebrated the female form through body-hugging silhouettes and delicate sheer pieces. Meanwhile, Dries Van Noten and Erdem embraced the spirit of fall with down-to-earth palettes of green and brown, punctuated by dark floral patterns.

Discover a shoppable first look at the key fall winter 2026 fashion trends before they fully take over. Don’t forget to come back to this story for more.

Unlike past seasons, there wasn’t one definitive silhouette for tailoring this fall. Let’s start with suiting. Designers don’t want you to stay within the lines. Many jackets were shown with mismatched trousers and skirts; lapel linings were exposed; traditional collared shirts were left undone and wrinkled; collars were turned up and styled with one in and one out. Some blazers shrank; some went long and lean. Others were cropped, nipped in, or even made sculptural.

In autumn’s sea of black coats and navy sweaters, choose color. This season, designers made it simple, showing a slew of head-to-toe looks in vivid monochrome hues. Take your pick! The runways offered a spin around the contemporary color wheel with splashes of color-drenched leather bags and footwear. From knits to denim, dresses to outerwear, we can’t think of a reason not to try this kind of energetic dressing.

This isn’t your grandmother’s lace! On the fall runways, designers from Altuzarra and Colleen Allen to Bottega Veneta, Dior, and Gabriela Hearst embraced a more dramatic kind of romance, with cascading layers, frothy ruffles, and sheer, sweeping silhouettes. Get the look with a delicate lace blouse paired with a tiered black skirt, or go all in with a floor-length lace dress. The effect is romantic, but anything but prim.

Photos: Courtesy of Kallmeyer, Khaite, Rabanne, Chanel, and Loro Piana

Although F. Scott Fitzgerald’s 1925 novel, The Great Gatsby, predates the height of the design style, its themes of 1920s luxury have permanently associated the story with the movement’s signature geometric patterns and overt opulence. From Khaite to Colleen Allen, designers embraced the era with richly detailed texture, glamorous shapes, fringe, gloves, and velvet. To quote the iconic host himself: “Can’t repeat the past? Why, of course, you can!” For 2026, the era returns with a modern, streamlined touch—ready for daily wear to elegant soirées.

Photos: Courtesy of Chanel, Stella McCartney, Givenchy, Jil Sander, and Chloé

The fall-winter 2026 runways showcased a sense of whimsy through bold proportions, featuring oversized designs, cropped styles, and exaggerated silhouettes. Cocoon-like tailoring, dramatic hemlines, and billowy pants and dresses create an expressive and eye-catching approach to fashion. This trend embraces a bit of drama (because that’s life!) while also maintaining a sense of balance. Unconventional silhouettes might feel daunting to incorporate into your daily lineup, so start with small touches. Destree’s jacquard blouse balances voluminous shoulders with a fitted waist that pairs easily with jeans, while Aflalo’s tapered white balloon pants feel airy and light—just add a layered knit and coat for transitional dressing.

Photos: Courtesy of Ferragamo, Loewe, Prada, Johanna Parv, The Row (middle)

Primarily inspired by the fall/winter 2026 Prada runway, the power of styling was a reminder that great style isn’t about owning more—it’s about seeing familiar pieces, like cardigans and tailored trousers, in a new light. Everyday wardrobe staples were transformed through unique pairings, layered basics, and unexpected accessories to create ensembles that demand a second glance. Styling, too, becomes a vessel for self-expression, allowing you to make each piece your own through personal touches and a careful attention to detail.

Photos: Courtesy of Dior, Chanel, Givenchy, Balenciaga, Proenza Schouler, Kallmeyer

Current, confident, and sophisticated, the Updated Lady emerged as one of fall-winter 2026’s defining archetypes, with houses like Chanel, Givenchy, and Balenciaga reimagining power dressing through a contemporary lens. Rather than rigid corporate suiting, the season favored softened structures, explored through gently sloping waists, sculpted jackets, and rich textures that balance strength with ease. The modern woman is multifaceted and full of contradictions, balancing polish with her own distinct edge—shouldn’t her wardrobe follow suit?

Photos: Courtesy of Gucci, Gorunway.com, Khaite and Alaïa

Bodies, bodies, bodies! The fall 2026 runways were an homage to the female form. From the second-skin dresses at Alaïa—some of which actually walked in flesh-colored nude—to Khaite’s sheer layers and Valentino’s lingerie-inspired slips, little was left to the imagination. Translating the trend into your own fall rotation may seem far-fetched, but by embracing it piece by piece, you can achieve an alluring look without going full naked dressing. Imagine Jil Sander’s knit bustier top with a slim trouser, Gucci’s cut-out leggings grounded by a crisp white tee, or one of Saint Lauren’s lace mini dresses beneath a sharp tailored blazer. To bring it back to one of the season’s strongest show notes—and one we outlined above: It all comes down to the styling.

It’s not unusual for fall trends to find inspiration in the countryside—whether it’s heritage plaids, khaki barn jackets, or some kind of cozy knit variation. As grounding as the source material may be, this season’s expression is more elaborate than ever. Nowhere is that more apparent than in the floral prints peppered throughout the collections: they’re richer, deeper, and even moodier than in years’ past. Par for the course, intricate floral brocades appeared at Dries Van Noten on everything from outerwear to midiskirts. Simone Rocha and Erdem also showcased their fair share of flora and fauna, from draped dresses to blouses, proving that the look can move between moods and occasions.

#6654. Meghan Markle Made a Subtle Change to Her Wedding Jewelry for a European Parliament Visit

For the solemn occasion, the Duchess of Sussex kept her look minimal, including her accessories and wedding stack. Meghan only wore her Welsh gold wedding band on her left ring finger, forgoing her engagement ring.

Meghan has a history of wearing just her wedding band while on trips abroad. Still, the piece is nearly priceless itself due to its material.

“Very little Welsh gold was ever mined, and it is traditionally used to make the British royal family’s wedding bands,” Hera Fine Jewelry founder and CEO Lauren Boc previously told ELLE while discussing Kate Middleton’s wedding stack. “Between its natural rarity and historic connections, I would consider her Welsh gold wedding band to be close to invaluable.”

Meghan accessorized her black Mackage coat with a few other gold pieces this morning, including her vintage Dior crystal earrings and Cartier Love bracelet.

Meghan has no other upcoming events that have been announced. Last month, following the Sussexes’ visit to Nottingham, a source close to the couple told The Telegraph that Meghan rarely joins Harry for engagements.

“They have different professional priorities,” one source explained. “Prince Harry’s is his philanthropic work and public service. Her focus is on her family and children, followed by her business, followed by rebooting her acting career. Her public appearances will be relatively rare. She is reading a lot of scripts at the moment. If she has time left after all of that, she will do some charity work. But she’s not a working royal; her day isn’t organized around a program pulled together by a private secretary. She’s a full-time mom and entrepreneur, and that alone takes up a lot of time and work.”

#6653. 13 Best Bathrobes for Men 2026: Reviewed by GQ

The best bathrobes for men will turn your modest apartment into a luxury spa in two seconds flat. Sadly, you'll still have to acquire all the relevant permits before you can charge randos for the home massage experience, but that's okay—a swaddly robe is more of a solo thing anyway.

The point: If you're gonna treat yourself to a robe, the robe has to treat you right, too. That means avoiding the cheap-o glorified towels with scratchy materials that just end up pilling after a couple washes. What you actually want is the creme de la creme of men’s bathrobes, the ones kitted out with the fluffiest, softest materials that take it from a mere post-shower dry-off to soul-filling self-care.

To help you in your quest for relaxation supremacy, we tested a slew of bathrobes to find the best of the best, which you'll find the below. While you're here, be sure to check out our guide to the best pajamas.

Imagine stepping out of the shower, wrapping yourself in a plush, thick towel... and then never having to take it off. Brooklinen’s best-selling towels are a mainstay of our home coverage, and the DTC brand's robes employ the same tricks of of the trade: absorbent, piled Turkish cotton repackaged into a calf-length, unisex bathrobe that feels as good as it looks. If ensconcing yourself in cloud-like, towel-caliber cotton isn't doing it for you, extra deep pockets to hide your snacks coupled with those eye-grabbing botanical stripes alone are worth the price of admission.

Quince is in the business of providing luxurious staples at non-luxury price tags. Its robe, one that feels like it could've been hung inside a five-star hotel room's closet, fits the bill. It's constructed from an ultra-soft long-staple cotton, complemented with two patch pockets for throwing in your hands (or the television remote) with a simple-to-use sash for making sure no one's getting a peek at your unsightly goods.

You can cancel that ritzy spa appointment: you don’t actually need to leave the house to score the type of restorative treatment that leaves you feeling refreshed and ready to dive back into your inbox—until, like, Wednesday. Turns out, all you need to cultivate peak spa vibes is a peak spa robe, like Onsen's deceptively absorbent, quick-drying silky Supima cotton number. Wearable in any climate, just looking at this cushy long robe from your steamy shower will set your mind at ease, and the extra-chunky waffle weave—cozy, but not clingy—more than justifies the price, especially when you consider the money you'll save not booking that retreat in the Alps.

If the de facto brand for cool-guy jammies doesn't make you want to cancel your plans and post up at home, its royally fluffy bathrobes will. The Scandi loungewear whisperers at Tekla cut their classic bathrobe from a soft-yet-sturdy midweight pile that’s intentionally slinky, so you can sashay from the bedroom to the kitchen fridge like you’re waltzing down a runway. There are, of course, generously-sized pockets for stashing your phone, remote, sundry snacks, etc. But the real selling point is the range of 19 (!) hyper-tasteful colors it's available in. The only con: limiting yourself to just one.

Dusen Dusen's playful takes on homewares and home decor make it the brand you turn to for something that'll have your houseguests going, “where'd you get that from?” Maybe you won't be wearing a robe in front of said guests, but they can admire its beauty when it's hanging in your bathroom. These printed robes are super plush for post-bath drying sessions, and cozy enough to accidentally fall asleep in while you're sitting on the couch waiting for your hair to dry.

There are robes you dry in, robes you lounge in, and then robes like this: an option meant to live in like you own a McMansion, smoke cigars every night, and enjoy a dram of scotch to “take the edge off.” This Derek Rose cotton robe has a satin-like finish, with a subtle stripe detail that gives it more of a pop than just a regular old blue colorway. The white piping also adds a bit more flair to what could otherwise be a boring robe, and three patch pockets help you tote your things around if you don't have anything going on under there.

Reigning Champ, the Vancouver-based brand behind some of our all-time favorite sweats, has been giving us the warm and fuzzies since we first pulled on its stellar heavyweight hoodie. So imagine our delight when Canada's foremost fleece whizzes brought their particular set of skills to the humble bathroom robe, juicing up the silhouette in the same loopback cotton-jersey that makes their loungewear so great. Wear this terry cloth robe while catching up on the Great British Bake Off from the comfort of your couch—and to make the inevitable trip to the grocery store for miscellaneous pie ingredients immediately afterwards.

This is type of robe you throw on when you want to toast things up but have zero plans to leave the house and therefore refuse to put on "actual" clothes. It has a sweet shawl collar and a full length that gives you all the cozy coverage you want.

What's better than sleeping on a cloud? Being swaddled in one while you slowly meander aorund your apartment. The Parachute robe offers a barely there feel, made possible thanks to its four-ply gauze construction. This one has side-seam pockets that make it easy to slip into

Hommey's catalog of cheerful colors and patterns help the brand stand out, but they're more than just nice to look at. They're made of a plush 100% cotton terry fabric that's as cozy as it gets and cut with a comfortable but un-sloppy silhouette. And while so many other robes use a shawl collar, we love that this one features an indulgently huge notch lapel.

Missoni's unmistakable and kaleidoscopic chevron patterns make for a suite of riche robes that telegraphs your bathroom is full of fancy soaps and lotions. Cut from an all-cotton French terry with a velour-like brushed hand, we'd understand if you'd never want to leave your home.

You may not live in a palazzo in Florence, but Brunello's rakish robe will make you feel like you do. The only thing missing? A cheeky cigar.

A Purple Label look, for Polo prices. Thank you, Ralph.

Material: A lot of fabric is about to touch your naked body, so you better make sure your robe feels good. Higher quality fabrics lead to higher price tags, but we found that it's worth it considering your whole body is about to be swaddled in it. For robes, cotton is the preferred fabric, and there are three common types: Egyptian cotton is the softest and plushes, Turkish cotton is known to be fast-drying, and supima cotton is just as soft as Egyptian cotton with the same quick-dry properties as Turkish. Lastly, you'll also see a lot of waffle weaves, which is mostly a design choice. Not only does it lend a certain look, we also like it because it's extremely absorbent and dries quickly.

Features: Robes don't offer too much in the features department, but at a bare minimum, find a robe with pockets. Two is probably the norm, with some offering a treasured third in the form of a chest pocket. Waist ties keep everything hidden and secure underneath, while some robes offer the option to remove them. And while maybe not a “feature” we chose to highlight robes that come in a variety of prints, patterns, and colors, because bone white robes for everyone isn't exactly fun.

Sizing: Shopping for robes is a lot like shopping for clothes: You need to find something that fits. We preferred to highlight robes that were size inclusive, in that a wide selection of sizes were available. Do note that some of the robes here feature combo sizes, which could make it difficult to figure out which option correlates to the typical shirts you'd wear. Take advantage of any size guides to really help you lock in your correct sizing.

Testing robes is no easy feat. We had to strip down to our skivvies, throw on a bunch of cozy options, and, well, chill. We made note of each robe's comfort factor, which usually had to do with the material of the robe. All of the robes we featured here are sure to suit the preference of basically anyone who would ever shop for a robe, whether they want a hood, prefer something to keep them dry and warm after a shower, or just lounge in like a baller.

#6651. In ‘Possible Love,’ Jeon Do-yeon Delivers the Year’s Most Shattering Performance. Can It Take Her to the Oscars?

In the nearly three decades since she made her big-screen debut in 1997’s The Contact, Jeon Do-yeon has earned a richly deserved reputation in South Korea—and far beyond it—as the kind of actor who inhabits her characters so fully that it ceases to feel like acting at all. (International audiences likely know her best for 2007’s Secret Sunshine, a wrenching portrait of a grieving mother that made her the first Korean performer to win an acting prize at the Cannes Film Festival.)

Critics often reach for the word “fearless” to describe her, but when we speak via Zoom at the tail end of a marathon press day in Seoul, Jeon waves that notion away.

“I’m always scared, and I’m full of fear, especially in front of the camera,” she says, with a laugh. “I’ve been working as an actor for quite some time, but it’s an ongoing process.” In fact, it’s important to her that she never gets too comfortable. “I actually enjoy having that fear and anxiety, and trying my best to work through it, because sometimes something completely new comes out of that struggle.”

That willingness to sit in discomfort is vividly, devastatingly evident in Possible Love, out now on Netflix after a theatrical run in October—and recently selected as South Korea’s official submission for the 2027 Academy Awards. The film marks the long-awaited return of legendary auteur Lee Chang-dong (arguably best known for the hypnotic 2018 masterpiece Burning, which became the first Korean movie ever shortlisted for the best international feature Oscar and topped critics’ year-end lists across the West) after an eight-year hiatus, and a reunion for Jeon and Lee nearly 20 years after Secret Sunshine.

Constructed with Lee’s signature novelistic sweep, Possible Love centers on the complicated dynamic between two married couples from starkly different strata of contemporary Korean society. Jeon stars as Mi-ok, a pragmatic mask-factory worker living quietly with her young son and her husband, Ho-seok (played by her frequent screen partner Sul Kyung-gu). Years after a mass factory layoff and a violently suppressed labor strike, Ho-seok remains haunted by the trauma. When the funeral of a former co-worker brings them into the orbit of Ye-ji (Cho Yeo-jeong, best known as the rich mom in Parasite), an intellectual documentary filmmaker chronicling laid-off laborers, and her wealthy, enigmatic husband Sang-woo (Zo In-sung), Mi-ok agrees to let Ye-ji film their daily lives. Yet what begins as a study of liberal empathy—and a moving portrait of across-the-divide solidarity—gradually warps into something far more strange and ambiguous, exploring the fault lines of privilege, hidden desire, and voyeurism.

Oscar buzz has swirled around her performance—a stunning and powerfully subtle tightrope walk of stoicism and simmering longing—since the film premiered at the Venice Film Festival in September, but Jeon remains pragmatic about what all this fanfare actually means. “When I won the award at Cannes 20 years ago, I remember thinking, Is this going to change my life? Probably not. Am I going to change as an actor because I have a Cannes award?” she reflects. “I’m very happy that we’re competing at the Oscars, and it’s wonderful that people are talking about the film, but I don’t think it will change me or the way I perform in any way.”

Below, Jeon discusses her on-screen reunion with Sul Kyung-gu, the unlikely magic fostered by Lee Chang-dong’s aversion to having his actors rehearse, and how a story of trauma and class tension deeply rooted in Korean society found universal resonance.

Vogue: Now that Possible Love is streaming on Netflix and reaching a massive global audience, how are you feeling? Have you been hearing much feedback?

Jeon Do-yeon: I watched the movie once again as soon as it was on Netflix. I think Possible Love is a type of movie that leaves a different afterglow for different people, and people can analyze it differently and interpret it in endless ways. Now that it’s on Netflix, I feel like people are finding their own ways to think about the movie—it leaves a lot of food for thought. I’m very proud that people are talking a lot about it.

To go back to the beginning, you obviously have a deep history with director Lee Chang-dong. How did he first approach you about Possible Love? I believe this idea had been gestating for almost a decade.

I do know that Director Lee worked on this project for quite a long time—he had it in his mind for more than 10 years. Ten years ago, he wanted to talk about this worker and his wife, the couple who had been laid off. Sometimes that story diverged into different paths, and sometimes they were scrapped; I know there were a lot of different versions along the way. When I knew that he finally had a script, I really wanted to know what it was about. I was waiting for that script. [Laughs.]

Given how layered the story is, what struck you most when you finally read it? What drew you to Mi-ok?

When I first read the script, I could feel the 10 years’ worth of thought and effort that Director Lee put into it. I was very touched reading it, and I was like, “Of course this is a Chang-dong film. It is this good.” And then I thought about what I could relate to about my character, Mi-ok. Of all the traits that she has, I think she holds this anxiety, and that anxiety stems from her wish to protect certain things. Although Mi-ok may seem a little bit fragile or weak from the outside, I think she’s a very strong woman inside, so that is something that I took note of.

Zo In-sung as Sang-woo, Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok, and director Lee Chang-dong on the set of Possible Love.

One of the aspects of your performance I found most striking is the physicality—the way Mi-ok holds herself is such an important part of how her story is told. Did you develop that beforehand, or did you find it on set?

If I hadn’t worked with Director Lee before, then I would have spent a lot of time trying to learn more about this character and to deeply understand her. But I know how he works, and he always stresses emptying my mind as an actor. He wants me not to express, but to feel the character. The preparation process for me was mostly about making sure that I get very accustomed to the lines that my character speaks, so it really feels like it’s me speaking, but it’s actually Mi-ok speaking through me.

You’ve worked with Sul Kyung-gu more than any other actor in your career, I believe. So much of the film relies on the subtleties of that relationship—did your real-life friendship and trust help?

I think I really know him well in real life, and as an actor as well. But while we were working on this project, and as he transformed into the character of Ho-seok, I discovered new facets of him. There’s always a new side of him that would surprise me, so I’m still looking forward to working with him on another project so I can discover yet another new side of him. We have this friendship and trust that we’ve built over the years, and I’m pretty sure that they helped. When you’re trying to express the chemistry of a very long relationship of a married couple, sometimes you have to make efforts to show that aspect. But we didn’t have to try too hard because we already had that trust built between us. If we do look like a real married couple in the movie, I think it’s partly thanks to that.

Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok and Sul Kyung-gu as Ho-seok.

At the opposite end of the spectrum, this was your first time working with Cho Yeo-jeong. The dynamic between Mi-ok and Ye-ji walks a very delicate line between sisterhood and something more transactional. Did the two of you discuss that, or did you prefer to discover it in the room?

We actually didn’t talk about our characters before going into production. When you work with Director Lee, he’s sort of against the actors having too many biases or prejudices or information about characters before going into the shoot, because he believes something very novel and fresh can come out when it’s your first time being in that scene, when it’s your first time talking. I didn’t want to jinx that by speaking with her about our characters beforehand. In the beginning, my character, Mi-ok, believes that if Ye-ji films us, then that is a way of protecting my family—a way of finding hope. But as they get closer, she realizes that there is a gap between the two people, herself and Ye-ji, and the wall grows higher and higher as they get closer.

Director Lee is known for his naturalism. Did that mean doing dozens of takes until you lost the sense that you’re acting? Did scenes ever head in unexpected directions based on how you were all interacting?

Director Lee is not actually the type who shoots a lot of takes, because he doesn’t really tell us exactly what it is that he wants from a scene. What he tries to do instead is to look into the chemistry and the conversation between the actors and try to find something new from it. Instead of Director Lee saying, “Okay, let’s do the lines,” he would just say, “Why don’t we talk? Let’s talk.” He likes everything very lively and raw, so that’s how we did it, and he didn’t want us to have a lot of rehearsals. And regarding whether the direction of a scene actually changed after working—it didn’t happen, because there was no fixed direction in the beginning.

Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok and Cho Yeo-jeong as Ye-ji.

Korean cinema has obviously exploded in popularity globally over the past decade, and Possible Love is getting a huge amount of international attention. How do you view this global wave? Do you think much about reaching Western audiences?

I don’t have plans on “going global” or anything as an actor! But I do hope that Korean content and Korean cinema will be beloved around the globe going forward as well. It’s just such a joy to be able to share different stories and different emotions with people that speak different languages and have different cultures. It’s such a good opportunity for us to transcend borders, transcend language. I hope more of those opportunities will come.

Possible Love is rooted in a very specific Korean tragedy, yet the hollowing out of industrial working-class communities is something people recognize all over the world. Did you worry at all during filming about whether such a locally specific story would translate, especially on a global platform like Netflix?

When we were filming, we actually did think about how this is a very Korea-specific sort of incident. Would people around the world be able to resonate with this story? Particularly because there is no dramatic event that happens throughout the movie; it’s just about the four people and their emotions, and that’s what really brings a narrative towards the end. We were a little bit worried. But I actually realized after screening overseas that the issue of laid-off workers is not something that’s specific to Korea—it’s happening all around the world, and it is still happening in some parts of the world. I could feel that a lot of people who are not Korean, who don’t live in Korea, are resonating with the themes and the emotions that are captured in the movie. And the moon at the very end of the movie? Anybody in any part of the world can see the moon. That’s something that’s very universal.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.
