#6648. The 15 Best Chelsea Boots for Men in 2026: Never Go Out of Style

The best Chelsea boots for men are trend-proof in a way that'll make you breathe a sigh of relief. Especially right now, when menswear feels like a WWE Royal Rumble, where anything goes and trends pile up like bodies in and out of the ring. But there, floating confidently above everything, is the trustworthy Chelsea boot.

If we had to make an educated guess as to why the Chelsea boot has stuck around for so long? Good design is good design. The slip-on simplicity, the sharp silhouette, the unadorned, uncomplicated details—the pared-back design makes it highly adaptable to any wardrobe and occasion, trends be damned. So don't be ashamed to cash in on a fleeting fad, but when it comes time for you to do some spring cleaning, we'd advise against letting go of a pair of Chelseas.

We've rounded up 15 of the best Chelsea boots out there, from hardwearing stompers to exquisite stunners, $200 steals to $1,500 beauties. Finding the perfect pair can be hard—but pulling them on? Easy.

Ask us what Chelsea boot you should get, regardless of price, and we'll point to the R.M. Williams Turnout boot, because it does so many things right that the competition can't. Off the bat, it’s a real stunner. The silhouette is sleek and chiseled, timeless and fashion forward. The leather is perfectly suited for a shine and polish to attend any black tie event, but takes on a handsome patina when worn hard. Plus, the quality is fantastic. The wholecut leather uppers are shaped as a single piece of cowhide rather than several panels stitched together, resulting in a shoe that’s as pretty as it is durable. With Goodyear-welted construction for easy peasy resoling, you’ll have these Aussie-born boots for well over a decade.

How does a boot originally built for Down Under cowboys manage to be the best-looking dress boot and hardest-wearing boot at the same time? Hard to say. The only drawback of the R.M. Williams Heritage Craftsman Chelsea is the price. But considering everything it can do—and how long it'll last—the math makes sense.

For the budget conscious/the dude looking for his first pair of “nice” Chelsea boots, my answer is easy: Beckett Simonon. Menswear bros might scoff at a web-only DTC clothing brand, but unlike the other brands with infinite marketing budgets looking to “disrupt the entire industry”, Beckett Simonon seems content with making great shoes at a great price, without all the soulless millennial pandering. The Bolton, a slick sleek, true-to-form  Chelsea boot, is just the poster child for the brand’s modus operandi. It uses full-grain Italian calfskin, which is lined with Vachetta leather, stitched by master shoemakers in Columbia using a Blake stitch construction.

In other words, it’s a Chelsea boot with A-list looks, Northamptonshire-style pedigree, and a price that’s at least half of the competition. But if there’s one thing that will stop most people from buying a pair immediately is, well, you can’t get them immediately. Each pair is made to order, which means you’ll have to wait a month or two to get yours. But you know what they say: patience is a virtue.

Some Chelsea boots lean heavily into their rockstar appeal, while others skew utilitarian, expressly designed to protect you from the elements. Then there, however, are the rare pairs that gracefully straddle the line between. Enter the other Australian boot: Blundstone. Those tough leather uppers might not be monsoon-ready, but on days when you just need to jump a few puddles en route to work, the whole package is the slickest way to ensure your socks stay dry without compromising your fit.

Tired Bushwick memes aside, Blunnies are truly good looking boots built like a tank. They can take a real whooping and still show up for their 5 a.m. shift behind the espresso machine, ready for more. They’re a boot you don’t have to feel precious about, and the de facto kicks to wear when the weather forecast just says “shitty.” They look great with vintage jeans, a pair of Dickies, cargo pants, and chinos—though, in our humble opinion, they don't belong anywhere with a dress code (explicit or implied).

Hedi Slimane’s legacy at Saint Laurent was all skinny jeans, razor-sharp suits, and rockstar Chelsea boots. The look he cooked up over a decade ago has come back again, with guys dubbing themselves Hedi Boys looking to recreate the magic. One glance at boots like these, and you understand why. A leading man gleam and a stacked Cuban heel mean these boots by themselves could headline Coachella. The only update: a very right-now edginess (literally) at the toe. Whether or not the rumored skinny jeans comeback arrives, these are the boots to wear when you want all eyes on you.

Astorflex has long been one of the best value propositions in the shoe space. The Tuscan vegetable-tanned leather and multigenerational craftsmanship alone should be worth more than what you'll actually pay. But value doesn't equate to style. Thankfully, the Bitflex has that in spades. Not too pointy and not totally bulbous, the shape splits the difference masterfully and looks great paired with any kind of pants. Plus, the cushioned crepe sole plays down the dressy factor while adding a ton of comfort straight out of the box.

Unlike other crepe-soled boots more famous than these—ahem—these don’t feel suspiciously soft, despite their extremely comfortable feel. In my experience, Astorflex boots maintain great arch support and mold to your feet, but also are built up enough to provide some noticeable ankle support. This is certainly the most casual Chelsea boot here, so don’t even think of trying to wear these to your friend’s wedding. You’re not “doing something”, you’re being disrespectful.

If you think of Red Wing, it's the brand’s iconic 6-inch moc toe boots you’re probably imagining. But the Minnesotan boot makers can do more than turn out jobsite-endorsed footwear. Introduced in the 1990s, Red Wing’s Chelseas were made for ranchers who wanted something durable enough for the farm, but easy enough to nap in. They’re a happy middle ground between a high-shine European Chelsea like the R.M. Williams and the woodsy Blundstones.

That means you get Goodyear-welted construction and built-to-stroll rubber outsoles topped by Hawthorne Muleskinner roughout suede. The texture is “rugged handyman,” but the dusty tan and dark brown color can hold down a coffee date-worthy fit. White tees and white jeans? Sure. Nice pants and a knit sweater or polo? Absolutely. Wranglers and a Stetson. Of course.

Morjas is best known for loafers worn by everyone from Wall Street bros to Greenpoint baristas. Now the Swedish shoemakers are coming for your ankles with a Chelsea boot. If you’ve already graduated from Chelsea Boots 101 by having a classic black or brown calf-leather pair in your closet, consider these brown suede beauties for next semester.

They're fully leather-lined but require absolutely zero break-in time, thanks to a studded rubber sole that feels like walking on a cloud... if clouds had traction. Style-wise, they walk the line (pun intended) between office-ready and weekend cool—equally dialed in when paired with a crisp white dress shirt beneath unforgiving Monday-morning fluorescents or some linen pants while you sip a doppia espresso in Florence.

You already know what that yellow stitching means. Dr. Martens’ chunky, iconic boots bring an EVA insole and the brand's patented oil- and slip-resistant AirWair sole to the water-resistant oiled leather. Add a pull tab for easy entry, and the only thing easier than slipping them on is adding them to cart when you realize they’re on sale.

By cutting out the middleman and selling direct-to-consumer, Meermin offers handsome, Goodyear-welted dress shoes—all made from leather with absurdly tony provenances—for well below what the other guys charge. You can’t go wrong with any of the brand’s boots, but a mocha-hued suede Chelsea looks especially killer in the fall.

Blundstones again, you say? Well, yes. But this pair has a leather lining and plusher sole, which make them both more comfortable and longer lasting, if for a slightly higher price.

From the rugged to the refined. Chelsea boots are named after London's affluent Chelsea neighbourhood, and this model from British quality shoemakers Crockett & Jones' lives up to that name perhaps more than any on this list.

The Spanish shoe-whisperers at Camper fly a little under the radar compared to their British counterparts, but their Chelsea boots offer just as much swaggering rock-god energy as any other pair on this list. Thanks to a cushy OrthoLite footbed, though, you can wear ‘em long after Mick Jagger takes his final bow.

The chatter about Our Legacy's Camion boots never seems to end. That handsome silhouette, the '90s-esque square toe, and the slick side zip make these a modern-day classic.

Viberg makes boots on steroids in its family-owned Canadian factory, turning out museum-worthy footwear designed to stomp through the worst the world—or the ground, at least—has to offer. These Chelseas are cut from a single piece of waxed leather, like a Barbour jacket for your feet.

NYC-based 3sixteen has been on an absolute footwear tear lately—first with its slick Saucony collab, and now with a lug-soled Chelsea boot that stands on business. The whole shoe smashes together slightly disparate details—an elegant tobacco-colored suede, a workmanlike wedge lugged sole, an skyscraper-height ankle—that add up to something fresh in the normally staid world of Chelsea boots. And if you’ve ever broken in a pair of 3sixteen’s top-tier jeans, you already know: things only get better as you put in the steps.

Chelsea boots have always looked cool—anywhere, on anyone, at any time. They were invented in the 1800s, but won their cultural cred later: the Beatles gave them a massive co-sign in the ’60s, then Hedi Slimane anchored his Saint Laurent rocker suits with them while rappers stacked their denim on top of Chelsea boots during the 2010s.

Which goes to show that Chelsea boots work with damn near anything in your closet, from your favorite Levi’s to baggy chinos to louche suits—even crew socks and shorts, depending on the boots and your taste for channeling some Pacific Northwest crunchiness. For more, check out our guide on how to wear Chelsea boots.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6647. Lauren Sánchez Bezos Matches Her Inky Red Bottoms With a Vintage Two-Tone Chanel Skirt

Lauren Sánchez Bezos has gone from television news anchor to talk show host to Bezos bride, and now, she’s making her name in the world of picture books. With the release of her debut children’s story, The Fly Who Flew to Space, back in 2024, Sánchez Bezos became a New York Times bestseller. And today, she released a new holiday-themed sequel and third installment in the series, titled The Fly Who Flew to the North Pole, which she promoted this morning with an appearance in New York City.

It may not be the holidays quite yet, but Sánchez Bezos showed off her unique, femme-fatale approach to yuletide style as she stopped by CBS Mornings to celebrate her book’s release. There, many of her dressing hallmarks were on full display. Luxury vintage? Check. A cinched silhouette? Check. Pops of bloody red? Check and check.

For the talk-show visit, Sánchez Bezos looked like a gothic Mrs. Claus in a black, skintight turtleneck from Wolford and a fuzzy black miniskirt that was cinched along the waist with a red tweed band that simulated a cummerbund.

This skirt wasn’t made by North Pole elves, though. Rather, it stemmed from the Chanel Fall/Winter 1994 ready-to-wear collection by Karl Lagerfeld, acting as the latest addition to Sánchez Bezos’s ever-increasing archival stock.

The two-tone mini also acted as the perfect complement to the former journalist’s inky patent-leather heels from Christian Louboutin, which featured the brand’s signature red bottoms.

To complete her look, Mrs. Bezos popped on a pair of oversize aviator sunglasses and silver, bow-shaped earrings. Plus, she carried a clasp clutch printed with the cover of her newly-released book (a cheeky marketing tactic that she’s employed in the past).

No matter the occasion, Sánchez Bezos will find a way to let her personality shine through.

#6646. How Creative Director Hype Is Lifting Resale

Hype around new creative directors’ hit pieces — from Matthieu Blazy’s pony hair heels for Chanel to Michael Rider’s massive Celine Disc sunglasses — is having a halo effect on the resale market that’s rippling across eras and raising prices on key items.

That’s according to Ebay’s latest Watchlist report, which goes live today and takes stock of the rising resale trends for Fall/Winter 2026. The report sheds light on how creative debuts over the past year are impacting what and who people are searching for and selling, and how far the hype is extending beyond current collections.

Searches for Matthieu Blazy, for instance, jumped 291% when his first Chanel collection hit stores. At the same time, listings for Karl Lagerfeld-era Chanel rose 87% year-on-year. Searches for Demna rose 42% following his first Gucci runway show, while listings for Tom Ford-era Gucci increased 49% year-on-year, reflecting how users capitalize on current interest to sell past collections. It follows the trend of pieces from defining designer eras increasingly entering circulation on Ebay, from Demna’s Balenciaga (up 204% year-on-year) to Phoebe Philo’s Chloé (up 165%).

At Dior, meanwhile, searches for Jonathan Anderson rose 92% following his first couture show. Average sales prices for John Galliano-era Dior, meanwhile, increased 81% in the same period.

It’s a shift reflected across the resale market. Blazy’s appointment drove searches for the designer up 10,976% on The RealReal, according to The RealReal’s 2026 resale report, released in August, reflecting the impact current brand heat can have on its past collections circulating in the secondhand market. On Ebay, Chanel didn’t rank among the top-purchased luxury brands from 2022 to 2024. In 2025, it snagged fifth place, and in 2026, it jumped to third, trailing Louis Vuitton and Gucci (which have held the top two spots since 2022).

“When someone new takes the helm, they’re not necessarily reinventing the wheel but very often revisiting the codes, silhouettes and references that made the house interesting in the first place,” says stylist Brie Welch, who worked on the report as Ebay’s resident stylist. “Seeing a brand reinterpreted through a new creative lens naturally sparks curiosity about its past collections.”

It’s a sign of where consumers are at. Shoppers are following brands and their runway shows closely — evidenced by their inclination towards ultra-specific fashion search terms and the impact of a debut show on searches, listings, and sales. That consumers are turning to secondhand marketplaces like Ebay is indicative of shoppers’ desires to tap into current trends while finding better value. It’s reflective of trends in the primary marketplace: spending isn’t expected to dip, but consumers are placing greater emphasis on perceived value. If shoppers determine that this is better in the secondary marketplace than the primary, brands ought to pay attention.

Galliano’s Dior isn’t the only category seeing a price hike. Cultural heat has driven luxury secondhand prices up across the board, per Ebay’s findings. However, the fact that luxury prices continue to tick up has likely also played a role in the spike. Still, Kirsty Keoghan, Ebay’s general manager of EU fashion and luxury, credits many of the price increases (measured for this report from January to September 2026) to celebrity sightings.

The Row’s Margaux Bag, for instance, continues to climb in price, up 137% year-on-year. Celebs including Zoë Kravitz and Kendall Jenner sported the bag during this timeframe. Demand for Gucci’s Giglio tote, carried by Dakota Johnson and Suki Waterhouse, was up 103%.

“There is an intentionality about owning something with cultural relevance, longevity, and a sense of value,” Keoghan says, noting that shopping for secondhand designer fashion has become more culturally responsive.

This ties into the return of the It-bag, as shoppers look for styles they’re seeing blow up in culture. Global listings for Balenciaga’s Le City bag, which is having yet another resurgence, are up 936%. (Who could forget Sienna Miller getting papped in a denim mini skirt with her red city bag slung over her shoulder back in 2004? Now, the bag — which had its 25th anniversary on Monday — is on the arm of everyone from Harris Dickinson to Bella Hadid.) The Loewe Amazona 180 (up 440%), the Valentino Garavani VLogo Bag (up 358%), and the Bottega Veneta Veneta Bag (up 158%) are also seeing surges in listings, which Welch puts down to celeb style mainstays such as Kravitz and Jacob Elordi getting papped wearing and styling such bags.

Current runway trends are also feeding back into secondhand market purchases, as consumers seek to tap into what’s trending now via previous season pieces. At a moment when consumer anxiety is still high — which isn’t necessarily putting a dent on spending — opting for a pre-loved item when making a purchase as a style choice can feel like a safer bet versus a brand-new splurge, even as resale prices hike with demand, Keoghan says.

“We are seeing how quickly a new runway moment can evoke an interest for shoppers in a brand’s archive, sending them in search of special pieces with a story,” Keoghan says.

Prada skirts were a breakout trend not just in fashion discourse. Seemingly inspired by the show, shoppers turned to the secondhand market to source past collection skirts in the days following Prada’s SS27 show on September 22 in Milan. Global searches for “Prada skirt” spiked more than 360% compared to the beginning of September, reflecting a runway-inspired uptick.

“Suddenly, you’re looking at a sort of dated silhouette differently,” Welch says, noting that consumers are finding success looking at the secondhand marketplace for A-line skirts that exist in the market. “You can find an earlier version of the idea, sometimes at a more accessible price point, and make it your own.”

This season’s emphasis on shoes was also a search booster for resale. On Ebay, Chanel — which has had a heavy rotation of slingbacks on recent runways — seemed to have the biggest impact once again. Searches for slingbacks are up over 65% versus the start as fashion month, and searches for Chanel slingbacks are up 50%.

“People are shopping with a sharper point of view; they want to be part of the conversation, and they are shopping pre-loved to achieve that,” Keoghan says.

#6645. How to Avoid Sports Injuries in Your 30s, 40s, and Beyond

At 38 years old, I’ve been fortunate enough to go most of my life without being bothered by sports injuries, despite being consistently and competitively active for the better part of three decades. On the soccer field, I’ve played on and off at club level since primary school. (I’m currently holding it down as a winger on my local club’s 30+ “seniors” team.)

Meanwhile, as a health journalist and all-around enthusiastic exerciser, I’ve thrown myself at countless physical challenges—mostly for fun, sometimes to help pay the rent. Marathons, trail races, triathlons, 24-hour relays, mud runs, obstacle course races, Hyrox—you name it, I was probably obsessed with it for a time. And through it all, the only injury I ever incurred was a torn meniscus (the undignified result of attempting a wildly above-my-paygrade basketball dunk on a charitably lowered rim, at a promotional event). If only the surgeon could have extracted the memory of that day along with the frayed connective tissue he removed from my knee. I’m pretty sure I was wearing jeans, too.

All of this to say, everything was smooth sailing for a very long time, until it suddenly wasn’t. Somewhere around the age of 35, for reasons beyond my comprehension at the time, the wheels started to come off the bus. First, a stress fracture to my foot. I noticed it one night in bed and couldn’t tell you when it happened. Regardless, it put me in a boot for three months.

Next, a torn hamstring during a routine game of pick-up football, making the same run I’d made so many times before. I thought I’d just pulled it, so I kept playing, which of course only made it worse. That decision cost me six months. Then a strained calf muscle at a gentle session of run club. And, finally, shin splints. I don’t even remember how many months that one took me out for, but it felt like forever.

This barrage of sports injuries—all sustained within a roughly two-year period—was unrelenting, each overlapping pang seemingly passing the baton to the next. Needless to say, I was miserable. And the worst part was that I couldn’t understand why this was happening to me. I had been maintaining the same activity level, same diet, same supplements, and the same sleeping habits.

You always hear people talking half-jokingly about how getting older takes its toll on the body. But nothing really prepares you for that moment when you start to actually experience it for yourself. It’s existential. Brutal. Depressing, honestly. Sometimes the best way to deal with these spiral-inducing moments is to simply talk to other people dealing with the same thing. So I recently asked my, er, seasoned soccer team’s group chat: What do you do to avoid and manage sports injuries as you get older?

Kevin, 40, chimes in first: “There’s no avoiding, mate. Just managing.”

“Funnily enough, I’ve actually started doing yoga, which has been great for mobility and stretching out my lower back,” writes Chris, 35, who battles with a recurring quadratus lumborum (QL) issue. “I was always a bit hesitant to give it a go—not because I didn't think it would help, but because I couldn't get past the fact that I'd essentially be following a similar exercise plan to my mum! I think that’s been one of the bigger adjustments as I’ve gotten older: accepting that looking after your body sometimes means doing things you might have previously associated with an entirely different generation.”

“For me, I think the most important thing is to keep going in general,” adds Brian, who recently turned 42. “I remember when I was around 20 years old, my manager at the time, who was in his late 30s and retired from playing, was doing some running on his own before the team training session started. The lads were joking and teasing him about making a comeback, but I remember him saying, ‘If you stop moving at my age, you won't be able to start again.’ I think that’s good advice.”

In some way, I suppose it’s comforting to know that this is something we all have to deal with, whether you’re a Sunday league bench warmer or in contention for the Ballon d’Or. "With age, there’s just a higher incidence of soft-tissue injuries as your muscles lose strength and elasticity,” says Dr. Brian Forsythe, a sports medicine surgeon, associate professor at Rush University Medical Center, and recreational soccer player himself. According to Forsythe, who also serves as head orthopedic officer for MLS team Chicago Fire FC and president of the Professional Soccer Physicians Society, over time athletes across the skill spectrum will ultimately begin running into the same injuries.

You may not be able to halt the inevitable march of time, but there are certainly ways to minimize and slow its toll on your body. This requires some work but could ultimately be the difference between hanging up your boots and kicking on for another 10, 20, or even 30 years.

“It's up to you to take some responsibility,” Forsythe says. “Think about the lifestyle you had when you were 17 or 18—how you took care of your body and what you looked like. Then look in the mirror and ask yourself, ‘How much of my life now resembles what it was then?’ If you’re going out and having a few pints, understand that that inhibits your recovery and decreases protein synthesis. If you’re overweight, that has effects beyond just not looking great in your clothes. It’s increased joint load, increased rotational torque. You’re more likely to get injured. So you have to set realistic expectations of what your body is capable of doing. But you can definitely still do a lot.”

If you’re not ready to call time just yet, here are a couple of strategies you can start implementing today, courtesy of Forsythe and a handful of other experts.

“Number one, and it should be obvious, is just general conditioning,” says Forsythe. Being a slightly less sedentary human being during the work week means you won’t send your body into total shock when you decide to get going over the weekend.

“A lot of injuries come from that mismatch between what we do all week and the intensity we demand from our bodies on the weekend,” says sports medicine physician Dr. Alex McDonald. As Dr. James B. Chen, orthopedic surgeon at DISC Sports and Spine Center, puts it: “The biggest risk isn't necessarily your age, but your schedule.”

“When you think about playing in your teens and twenties, you were probably close to your ideal body weight,” says Forsythe. Ideal body weight, or IBW, refers to the weight at which your body is able to function most efficiently and without a heightened risk of injury. “All of the forces across your joints are multiplied almost exponentially with additional pounds on your frame,” says Forsythe. For example, “the force across your knee can be up to six times your body weight.”

“If you've ever been overweight and you climb up and down stairs and notice that the front of your knee hurts, that's because of those increased pressures and forces,” adds Forsythe.

Jogging a couple of miles a week may help you get through your Sunday workout our pick-up game, but it’s not doing anything to prevent in-the-moment injuries. To do that, you have to train the way you play—whatever game that is. “It's changes of direction," says Forsythe. It's rapid acceleration and deceleration. It's pivoting, twisting, turning.” Basically, bring back whatever drills you used to do on your sports team when you were younger, and adjust them for the demands of the sport you currently play.

No matter how frequently and comprehensively you train, a complete warm-up before any big athletic event is a non-negotiable. "It helps dramatically, which is why you'll always see professionals warm up for 30-45 minutes before a match,” says Forsythe. When it comes to soccer, FIFA actually has developed a warm-up program, called FIFA ll+, specifically for injury prevention. “It’s a free routine that only takes about 20 minutes, and includes running drills plus strength, balance, and landing exercises such as Nordic hamstring curls and single-leg balance work,” says McDonald. “It’s been shown to cut injuries by about a third, but it needs to be done at least twice a week—ideally three or four times a week—to really reduce the risk of injury.”

Stretching after your warm-up is important, too, but just be careful not to overdo it. “If you stretch too aggressively, you can actually predispose yourself to muscle tendon injuries,” says Forsythe. “First warm up, and then stretch out within your range of comfort. But right before a game is not the time to all of a sudden correct tight hamstrings.”

Often, being sidelined by an injury can be more painful than the injury itself, causing guys to get back out there earlier than they probably should. “A common issue that I see is when someone sustains a minor sprain or strain, and they return to sport-specific activity too early,” says Chen, “which can result in either worsening the injury or even picking up a secondary injury.” As annoying as it might be to sit out for 2 or 3 weeks with a strained hamstring, turning that into a tear will bench you for far longer. Believe me.

Playing sports in your 30s, 40s, and beyond also means coming to grips with your body’s increasing need for routine recovery between training sessions and games. "You have to recognize that your body needs more time to recover,” says Forsythe. You may have great cardiovascular fitness and feel ready to play on consecutive days, but your muscles and connective tissues need time to bounce back. “It’s important to respect fatigue, and ensure proper recovery after games or training,” says McDonald.

To be fair, this is universally sound guidance regardless of age. But it's advice that tends to take decades to sink in. “I think that one of the key advantages of playing as you get older is that you know your body better in your 30s and 40s,” Brian’s response concluded. “You understand what it's saying to you now. You might not have even listened when you were 21!”

A version of this story appeared in British GQ.

#6643. The Most Extravagant Horseback Trips in the World

The horse girl is having a moment. Riding boots are officially trending. Kendall Jenner recently celebrated her 31st birthday with an equestrian-themed fête. And who could forget earlier this year in Paris, when Stella McCartney kicked off her winter 2026 show at a riding hall in the Bois du Boulogne by sending out 10 horses—five black, five white, all chic?

Fashion, it seems, has discovered the horse. But horses have never needed discovering: Gucci and Hermès built empires on saddles and tack long before equestrianism found its way back into the fashion conversation. It may be the lunar calendar’s Year of the Horse, but for some of us, every year is Year of the Horse. For those ready to graduate from dressing like a horse girl to living like one, here are some of the world’s most extraordinary horseback adventures, from Patagonian ridge lines to African safaris. Some of them are multi-day journeys across wild landscapes, and others allow for deep immersion into single properties that offer particularly exceptional horse experiences. Saddle up.

In the rolling pampas of Uruguay just inland from luxe-boho beach town José Ignacio, Estancia Vik offers horseback riding with a pretty spectacular dinner reservation. Guests saddle up on Criollo and Quarter horses alongside expert gauchos for an evening ride timed to the rising of the full moon. The ride begins at the stables before sunset, winds toward the José Ignacio River, and culminates in wine and a traditional Uruguayan dinner under the stars. The estancia also offers equine meditation, through which you can connect with the herd and experience just how intuitive these animals really are.

One of my personal favorite horseback experiences anywhere was in the vast, unending salt pans of the Makgadikgadi in Botswana. Here, at Natural Selection’s Jack’s Camp, guests visiting at certain times of year can ride alongside 30,000 zebras and wildebeest at the peak of their migration before the rainy season.

For experienced riders, Micato Safaris offers a nine-night “Riding the Wild Plains” equestrian safari through Nairobi, Laikipia, and the Maasai Mara. The idea is to experience Kenya’s wildlife from the saddle, crossing through conservancies alongside giraffes, elephants, and rhinos with little more than the sound of hooves beneath you. After two nights at the posh Hemingways Nairobi, guests head into the bush for a trip through some of the country’s top safari lodges. But you’re hardly roughing it. Think champagne breakfasts and sundowners overlooking the savanna.

Few travelers know Central Asia quite like explorer, author, and television presenter Alexandra Tolstoy. A relative of the novelist Leo Tolstoy, she has been traveling the region since the 1990s, when she and three friends embarked on a nine-month, 5,000-mile journey by horseback and camel along the Silk Road, from Turkmenistan to China. More than 25 years later, Kyrgyzstan remains her great love. Tolstoy leads journeys through the country’s dramatic Tien Shan Mountains, drawing on decades of relationships with local horsemen, nomadic families, and mountain communities.

“Kyrgyzstan is, quite simply, one of the most extraordinary and beautiful countries in the world to explore on horseback,” Tolstoy told us. “Horses are deeply woven into the country's history, identity, and everyday life. This is a place where horsemanship is not a recreational pursuit but an integral part of the culture, and that makes the experience particularly special.” Not ready to commit to a trip? Check out Tolstoy Dacha, her line of beautiful objects for home and travel.

Many come for the skiing and hiking, but real horse lovers come for the riding. Above the town of Merano, just steps away from Miramonti Boutique Hotel, the Sulfner riding stable is home to the region’s distinctive flaxen-maned Haflinger horses, with guided excursions, riding lessons, and carriage rides available. After a day in the saddle, there’s an outdoor Japanese-inspired onsen pool and forest sauna to look forward to.

For experienced horseback riders, Tswalu Kalahari Reserve offers a different way to explore the largest private reserve in South Africa—by riding across grass-covered plains, over rolling red dunes, and over seasonal riverbeds. With a stable of breeds including Thoroughbreds, Arabians, Appaloosas, and English Shires, Tswalu’s horseback safaris bring adventurous travelers closer to the landscape and wildlife, offering an immersive perspective beyond the traditional game drive.

Chile offers a compelling case for exploring on horseback. This neck of the continent is known for excellent hiking, yes, but horseback allows you to cover much more ground in a single trip. Luxury travel company Red Savannah offers a 10-night riding “Chilean Riding Adventure” that begins in the Atacama Desert, where guests ride across the otherworldly sands of Moon Valley before spending evenings stargazing under some of the clearest skies on Earth. The trip continues on to the Lake District and ultimately down to Patagonia, where you’ll canter along remote lagoons beneath the watch of Andean condors. You’ll stay in luxe lodges from AndBeyond and Explora along the way.

In the heart of France’s Camargue, where wild marshlands meet the Mediterranean, horses are as much a part of the landscape as the salt flats and flamingos. At Les Bains Gardiens, a countryside retreat with its own stables, guests can saddle up for guided two-hour rides through the wetlands and out to the beach. Experienced riders can even gallop along the shoreline. The estate is home to traditional Camargue horses and often stages “jeux gardians,” the horseback games that celebrate this region’s centuries-old riding culture.

#6641. How Lafayette 148 Became New York’s Newest Heritage Brand

What’s Lafayette 148’s secret to longevity? After 30 years in business, the company has claimed a seat as one of New York’s newest heritage labels. Co-founder and CEO Deirdre Quinn attributes that status to a combination of consistency and evolution, the balance of knowing what needs to change over time and what absolutely cannot.

The brand’s biggest turning point was during the pandemic, when abrupt shifts in customer demand collided with supply chain challenges that threatened to put the company out of business. Quinn and Emily Smith, Lafayette 148’s creative director who joined the brand in 2002 as an intern, made the decision to move the label further upmarket, recommitting to its vertically integrated business model to protect margins and avoid the trappings of order minimums that most brands have to meet. They invested in better quality materials and shifted the majority of sales from wholesale retail to its direct-to-consumer network of stores and e-commerce, with more full-price sellthrough even as prices have increased in step with quality improvements.

Lafayette 148 creative director Emily Smith.

Today, Quinn says she sees Lafayette 148’s competition being less fellow American brands and more as European luxury brands. The brand holds a two-hour slot for its presentations on the New York Fashion Week calendar (Quinn and Smith say they prefer the time to mingle with guests and clients that presentations allow), and this year, started hosting a showroom in Paris for the first time, and also started selling in Harrods last year. The brand projects sales for 2026 will be up 12% over 2025, to $150 million, growth that Quinn attributes to the luxury pivot.

Now, Lafayette 148 is eyeing a bigger US takeover. This fall, the brand opened its first store in Dallas, Texas — an investment more brands are making following the shuttering of the city’s Neiman Marcus — and has plans to open its first stores on the West Coast in 2027. Abroad, the Middle East and Japan are two regions the team is eyeing. That expansion is bolstered by the company’s first C-suite supporting Quinn, including a chief marketing officer, chief merchant and chief commercial officer.

In a joint interview, Quinn and Smith spoke about the recipe for succeeding as an independent label in New York through intentional reinvention.

Vogue: Lafayette 148 is celebrating 30 years in business this year. What are the steps you took to ensure you’re still standing today?

Deirdre Quinn: Luckily, as a privately owned company, we were able to evolve into what works for us. We want people who want our brand. I don’t need to force it. I’ve watched a lot of people come and go in my 30 years. Luxury has always been the goal for us. So we had to rebuild. Now, we live in the next six months because if that last several years have taught us anything, it’s that you can’t plan like you used to. It makes it exciting.

Emily Smith: For me, it comes down to the quality. We’ve always had really great quality, and I think that’s because we’re vertical. I’ve been working for Deirdre for many years. I grew up here and grew up within the factory, and so I just knew the potential and that we could push it even more. We’re more innovative today with our suppliers, with more of our point of view. That has been the defining difference between where we were and how we’ve evolved. And that part’s been really fun because each season you can stick to the DNA, but just move it a little bit further each season, and move our client along a little bit every season too, but not to a place where she doesn’t recognize it anymore. That’s really important.

Vogue: What advice would you give to a brand starting out today? What advice do you still find yourself seeking?

DQ: I deal with a lot of young brands that want advice and how to get into this industry and what do I think? I usually say to people, “You might not want to hear it from me because I’m a very straight shooter and I have to be honest. I’m not going to act like this is an easy industry. It’s a really tough industry and you should learn from others before you get too far down a road that you didn’t realize was so complicated.”

The advice that I would ask might be around the best way to finance a privately owned business. That’s quite interesting to me. Certainly the thoughts on being more of a global business and what experiences they’ve had in it, doing more non-sized business. We are such a sized business — we have 58 sizes — and it’s the hardest part because of the engineering that goes into making a garment look good.

The label, previously a mid-tier department store name, has transformed itself into a luxe favourite for high-end customers. How did it pull it off?

Vogue: Have you ever considered raising outside investment?

DQ: I thought about it at the worst possible times — the times where you needed help with your cash flow — and that would be a horrible time to sell the company because you wouldn’t get what you put into it. Today, I’m not looking at all. I don’t need the distraction. I have learned to never say never, but for now, I think it’s too exciting for us. I have a plan through 2030, and I’m optimistic about that.

It’s such a power and privilege to not have to answer to someone else. I know that I am a very hands-on owner, and I would be so afraid of somebody coming in here and the first thing they want to do is buy less expensive fabrics or something that just starts a race to the bottom. And it doesn’t mean that there might not someday be some amazing person or company that says, “Wow, you’ve built an American luxury brand. That’s not normal around here. Together, we could take it to another level.” But again, for now, I’m just really focused on rebuilding and continuing to grow what we have and both top and bottom line.

Vogue: You’ve described Lafayette’s customers as successful career women. How do you evolve who your customer base is? Are you thinking about how to capture younger generations?

ES: I want to take a step back to take a step forward on that one. When we were working on the 30th collection, I came across some old sketches that were by the first designer here. And there was sort of this sense of responsibility that you always have in this position to maintain the brand and maintain the clients and never change it too much. And it was really fun to go back and look at where we had started and to see the progression of how it’s evolved. The biggest takeaway was that when Lafayette started, it was addressing career women who needed boardroom suits for the office every day. And look how much our lifestyles have completely changed.

Those are things you almost don’t think about. You just subconsciously are evolving them because we are the clients. We know what those changes are. We are in touch with them. I think it’s more of a sensibility than really thinking about a demographic. We never talk about an age range. We know our customer is an accomplished woman. She’s busy at any age, at any stage in her career or whatever she has going on, and that idea of versatility and wardrobing and the product that she has in her closet is always relevant. I think the one thing I’ve heard consistently from all of our clients is usually she wants to be taken seriously. She never wants to walk in a room and feel silly or fussy or like the clothes are wearing for her. I think that can cross any age range and just making sure that we stay true to our brand DNA and all of that just will kind of translate to any demographic.

Behind the scenes of Lafayette 148’s 30th anniversary campaign shoot with costume designer-turned-model Sophie de Rakoff.

ES: Not really. Proportion and relevance are two important things for me, and technology, because I do think it affects fabric constructions and things like that. But we’re not interested, and our client’s not interested, in what’s the new hot thing right now. To me, it’s more the hemlines are up or down, little things that are more subconscious than specifically this is the statement for the season. I love when people are like, “What’s the color of the season?” I’m like, “I don’t know.” That feels like such an old-fashioned thing to ask, right? It’s sort of what works for you. You should dress for what works for your shape, your style. For us, tailoring is always more important.

As far as building and growing our base, to me, it’s more about getting our brand in more conversations and having more people wearing it, I think is the biggest growth opportunity. I think we’ve been a hidden secret, and there’s sort of a nice nuance to that. But I think just slowly getting it to other people at a healthy pace that feels manageable that we don’t ever sacrifice the integrity of what we’re doing. And potentially we do collaborations with other categories, but we’re so focused on ready to wear and that’s our expertise.

DQ: We’re open to that. I think building the brand and investing in the marketing is really a focus for now because I think that’s what helps set us apart on top of the product. It has to feel right.

Vogue: How do you plan to maintain this growth from here, what comes next?

DQ: We’ve planned it into the future. Luckily as a privately owned company, we’ve able to evolve into what works for us. We want people that want our brand. I don’t need to force it. [As we continue to expand into Europe], I think the best part will be, we’re a 30-year-old company. We’re not this new brand. We’ve got the stability and the infrastructure to support everything it takes to build a proper long-term partnership.

I am lucky that my partners don’t push me. I don’t have a board. I don’t have a boss, so I don’t focus on a metric like having to hit double-digit growth. Single digit is fine. It’s an extremely open-book type of operation that we’re running, and my team has been with me through the bad times, and I want them to be with me through the good times. We’ve had some really tough times in the last five years, and now I see a really bright future, so I'm excited. I’m more excited for them than for anything else.

#6640. No-Knead Pumpkin Focaccia

For those who fall into the pumpkin-should-be-savory camp, let us offer up a new recipe primed to become a seasonal staple: Pumpkin Focaccia. The iconic bread we know and love is ripe for the pumpkin treatment, lending it a golden hue and earthy sweetness. This version doesn’t require any kneading, making it straightforward enough to pull off in a single afternoon. You’ll perform a series of folds as it rises, simply pulling and stretching the dough to help it develop gluten. A showering of fresh rosemary and flaky salt on top is optional, but it takes crisp, plush focaccia from good to great. Serve it with a bowl of olive oil for dipping or a schmear of cream cheese to lean into the seasonal profile.

This recipe is part of Pumpkin Week 2026, Bon Appétit’s annual celebration of autumn’s most magical pantry staple.

Mix one ¼-oz. envelope active dry yeast (about 2¼ tsp.), 5 cups (625 g) all-purpose flour, 1 cup canned pumpkin purée, 1 Tbsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar, and 1½ cups warm water in a large bowl until a dry, shaggy dough forms. Cover with plastic wrap and let sit in a warm spot 15 minutes.

Place a small bowl of room-temperature water on work surface for easy reaching. Uncover dough and moisten hands in water, shaking off excess. Stretch 1 side of dough up and over to meet opposite side; turn bowl 90°. Repeat process 3 more times, turning bowl after each fold. (This constitutes 1 full fold.) If dough doesn’t always stretch to meet opposite side, just aim for the middle. Remoisten your hands and shake off excess water any time dough begins to feel sticky. Cover dough and let sit 30 minutes (set a timer). Repeat entire process 2 more times (folding and resting), about 1 hour more. By the last full fold, the dough should be significantly smoother and tighter.

Pour ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil into a 13x9" metal baking pan. Lightly oil your hands and punch down dough, then transfer to prepared pan. Sprinkle with flaky sea salt. Turn dough salted side down to coat both sides in oil. Press into an even layer with your fingers. Cover with plastic wrap and let sit in a warm spot until puffy, about 45 minutes.

Place a rack in bottom third of oven; preheat to 450°. Unwrap focaccia and scatter rosemary leaves and more sea salt over if desired; drizzle with oil. Oil your hands and press fingers into focaccia several times to create dimples across surface, making sure to press fingers all the way down to the bottom of the pan. Bake focaccia, rotating pan front to back halfway through, until deep golden brown, 23–28 minutes. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let focaccia cool slightly in pan. Using a metal spatula, turn out focaccia onto rack and let cool completely.

Do Ahead: Focaccia can be made 1 day ahead. Store tightly wrapped at room temperature.

#6639. All About Este Haim’s Husband, Jonathan Levin

Este Haim is one of three sisters forming the superstar pop group Haim. The trio has historically been very discreet about their personal lives. Most of their stories are shared through their songs, which are often about both love and heartbreak. In 2023, Este did share some more intimate thoughts with The Cut about self-love.

“I don’t think I truly understood self-love until the last three or four years,” she said. “There’s an air of confidence that comes from just loving yourself, the good and the bad.”

But in mid-February 2025, the oldest of the musical sisters revealed on Instagram that she was engaged to tech CEO Jonathan Levin. The post showed Este smiling in celebration, flashing a square-cut diamond engagement ring and wearing a T-shirt that read, “I’M TAKEN.”

On Jan. 1, 2026, a source told People that the couple had gotten married on New Year’s Eve.

Here’s everything to know about Levin and his relationship with Este so far.

Levin is originally from the United Kingdom, where he studied at the University of Bristol for economics before attending the University of Oxford, where he received a master’s in philosophy and economics, per his LinkedIn profile. Levin then moved to New York City, where he cofounded Chainalysis, a cryptocurrency company.

Talking to The Big Whale in February 2025, Levin said he “discovered crypto in 2012 at a time when only computer scientists were researching Bitcoin, which was then the leading cryptocurrency.”

Levin said he wrote a number of academic studies on the topic and “why people use cryptocurrencies,” believing this understanding would support industry growth. Levin has stated he wants to “bring greater trust and transparency to cryptocurrency markets.” He was made CEO of the company in December 2024.

Chainalysis is known for focusing on tracing data and attempting to institute safety measures around blockchains and cryptocurrency. Levin has even testified before the U.S. Congress around national security and blockchain technology.

The couple has not shared anything about how they got together or how long they’ve been dating. However, fans have noticed Levin on Este’s arm at various events dating back to 2023, when he was her date at Taylor Swift’s birthday that December.

In September 2024, he joined Swift and Este in a suite for a Kansas City Chiefs game and was seen with the friend group again that month at the US Open.

Since announcing her engagement, Este has been posting on Instagram with allusions to Levin. Her first came on Valentine’s Day, where she shared a screenshot of herself FaceTiming him in her Instagram Stories. She called him her “love,” writing, “happy valentine’s day, i love you j.”

She then posted about Haim’s upcoming album on March 12, writing in the caption, “i don’t think my fiancé is mentally prepared for relationships to be out.”

Este told Vogue on Jan. 1, 2026, that Levin popped the question on Dec. 22, 2024, during a hike. “I had Mario Badescu acne medication on, my hair was in a top knot, I had no makeup on, I was in sweats and sneakers,” she said.

He had a placeholder ring, she recalled: “A beautiful ring still, but the second he was like, ‘Will you marry me?’ and I said yes, he also was like, ‘We need to design this ring, because this is not the final thing.’ I thought [that] was really, really sweet.”

A source told People that they tied the knot on Dec. 31, 2025, with Vogue revealing that they had their “intimate ceremony in California.”

Este spoke to Vogue about her Louis Vuitton wedding dress, saying, “I’ve been looking forward to this day since I was five. When I was five, I had this vision of me, coming down a spiral staircase, there’s a scene in Cinderella that probably is what I was mimicking, but I would descend in this giant ballroom gown, and then my fiancé, [my] husband, would be at the bottom and grab my hand.”

On July 13, Kloss shared an Instagram Story from Jay-Z’s concert at Yankee Stadium featuring herself and husband Joshua Kushner seated with Este and Levin.

Kloss and Este had been guests at Taylor Swift’s wedding 10 days before.

At the New York City premiere of One Night Only, Haim shared some of the relationship advice she applies to her own marriage to Levin.

“Keep pursuing, even if you’re in a relationship,” she told People. “You should always be dating. You should always be working toward having a better relationship. Relationships are work, and I think people forget about that.”

In a November 10 interview with People, Haim elaborated on her previous advice about continuing to date your spouse, sharing some of the ways she and Levin keep their relationship exciting.

“I think people misunderstood that,” she said. “What I meant was, you need to continue leaving little notes for each other. Flowers are nice. Don’t get complacent—and separate bathrooms, I think, are also pretty essential.”

Haim also revealed that she and Levin recently returned to Pioneer Works in New York City, where they had their first date.

“We went back to where we went on our first date. We went to [NYC nonprofit cultural arts center] Pioneer Works...It was a day of music and art, and there was good beer and wine, and it was just really nice to go back to our roots,” she said.

#6637. Balmain Resort 2027 Menswear

“For me, the men’s and the women’s, they are kind of like one entity. I really want the men and women to be able to talk to each other.” Antonin Tron was riffing as we riffled the rails of his first pre-spring collections for Balmain, including this first-ever menswear collection for the house. “I don’t necessarily think of them as a couple,” he qualified: “but I think at Balmain the women are quite sexy and out there. And if I was to do something very different for men, it would be weird.”

Looking at his rails simultaneously, that relationship was manifest in substance as well as spirit: “We have a lot of overcrossing material,” Tron confirmed. Yet there was one fundamental difference in his approach to the two wardrobes: while his Balmain woman is an imagined character, his man is more internalized. “I’m also making things that I can’t find myself,” he said. The tailoring block, for instance, arose from his frustration with suits that were either too narrow or too oversized. Here the shoulders were strong, the waist defined and the fit flattering without constriction: very much surfer’s suiting. “Clothes have to help you, no?”

This approach was also apparent in a bodacious pair of heavy cotton canvas trousers, artfully distressed and cut with articulated knees, whose engineered construction looked more thoughtful than luxury utilitarianism tends to. Alongside them was a boxy, leather-collared canvas jacket whose pockets were edged with metal rivets: work-kink.

Reference-wise Tron’s masculine pantheon ranges from the impishness of 1990s Brad Pitt to the gothic wasted glamour of Lou Reed and Andrew Eldritch, the Sisters of Mercy frontman whose band Tron has seen 25 times. Most significant is David Nuuhiwa, the Hawaiian surfer who disrupted and drove forward that pursuit in California during the 1960s and ’70s. What appeals to Tron, he said, is less the beach aesthetic than Nuuhiwa’s impulse to experiment and push forward.

These influences washed through a wardrobe that veered between faded denim and utilitarian outerwear, leather tailoring, laced trousers, leopard-print silks, and richly embroidered evening pieces (many of whose kaleidoscopically immersive fabrics were, like the denim, shared with the womenswear). The double-breasted suiting was especially powerful, its confidently controlled proportions offering a co-ordinated counterpoint to the more flamboyant material treatments.

Tron sees this sensuality as a rejection of bourgeois convention in luxury: the class-generated current his personal temperament crashes against. His instinct is that luxury should enable freedom rather than impose rules. Entwined within spirit and material, the central filament that connects this new Balmain man and the new Balmain woman is Tron himself. “I think slowly, I’m finding that line: I’m finding the woman and the man, basically.”

#6636. Pumpkin Cheesecake Muffins

Loaded with pumpkin seeds, canned pumpkin purée, and pumpkin pie spice, these big streusel-topped muffins are every fall lover’s dream come true. And to make things even better, the inside of each muffin is stuffed with a soft, lightly sweetened cream cheese mixture. Make the cheesecake filling and the streusel ahead of time to pace things out if it’s helpful. Don’t, even for a minute, consider using anything other than Libby’s 100% pure pumpkin purée (not pie mix). Nothing is better. We promise.

This recipe is part of Pumpkin Week 2026, Bon Appétit’s annual celebration of autumn’s most magical pantry staple.

Whisk ⅓ cup coarsely chopped raw pumpkin seeds, ¼ cup (31 g) all-purpose flour, 2 Tbsp. light brown sugar, ½ tsp. pumpkin pie spice, and a pinch of kosher salt in a small bowl to combine. Using your fingers, rub in 2 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, until moist pea-size lumps form. Chill until ready to use.

Do Ahead: Streusel can be made 1 day ahead. Cover and keep chilled.

Vigorously whisk 6 oz. cream cheese, room temperature, ¼ cup (50 g) granulated sugar, 2 tsp. all-purpose flour, 2 tsp. vanilla extract, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a medium bowl until combined and smooth.

Do Ahead: Filling can be made 1 day ahead. Cover and chill.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 375°. Line cups of a standard 12-cup muffin pan with paper liners. Whisk 2 large eggs, 1½ cups (packed; 300 g) light brown sugar, 1 cup canned pumpkin purée, ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil, 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, melted, slightly cooled, ¼ cup whole milk, 1 Tbsp. pumpkin pie spice, 1 Tbsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract, and 1½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1 tsp. Morton kosher salt in a large bowl until smooth, about 1 minute. Whisk 1¾ cups (219 g) all-purpose flour and 1½ tsp. baking powder in a small bowl to combine. Add to pumpkin mixture and fold in with a rubber spatula until just incorporated and no large dry streaks of flour remain (don’t overmix; batter will be lumpy).

Bake muffins until streusel is crisp and muffins spring back when gently pressed, 24–27 minutes. Let muffins cool in pan 5 minutes, then turn out onto a wire rack and let cool until just warm or completely.

Do Ahead: Muffins can be made 2 days ahead. Store airtight at room temperature.

There’s Thanksgiving dinner, and then there are Thanksgiving desserts. These are two separate events, and in between, it’s perfectly fine to take a disco nap. (In fact, we recommend it.)

“This is the magnum opus of Thanksgiving dessert recipes,” writes one reader whose 90-year-old grandfather asked for a repeat performance. “You bet your boots I will—it’s the best apple pie, ever.”

A colossal, self-serve mousse is a baller host move—no tidy slicing, no fussy garnishing, no jockeying for oven space. Set out bowls and spoons and let guests help themselves. Vanilla lovers, try our Colossal Crème Brûlée.

Butter-toasted pecans get the glory, but sour cream does the work, balancing both the gooey pecan layer and the pile of whipped cream. Between them? A thick layer of silky brown sugar custard.

A slice of Thanksgiving pie is nice, but how about a slice of pumpkin flan under a veneer of caramel? This one takes a page from Puerto Rican flan de queso, with cream cheese lending richness, tang, and luxurious texture.

Brown sugar takes some of the fuss out of caramel cake’s famously finicky frosting while browned butter adds toasty depth. Spread it over tender yellow cake layers and finish with flaky salt.

Silky filling, flaky crust, a whisper of spice: This is pumpkin pie done right. Sweetened condensed milk gives the custard its richness; maple syrup and vanilla round out the flavor.

This plush pumpkin spice cake wears a thick swoop of tangy cream cheese frosting. Bake the cake a day ahead, then add the frosting in the lull between dinner and dessert.

If most holiday desserts feel a tad too sweet for you, herbal rosemary and fruity olive oil give this cake just enough savory elements to justify a second helping.

Blanketing quartered apples with rough-puff pastry feels rustic enough, but once baked and inverted, this shimmering, blushing tart dominates the holiday table.

Sweet potato, warming spices, and orange zest get a gloss of dark chocolate in this autumnal loaf cake. Bake it up to three days ahead, leaving the ganache for later.

A deeply browned top and cracked sides are the goal here, not a baking mishap. Underneath: a custardy, pumpkin-spiced cheesecake that needs neither a crust nor a water bath.

Pecan pie meets Hawaiian-style butter mochi in this chewy, coconut-milk-rich cake. Miso-and-maple-coated pecans bring salty-sweet crunch, with some sinking into the batter as the cake bakes.

This date cake blanketed in toffee sauce tastes like cozying up by the fireside after a game of football on the lawn. Bake the unsauced cake a day ahead, then rewarm before finishing.

Cinnamon-spiced pumpkin cake rolled around walnut-studded cream cheese filling needs little embellishment. A dusting of powdered sugar is plenty—each slice supplies its own dramatic swirl.

This glamorous layer cake, with jewel-toned swipes of cranberry jam, white chocolate–cream cheese frosting, and sugared cranberries, looks and tastes like winter is coming (in the best possible way).

A no-bake Oreo crust, a rich chocolate filling, and a tower of whipped cream come together in this shut-the-room-up dessert.

Caramel-glazed apple halves crown this nutty upside-down cake, all baked in the same skillet used to brown the fruit. A scoop of vanilla ice cream on top? We wouldn’t say no.

A floral, earthy saffron soak gives this sponge cake a pudding-like texture and its vibrant yellow color, while pistachios add nutty crunch.

Inspired by the flavors of a pumpkin spice latte, this rendition of old-school tiramisu calls for soaking ladyfingers in bourbon-spiked espresso, then enveloping them in plumes of pumpkin-mascarpone cream.

Cranberries with dinner and again for dessert? Absolutely. Tart filling hides between layers of buttery, crumbly walnut crust in this Linzer tart. Prefer individual portions? Try these cranberry hand pies.

Pumpkin crumb cake is like the brownie of Thanksgiving desserts: easy to tote and slice for sharing. Here, sweet rice flour gives the cake a chewy bounce beneath a buttery, crispy-crumbly pecan streusel.

The ultraflaky crust barely contains the lofty, fudgy almond filling, but have faith: It will puff and dome as it bakes, then settle back into sliceable form once cool.

Alternate layers of pumpkin and chocolate batter create this Bundt cake’s mesmerizing marbling—no knife-swirling necessary. A blanket of chocolate ganache makes it equally appealing to the pumpkin crowd and the chocolate diehards.

Whole roasted birds, spatchcocked showstoppers, braised legs, breasts for smaller gatherings, and more ways to put turkey at the center of the table.

Like brown-butter brussels sprouts with gochujang, green bean salad with Parmesan dressing, our best-ever mashed potatoes, and more reasons to rebrand the holiday “Sidesgiving.”

Squishy rolls for mopping up gravy, flaky biscuits for piling on leftover turkey, and a seedy pumpkin loaf to start the day.

From hot toddies to big-batch punch, these festive drinks will get you in the holiday spirit.

#6634. A Kitchen in Bloom: Inside Miele’s Lush Botanical Oasis Where Art Meets Design

Step inside Art Basel as visionaries Tiffany Howell and Asher Young merge cutting-edge technology with untamed botanical romance, staging an immersive sensory landscape you don’t just see, but feel.

Miele crafts appliances for enduring performance, but they also curate experiences that engage all the senses. Imagine the intuitive precision of a sleek induction cooktop that subtly adjusts temperature on its own; the soft, inviting glow of a French door refrigerator that reveals a curated bounty within ample space; the alluring aroma of a built-in coffee system greeting you each morning; and the calming, almost meditative hum of a steam at work. Each of these choreographed moments elevate Miele appliances beyond a functional tool into something you fundamentally feel. By blending groundbreaking innovation and sustainable features with timeless, minimalist design, Miele frees you to embrace the pure joy of cooking and to savor the intimate, everyday moments that make a house a true home.

For this year’s Art Basel, the ultimate intersection of culture and creativity, Miele brings together two visionaries to redefine the culinary space. Tiffany Howell of Night Palm Design is passionate about creating cinematic homes that tell a deeply personal story. Asher Young is a multidisciplinary artist known for merging art with technology to create immersive spectacle. Together, they have imagined a sensory kitchen built entirely within a lush botanical garden setting. Here, the wildness of the natural world frames the precise German engineering. It is a space where the smooth, handleless silhouettes and cool touch of Miele's Obsidian Black glass stand in striking relief against their organic surroundings, harnessing technology for a design that is both tactile and state-of-the-art. “We used the sleek, matte black Miele appliances as the driving force to bring this to life,” says Howell of the striking visual contrast between nature and cutting-edge machines. “They’re like the perfect little black dress—timeless, elegant, and grounding the entire room.” A deep respect for the environment is also reflected in Miele engineering, which uses innovative technology that preserves ingredients longer—ensuring that the natural world isn't just an aesthetic inspiration, but a core priority for the brand.

Howell’s signature nostalgic romance blends with Asher’s analytical thinking and knack for creating physical, interactive experiences to make the space come alive. Young's touch ensures the installation feels genuinely dynamic, translating the unseen power of Miele’s technology into a captivating physical experience. “How can we create a space that rebalances and also is a place that you can actually engage with? So you can smell the flowers while you're washing your hand. You can feel the cool breeze as you open the fridge,” explains Young. “All these elements is not just for the sake of a visual presentation, but actually to experience and be inside.” The result is an experiential reminder that kitchens should always reflect your personality and passions. “We both said let’s bring our shared language together,” he recalls. The finished design is the undeniable heartbeat of the garden, a space with one foot rooted elegantly in the past and the other stepping boldly into the future.

“A kitchen expresses how you live, what you love and desire, and it’s also where your most cherished memories are made,” says Howell. As the Miami sun sets, the botanical space seamlessly transitions into an elegant, moody enclave for evening entertaining, inviting you to linger just a little bit longer. And in a Miele kitchen, those memories continue into the next day, beginning the moment the fragrant aroma of freshly brewed coffee announces the limitless possibilities of a brand-new morning.

Experience the convergence of culinary art, design, and innovation firsthand. Explore the full Miele collection and begin designing a kitchen that awakens every sense.
