#6633. How a Strip Club DJ Helped Xtube Kill the Porn Studio

For decades, anyone driving down the far-east stretch of one of Toronto’s main arteries would likely have caught a glimpse of a white Cadillac perched atop the entrance of a strip mall beckoning them to stop and step through a set of dark blue doors, behind which lay a world of male fantasy. So iconic was Caddy’s that when the strip club closed in 2017 the Toronto Sun sent out a reporter to mark the moment. In a story titled ‘Last Dance’, the paper somewhat melodramatically bewailed the end of an institution that had provided thousands upon thousands of Torontian men with an ‘escape from the drudgery of [ . . .] lives, wives and troubles’.

Judging by the long streak of one-star ratings on Caddy’s now inactive reviews page, its glory days were long gone by the time the classic car was taken down for good—and it wasn’t the only strip club to have suffered such a fate. By 2017 only twelve clubs were still operating out of the nearly fifty joints that had been dotted around Toronto at the turn of the century. Strip clubs have always been controversial, criticized for monetizing the objectification of women in a way many find too crude. Yet the stripper—at once desired and despised, exploited yet feared for her power to corrupt the good man—has not vanished. She permeates popular culture, from blockbusters and music videos to fitness crazes. Why, then, have strip clubs been shutting down? The reason was clear to Canadian stripper and activist Valerie Scott. “It’s called the internet,” she said in the report on Caddy’s closure; now, anyone seeking to escape the drudgery of reality had only to turn to their phone.

As fate would have it, Caddy’s made a cameo appearance in the story of how adult entertainment moved online. A little over a decade before the club played its last song it hired a man named Kurtis Potec, a former welder who’d just traded his tools for the DJ booth. “It wasn’t great; it wasn’t horrible,” Kurtis told me over a video call years later. “It was something to pay the bills.” Young and restless, he was a jack of all trades trying to find his way in life, but playing the tunes to which strippers danced and derobed turned out not to be Kurtis’s ultimate calling either. When he spotted a newspaper ad for a desk job at an online start-up not far from Caddy’s he figured he’d try his luck in the emerging virtual world.

“That was probably one of the most unique interviews of my life,” Kurtis told me with a chuckle. After a cursory skim of the twenty-something’s CV, the interviewer began to spout some “pretty vulgar, sexual-related terms” to check whether Kurtis was prepared for the world of online pornography. On the internet, there were no gatekeepers to control what people said, “so I guess they wanted to prepare me for the worst.”

He got the job. Kurtis’s new employer was Xtube, the first tube site targeted at gay men. The start-up’s first office was housed in a warehouse on the outskirts of the city, piled up to the ceiling and wall to wall with boxes, leaving only a two-foot-wide passage for Kurtis and his handful of co-workers to reach their desks. Inside the perilously stacked towers were countless pornographic DVDs awaiting digitization and to be uploaded online, along with merchandise like mouse pads and socks emblazoned with the company logo. Other than the three founders and the main financier—a Chinese man whom the others referred to as Mr X—the only other employee was a video encoder who at times appeared to live in the back room, where he worked and chain-smoked in solitude.

Kurtis, by contrast, was drawn into a far more public role. When one of the co-founders left, spooked by the threats that started rolling in from porn producers angry at finding their films on Xtube, Kurtis essentially picked up the day-to-day running of the site. Soon, he became the face of the company. Literally. “If you had any issues on the site, there was my ugly mug plastered in the corner,” he said. His associates didn’t use their real names, let alone their faces, and they had Kurtis—still technically a junior employee—front the company. “They were older and smarter than I was,” he told me, laughing.

By 2008, a year after Pornotube halted user uploads over fears of illegal content, business at Xtube was booming. Each month, it was pulling in hundreds of thousands of dollars from advertising. Advertisers were enthralled by this new medium, which allowed them to target their messages with unprecedented precision. Traditional media, including TV and magazines, had long promised access to certain demographics, but the internet offered the ability to drill deeper and glean details about people’s lives without asking a single question. Those being tracked hardly resisted. Many did not grasp the scale of the surveillance, while others were simply distracted by all the free stuff, like porn. Xtube’s users, Kurtis said, “really liked it.” But most of his days were spent fielding calls from within the industry, and their reaction was less enthusiastic. “People hated us.”

Around this time Kurtis started feeling depressed and began using drugs to cope with a job that, more and more, involved people shouting at him. “I got very used to hearing the words: I’m going to sue you.” But the threats were toothless. Free-speech laws governing the internet meant that user-generated platforms were, in essence, not legally responsible for what users uploaded.

All the studios could do was stand by and watch as their films were repeatedly stolen and re-uploaded by fans and, insiders complained, by tube sites themselves. It didn’t take long before their powerlessness began to attract investors’ attention. User-generated platforms seemed legally untouchable. And while there were plenty of platforms selling ad space, few received as many visits as the free porn sites. Kurtis was told to fend off callers claiming they wanted to buy Xtube. But one day in 2008 a man on the line refused to take no for an answer. He kept repeating: “Just give me a number.”

Kurtis walked into the back room, where one of the owners was sitting, and asked if there was any amount of money that would make it worth parting with Xtube. The slightly facetious reply was 35 million Canadian dollars. ‘I walked back, picked up the phone and said: “You’re not gonna like this,”’ Kurtis recalled. The man on the phone didn’t hesitate; he’d be there early next week to sort out the details.

In the months that followed a German in his early thirties would regularly occupy the carpet by Kurtis’s desk. Fabian Thylmann would stretch out on it to ease a back battered by his now frequent flights to Toronto. Fabian was a self-proclaimed nerd who as a teenager in the late nineties had developed one of the earliest pieces of software capable of tracking what internet users clicked on. Crucially, it allowed website owners to earn money when a link on their page redirected a visitor to make a purchase elsewhere. Hawking online advertising space had been a fairly unsurprising evolution of traditional marketing, but here was something that made it possible to monitor what people did next, and profit from it. The internet was turning into a game of digital chess, with money to be made by those who could anticipate, and manipulate, the whims of anyone who entered their domain.

Fabian’s first company had given him a rare glimpse into the back end of some early online businesses. Many were trying to sell porn, which was one of the first things people were willing to pay for on the internet. But these were small, makeshift operations, and few were making real money. In the early noughties, most of the people buying smutty DVDs or renting pay-per-views alone in hotel rooms hardly knew how to use the internet. Sure, they would eventually find their way to adult sites. But Fabian realized that a truly successful enterprise could do far more than tap into existing demand—it could bring pornography to people who had not even looked for it, through pop-ups, ads and search results, turning it from something you searched for into something that found you. Once porn became part of the internet’s background noise, those who’d never have dared to cross the threshold of a sex shop or an erotic cinema, or breach any of the other hurdles the curious had once faced, could now stumble upon porn with a single click.

The purpose of tube sites, like that of virtually every internet platform since, was not to replicate what existed in the physical world but to construct a new, disembodied mode of interaction built around visual stimuli—what we now call “content.” Their rise marked the start of a new era, when every step online began to be tracked. It was a turning point in the story of how data, harvested without people’s informed consent, became one of the world’s most valuable commodities, eventually proliferating to a point where it could be used to teach artificial intelligence to think and act like us. The tale of tube sites, then, is also the story of how the digital world came to be organized around priorities that do not necessarily align with those of the people who inhabit it.

The practice Fabian helped pioneer as a teenager is today known as affiliate marketing—put simply, the business of getting people to click on things. It’s how content creators make money when they offer their followers discounts on whatever must-have food supplement, skin cream or therapy app they claim to love. And the business of funneling people’s attention was first stress-tested in the porn industry. Long before influencers started peddling protein powder, porn sites were paying webmasters to drive clicks to their content, offering a cut for every paying visitor who came to their site through a tracked banner ad or link. It was the logic of the early thumbnail galleries, like the one built by the pale programmer who had filched sandwiches from Mario Salieri.

One of the key reasons why Amazon grew to become the place for online shopping was its early adoption of an affiliate programme which paid website owners a commission to direct people to products sold on the platform. Once people got accustomed to buying all their stuff on Amazon, the companies that actually made the stuff—anything from phone chargers to vacuum cleaners to cat litter—realized that if their products weren’t on the site, no one would buy them. Needless to say, affiliate marketing is still one of the main legs of Amazon’s business model, and its foundations were laid by people like Fabian, who understood that to make real money online you need to attract plenty of people and make sure they keep coming back. This is called “the network effect,” and it is one of the basic laws of the digital economy—the more people use your service, the more powerful you become.

As sites such as Xtube were attracting a critical mass of visitors, they could essentially dictate terms to studios that were now, in desperation, drawn into partnerships with the platforms. The tube site’s offer was to feature ten or fifteen minutes of footage and call it “advertising.” The only problem was that the average visit to a porn site ends—rather abruptly—after about nine minutes.

Still, as free content washed over the Web, studios started uploading their own clips to tube sites, hoping a few hundred or thousand viewers might click the affiliate link and pay for the full film. If they did, the revenue would be split 50:50 with the tube site. “We put a lot of studios out of business,” Kurtis said, “they couldn’t afford to get traffic from us.” Companies like Xtube quickly learned to steer what users clicked on next, and once they controlled enough traffic to funnel millions of potential customers towards paying sites or advertisers, they became powerful.

This dynamic has since spread beyond porn. Many news outlets now depend on posting their stories to Facebook, TikTok or X, hoping to lure readers back to their own sites, where some might eventually pay for their journalism. Few do. Like porn, news has become something most of us expect to get for free. The number of independent newspapers has collapsed, many swallowed by conglomerates that rely on scale to profit from advertising and, increasingly, from affiliate links.

Fabian’s ambitions were clear from the beginning. Xtube was offering more than a quick buck. It was a stepping stone in the making of an empire. He wanted to “basically take over the adult industry,” Kurtis said. As the traditional studios buckled under the tube-site business model, many went up for sale, which meant that their huge libraries of content could be bought on the cheap. For a site like Xtube, buying studios and their subscription platforms made perfect sense. It gained access to more content, and could funnel its huge traffic towards paid sites it now controlled. If people paid for porn, the company profited; if they preferred the free stuff, it profited too.

It was a business model that thrived on relentless expansion, and Fabian had his sights set on a much bigger rival. In nearby Montreal, another group of young men were running a site that would become to pornography what McDonald’s is to the burger: Pornhub. To simplify, it was founded in 2007 by three Concordia University students—Matt Keezer, Steph Manos and Sam Youssef—whose company, Mansef, was already running several porn businesses, including the still influential production studio Brazzers (a slang take on “brothers”). Publicly, Youssef posed as a concerned studio owner and railed against tube sites on the industry forum GoFuckYourself.com: “They will not steal it and get away with it. Their days are counted!” What few in the industry realized was that Pornhub was run by the same group.

In a 2023 interview about his most recent venture Youssef said that he and his co-founders made the decision to sell Pornhub to Fabian in 2010 for roughly $140 million because he “didn’t feel comfortable at all with what the company had become.” He sounded almost surprised to have found himself in the sex industry, despite having made his money from sites such as JuggFuckers and RacksAndBlacks.

When I tried to contact them all in 2020, Youssef declined to talk to me about those early years, saying he was never really “much involved” with the business he had founded. Keezer likewise responded: “To be honest with you, I never had any involvement in the ‘industry.’ I just made Pornhub in 2007 with a couple of friends and sold it [ . . .] and that was about it.” I had no response from Manos. This detachment was typical of the internet entrepreneurs who profited from unleashing pornography. Many preferred to remain anonymous, even as the industry’s most important workers literally have to bare all. In a move that reflected the trolling culture of the nerds who were taking over porn, Fabian—the new king of smut—renamed his empire Manwin.

#6632. Balmain Resort 2027

“For me, the men’s and the women’s, they are kind of like one entity. I really want the men and women to be able to talk to each other.” Antonin Tron was riffing as we riffled the rails of his first pre-spring collections for Balmain, including his first-ever menswear collection for the house. “I don’t necessarily think of them as a couple,” he qualified: “but I think at Balmain the women are quite sexy and out there. And if I was to do something very different for men, it would be weird.”

Looking at his rails simultaneously, that relationship was manifest in substance as well as spirit: “We have a lot of overcrossing material,” Tron confirmed. The same embroidered surfaces, treated denim, and snakeskin jacquards traveled between the two wardrobes, but in womenswear their expression was differently calibrated. This was a more relaxed continuation of the luxuriously louche and wanton glamour of his last runway collection, which itself had been informed by the Californian desert. “With pre-collection I can explore a bit more the casual side of Balmain,” he said.

That meant injecting a dose of dishevelment into Balmain’s appetite for decoration. Sensuously draped dresses in snakeskin and beautiful, borderline-hallucinogenic florals came with cropped flight jackets, while satin tailoring, sheer lurex blouses, and embellished animalier were countered by washed denim and biker leathers. There were micro cardigans over exposed bras, skirts that succumbed into flowerings of asymmetric ruffle, and lavishly embellished pieces whose surfaces seemed weathered by weeks of sun exposure. What blurred in the haze of Tron’s gaze was much distinction between dressing up and down.

This dissolved tension was more than a styling proposition. Tron has little patience for the idea that luxury dressing must conform either to bourgeois standards of propriety, or fashion’s customary cringe of faux-intellectual snobbishness. “For me, this thing with sexiness is also a class thing,” he said of the physical ideals through which notions of elegance are policed. Balmain, in his view, offers a space to articulate sexuality rather than apologize for it.

He recalled meeting a client in Los Angeles while shooting the collection in Joshua Tree. When he mentioned his last show’s desert-and-hallucinogenics theme, she cheerfully volunteered that she had a house there and used to do exactly that. She was accompanied by her muscular husband. “They both wanted to be hot, and that’s it,” Tron observed. This seemed a pretty good expression of the collection’s carefully direct hedonism. Super-long leather boots and Lucite-heeled wedges provided the strut, while the tasseled Zabriskie bag offered easygoing swing.

His instinct is that luxury should enable freedom rather than impose rules. The filament that connects this new Balmain woman and the new Balmain man, in spirit as much as substance, is Tron himself. “I think slowly, I’m finding that line: I’m finding the woman and the man, basically.”

#6630. Farmers Market Salad With Miso Maple Dressing

Most recipes serve multiple people. In Me, Myself, and Dinner, each dish serves one and only one. That's you! Because whether you live solo (almost 30% of Americans do) or are enjoying a night to yourself, sitting down to a meal should be something to look forward to.

The farmers market is my biggest hack for solo cooking. Instead of committing to a three-pound bag of carrots, you can buy just one, and choose your produce based on beauty or mere curiosity. That’s how I came up with this fall salad, comprising a single head of Little Gem lettuce and some crunchy produce, including that carrot we mentioned, cucumber, watermelon radish, and an Asian pear. Slicing those elements thinly with a mandoline and throwing them in an ice bath for a brief soak helps keep everything cold and crisp.

The dressing is equally important. I start with Kewpie mayo for its richness and savory depth—it’s made with only egg yolks and contains MSG, giving it a fuller flavor than standard mayonnaise. White miso brings even more umami, while rice vinegar keeps things bright. The result is a creamy finger-licking dressing that clings to every bite. Serve with a fried egg (or your protein of choice) and a piece of fresh bread to scoop up the leftovers. Make a double batch of dressing for dipping crudités or marinating salmon, chicken, or roasted vegetables.

Read More: The Farmers Market Is Built for Solo Cooking

Combine 1 small head of Little Gem or other crunchy lettuce, leaves separated, cut into 2"–3" pieces, 1 rainbow carrot, peeled, very thinly sliced lengthwise into ribbons with a mandoline or vegetable peeler, 1 Persian cucumber, thinly sliced, preferably with a mandoline, ½ small watermelon radish, peeled, halved, thinly sliced into half-moons, preferably with a mandoline, and ¼ medium Asian pear, thinly sliced into half-moons, preferably with a mandoline, in a large bowl of ice water. Let soak 10–15 minutes.

Meanwhile, whisk ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil, 2 Tbsp. seasoned rice vinegar, 2 Tbsp. white miso, 2 tsp. Kewpie mayonnaise, 2 tsp. pure maple syrup, and 1 tsp. toasted sesame oil in a small bowl until smooth and emulsified (or combine in a small glass jar, cover, and shake). Taste dressing and season with kosher salt.

Drain vegetables and run through a salad spinner or pat dry with a kitchen towel to remove excess water. Wipe out large bowl, return vegetables to bowl, and drizzle 2 Tbsp. dressing over. Toss, adding more dressing as needed, until evenly coated (reserve any remaining dressing for another use).

Transfer salad to a shallow bowl or rimmed plate. Top with a fried egg and serve with country-style bread if desired.

Do Ahead: Dressing can be made up to 3 days ahead. Cover and chill.

#6629. If You Didn’t Already Think CrossFit Was a Cult, This Book Will Convince You

CrossFit could easily hold the distinction of being the most popular—and certainly the most universally recognized—fitness movement ever. At its peak, the company, founded in 2000, boasted over 15,000 affiliate gyms, or “boxes,” in more than 150 countries and was enlisting nearly 50,000 coaches each year. Somewhere along the line, to many people (including my mom), the word CrossFit became synonymous for working out.

But you may have noticed that people don’t talk as much about CrossFit nowadays as they used to. Following an assorted variety of allegations against its mercurial founder Greg Glassman in 2020, and the tragic and seemingly avoidable death of an athlete at the 2024 CrossFit Games, the company has been gradually losing its chalky grip on popular culture.

But the storytelling around CrossFit’s rise and fall is anything but done and dusted, as I learned while reading Calum Marsh’s new book, The Glass Box—a fascinating, staggeringly detailed account of not only CrossFit’s rocket-propelled rise and spectacular flame-out, as told by insider sources including Glassman himself, but a trove of previously unshared anecdotes that make it hard to put down.

A few days after the book’s late-October release, I spoke to Marsh about airing CrossFit’s dirtiest laundry, how the cultishness of CrossFit has spread to other fitness crazes, and why an old-fashioned barbell snatch is the ultimate kryptonite for the modern performative male.

Calum Marsh: The first 50 people I spoke to were basically warning me off the subject, telling me that it was going to be an uphill battle and that if the people that I was writing about were unhappy with how I wrote about them, they would criticize me or attempt to defame me. They have a long history, which I've chronicled in the book, of creating campaigns of bad will towards their critics. They are really experienced at mobilizing the internet and mobilizing social media in order to discredit people that they consider critics or, as I sometimes frame it in the book, heretics.

But, on the other hand, I knew that I was going to be approaching this from a journalistically rigorous perspective, and that it was going to be an account of what actually happened, from the people who were involved. It wasn't going to be a book about my opinion or my personal take on CrossFit. So I felt quite secure that the things I was writing in the book were the best possible representation of the truth.

There's been a lot of focus on some of the more, shall we say, sordid details. There was an article in the New York Post that basically pulled all that out and made the book seem like it was some scandalous hit piece. That's not the book I tried to write, and it's not the book I think I wrote. The book I wrote is a very fair, even-handed assessment of a man and the business that he created. It includes true stories about his behavior that may not be the most flattering, and it includes, I think, testament to the brilliance of what he created and all of the great things that he did.

It's been amusing to see the reception somewhat split among some hardcore CrossFitters. They're upset because they think it's a takedown of Greg Glassman that's biased against him. And then other critics think that I'm way too generous to him, or way too flattering. Someone said I was too affectionate towards him. It's funny to me that two different people can read it and have polar opposite views. But hopefully that means that it's just objective.

It’s something that is always mentioned in relation to CrossFit. There’s actually another book that came out while I was writing my book, called The Cult of CrossFit by Katie Rose Hejtmanek. It's an academic study of the history and sociology of cults, and how CrossFit is a cult by definition. I thought that was really illuminating. There are a lot of similarities that go beyond all the joking, and there are a lot of things about the cult-like nature of CrossFit that have made it intoxicating for some people, and maybe painful in other ways as well.

The thing that struck me was how a lot of people, especially earlier CrossFit enthusiasts or devotees, talked to me about the difficulty they had leaving CrossFit. Greg has said many times, and will still say, that they weren't a cult because you could leave at any time. And I always found that to be a fairly unconvincing argument, because many cults say the same thing. A lot of CrossFitters had this experience where they felt like they had really close friends—sometimes even family members or partners—who essentially excommunicated them—cut them off, iced them out, however you want to phrase it—after they either left CrossFit or spoke out against CrossFit. That, to me, is the strongest cult-like parallel.

It really wasn't. I had heard from people that Glassman was generous, and so I knew stories about him spending lavishly on dinners, for example. But it all seemed pretty much within the realm of normal spending for someone who's massively wealthy. It wasn't until I started speaking to people who were more familiar with CrossFit’s finances when I started to learn about the degree to which the spending was out of control. The amount of money they spent relative to what they earned is the most shocking thing to me, because it's not like they were spending that much money while wildly out-earning it. They were almost spending more than they were making. And they were not slowing their spending when the earning slowed. That, to me, as a business reporter, is very, very interesting, because you start to wonder how they let that happen. Was no one there to say, ‘Hey, we need to pump the brakes a little bit?’ As it turned out, there really wasn't.

A lot of people had this reckoning in 2020 after [Glassman’s George Floyd tweet] and some of the accusations that came out about Glassman then. A lot of gyms deaffiliated from CrossFit, and Greg, of course, stepped down and sold the company. I personally know many people who had a kind of personal reckoning with their level of comfort in supporting the company. But I think that was, at least in some cases, answered by Greg's departure.

Others went through this process of divorcing themselves from the CrossFit identity as a brand, but continuing to practice CrossFit as a methodology. There are thousands of gyms all over the world that are CrossFit gyms in all but name. They probably call themselves cross-training or functional fitness now, but if you go in, they still have a Rogue rig, they still have wall balls, they still do snatches—everything you'd expect to see if you walked into a CrossFit gym. The people there are still CrossFitters at heart. They've still given themselves over to the transformative power of the methodology that Greg Glassman invented. But they are no longer endorsing or supporting the brand or the company at large.

I would be willing to bet that if you walked into any CrossFit gym in North America and asked them what the difference is between when Glassman was in charge and when corporate private equity took charge, they wouldn’t know that even happened. The average person, who just goes to CrossFit to get fit and doesn't follow the CrossFit news and isn't obsessed with CrossFit Games, would not have any idea. And this is partly a consequence of the fact that the affiliate model is so loose.

I first started going to a box in 2018. I was doing personal training with someone at what I didn't realize was even a CrossFit box. I was doing that personal training for probably about a year before I realized I was doing CrossFit, and then I started doing classes in 2019.

I do! In fact, I coached a CrossFit class earlier this morning.

Yeah. Well, they don't allow you to call it a certification with the one I have. They're very clear about that language. But I do have my CrossFit Level 1 certificate. I did the course and passed the test.

I think that part of it is just the nature of fandom. If you become enthusiastic about something, you want to identify as an enthusiast, and that means wearing the T-shirt. I went to a Bob Dylan concert on the weekend. I bought a Bob Dylan hat. It's just part of the experience of being a fan of something. I think Hyrox is super interesting, because it does have a lot of cult-like similarities with CrossFit. But the really toxic side—the side that's a bit more nefarious, that involves really giving your life over to it—I haven't seen with Hyrox, at least yet. I feel like there's something missing at the center, possibly the guru figure that Greg Glassman was for CrossFit. Hyrox doesn't really have that. So maybe that missing ingredient has stopped it from being as intense. But, on the whole, I think it's just this level of fandom that is baked into everything in culture now.

There's a journalist named Hannah Singleton, who writes about fitness a lot. She recently wrote a piece about how athleticism is the new flex for people, and I think that’s sort of the direction we're moving in. For example, Hyrox. It's in the same vein as running a marathon or doing an ultra race, where it's an achievement that you can brandish. You can put it on your LinkedIn. You can say that you've done this and that. Maybe it's on your resume. You're almost putting up points on your scoreboard. And I think that we're probably going to find either more events along those lines, where it's something that you can be proud of because it's so difficult, or a system in which you can have an overall fitness score. I think people really want to show that they put in the work. They want to impress people with how fit they are. In the same way that people who are into running are obsessively posting about their marathon time or their 10K time, and those milestones are universally understood in running—everyone knows how impressive it is to run a sub-10-minute 5K or a sub-three-hour marathon—the fitness equivalent of that, I think, is still coming.

This is one of the things I find interesting in light of CrossFit—and I know that some people are going to think this is not true—but I really do think CrossFit, or let’s say functional fitness, is an antidote to this whole performative thing. It's so hard that it's humbling. You can’t really show up and pretend you did it. You cannot performatively do barbell snatches. If you see someone on social media snatching heavy, it's because they've worked at it for hundreds, if not thousands, of hours, and because they're really strong, really mobile, and really fit. And I say this as someone who both has a copy of Infinite Jest on my bookshelf and wears Satisfy.

You can ‘perform’ your sport of choice by posting about it, but things like snatches or ring muscle ups are so physically demanding that it kind of cuts through that bullshit. And I think that we're probably going to increasingly value things that we know on-site are authentic, because there's an authenticity built into it. There's no shortcut there. And that authenticity will become more and more valuable.

How to Catch Up on Sleep the Right Way, According to Experts

#6628. The World's 50 Best Restaurants, According to ‘The 50’ Best List

Asador Etxebarri from the Spanish Basque Country won the title of world's best restaurant Wednesday night when The 50, the organization formerly known as 50 Best, announced its annual ranking during a ceremony in Lima, Peru. It was the first such event for The 50 in South America.

Situated between Bilbao and San Sebastián, Etxebarri claims some of the world's most famous chefs as major fans of Bittor Arginzoniz, who matches the characteristics of the wood in the grill with the plants and proteins sizzling over the flames. It was No. 2 on The 50 in 2025 and maintains one Michelin star. Tasting menus are around $337.

Wednesday was also a big night for Atomix in New York, which came in at No. 11 and was named Best Restaurant in North America. Junghyun Park took home the Chef's Choice Award.

A spot on The 50 is among the highest honors a restaurant can receive. (Disclosure: Bon Appétit editor in chief Jamila Robinson is a 50 Best Academy Chair.) The list favors some of the most expensive, elaborate cooking in the fine-dining world. It often rewards tasting menus that employ molecular gastronomy, but also celebrates the simple elegance of a Spanish grill, the sourcing mission of an Argentine steakhouse, or the laidback cool of Parisian neo-bistro.

This year’s ceremony ran into controversy earlier in the year when it was announced that it would be hosted in Abu Dhabi, which has faced criticism for human rights violations. In June, it was announced that the ceremony would instead be hosted in Peru, home to Maido, the Lima restaurant that won No. 1 last year. Maido is not eligible for this year’s list. After winning the top spot, it moves to the organization’s Best of the Best list alongside other past number ones like Barcelona’s Disfrutar and New York’s Eleven Madison Park.

Quintonil landed at No. 4. The two-Michelin starred restaurant in Mexico City led by Jorge Vallejo and Alejandra Flores, serves dishes that revolve around local, pre-Hispanic ingredients, which come together in energized and unexpected ways throughout its multi-course prix-fixe menu. A November menu listed on its website was priced around $327 per person for a meal that included lobster in a pipián verde with makrut lime, and an heirloom white corn tamal made with ayocote beans and young corn cream.

At Alchemist in Copenhagen (No. 3), chef Rasmus Munk oversees a meal that skews sci-fi with dishes that mimic brains and eyeballs and a show that advertises “up to 50 impressions,” both edible and “purely experiential.” It holds two Michelin stars and was ranked fifth on The 50 last year.

Gaggan in Bangkok (No. 2) claimed the title of Asia's Best Restaurant behind the creative, genre-bending Indian cooking of Gaggan Anand.

Critics of the World’s 50 Best have raised questions around its voting practices, which do not demand that voters pay for their own meals. The organization, for its part, explains that the list is voted on by more than 1,100 voters across 28 regions, a quarter of whom are new each year. The voting body is made up of equal parts chefs and restaurateurs, food writers, and “well-travelled gourmets,” as the The 50 website puts it. Aside from the Academy Chairs, voters must remain anonymous.

There is no set criteria for what makes a restaurant the best, and “every style of restaurant in the world is eligible,” though the list skews completely towards high-end dining even as other best-of links have begun to include street food venues, for instance.

Ana Ros, the chef at three Michelin-starred Hisa Franko in Slovenia, published a statement on Instagram last month explaining why she would not attend the ceremony in Lima. “Success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system,” she wrote. “…When visibility becomes the currency, the ability to promote a restaurant in this system risks becoming as important as the work happening inside it.”

Ros, who won the award for best female chef from The 50 in 2017, told Bon Appétit that restaurants and tourism boards “more or less” know who the anonymous voters are, and they often have to make decisions about whether to pay for travel, meals, and other entertainment to try and boost their standing.

“With more rules and a little bit more control, this list can really shine again,” she said in an interview.

Hisa Franko was No. 60 on the ranking of 51-100 this year. It was 69th last year and No. 48 in 2024.

Ros said seeing her restaurant at any place on the list was “a fantastic result” and she would be wishing everyone all the best at the ceremony.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like peanut butter and jelly or macaroni and cheese, cozy knits and skiwear just go together. So it only made sense that the female-founded and cool girl-approved ski brand Halfdays would team up with one of the coolest girls of our generation: Gigi Hadid—and more specifically, Hadid’s luxury knitwear brand, Guest in Residence.

#6626. Spiced Beef Baked Potatoes

Baked potatoes may not seem like a dish that requires much technique, but there’s a difference between a good baked potato and a great one. Wrapping them in foil can lead to a tender interior but render the skin too soft. For the most crisp skin, bake them right on the oven grate at quite a high temperature. (To make it taste almost like a potato chip, rub those scrubbed russets with vegetable oil and season with plenty of salt.) Shockingly, this doesn’t prevent a fluffy interior—that comes down to the internal temperature reaching at least 212°. Sure, it may seem strange to take your potato’s temperature, but it’s a fail-safe route to the most enjoyable potato.

Some, including the Idaho Potato Commission, swear that the way a baked potato is opened affects the texture, eschewing a knife for a fork to gently poke a plus sign into the surface. Others, like beloved food writer Nigel Slater, prefer to strike the potato as though it were part of a martial arts competition. “Bring your hand down fast and sharp as if you were doing a karate chop but only hard enough to crack open the potato skin,” he writes in a piece for The Guardian. (Whether or not to attempt this feat is literally in the hands of the potato-baker.) We found a single fork-pricked line down the length of the spud is the most effective.

Once opened, a baked potato can be filled with anything from tuna salad to cheesy baked beans. Here, it’s a picadillo-inspired mixture with ground beef (packed with protein, iron, and creatine—though ground turkey or chicken work nicely too) adorned with golden raisins and stuffed olives. A spicy tomato sauce will slowly soak into the potato, further seasoning it with every passing minute. Make sure to use a chili powder that’s a mix of spices like chiles, cumin, oregano, garlic (not just dried ground chiles).

Healthy cooking doesn’t have to be complicated. In fact, often the simpler it is, the better it tastes. In Good for You, Test Kitchen editor Rebecca Firkser shares feel-good recipes for any day of the week. Read more: White Potatoes Are Not Empty Calories. They’re Actually Great for You.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 425°. Prick 4 large russet potatoes (about 10 oz. each), scrubbed, patted dry, all over with a fork, then rub all over with 1 Tbsp. vegetable oil and sprinkle with 1¼ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt. Bake directly on oven rack until skins are crisp and flesh is very soft (an instant-read thermometer inserted into the center should register 212°–220°), 55–75 minutes. (If thermometer registers 210° before beef mixture is finished, turn off oven and leave potatoes inside until ready to use.)

Meanwhile, pick enough leaves from ½ bunch cilantro (about 2 oz.) to measure ½ cup; set aside for serving. Finely chop remaining leaves and all of stems (you should have about ¾ cup); set aside separately.

Heat remaining 2 Tbsp. vegetable oil in a large skillet over medium. Add 1 medium red or yellow onion, finely chopped, 5 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt and cook, stirring occasionally, until onion is softened, 8–10 minutes. Add ¼ cup double-concentrated tomato paste and cook, stirring constantly, until deepened in color and slightly tacky, about 2 minutes.

Increase heat to medium-high. Add 1 lb. ground beef, 2 Tbsp. chili powder, and 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt. Cook, stirring and smashing often with a heatproof rubber spatula to break up meat, until cooked through, about 4 minutes.

Reduce heat to low. Add ½ cup golden raisins, ¼ cup sliced pimiento-stuffed green olives, reserved chopped cilantro, and 1 cup water. Cook, stirring occasionally, until sauce thickens slightly and flavors come together, 8–12 minutes. Taste beef mixture and season with more salt if needed. Remove from heat and cover. Let sit until ready to use.

Transfer potatoes to a platter or plates and let cool 5 minutes. Using a fork, prick a 3"–4"-long line in the center down length of each potato, leaving 1" unpricked on each end. Using your hands, firmly press ends down toward platter to split potatoes open. Smash flesh as desired, then season inside of potatoes with salt. Divide beef mixture among potatoes and top with reserved cilantro leaves.

#6625. Bill Hader Has Highbrow Taste in Children’s Movies

You can trust Bill Hader on movies. From gushing about John Ford and Akira Kurosawa in the Criterion Closet, to spoofing Errol Morris and D.A. Pennebaker in Documentary Now!, to creating, directing and starring in four seasons of HBO’s Barry—the SNL alum’s cinematic expertise is well-documented. Hader knows a thing or two about kids’ movies in particular, having lent his iconic voice to animated tentpoles like Inside Out and Cloudy With a Chance of Meatballs. This week, you can hear Hader in The Cat in The Hat, a new adaptation of the 1957 Dr. Seuss classic, in which he voices the titular troublemaking feline.

Written and directed by Alessandro Carloni and Erica Rivinoja, this new iteration of the beloved children’s story is an inviting romp that is mischievous enough for kids, grounded enough for grown-ups, and teeming with gags and good nature that all can enjoy. The film is an ideal showcase for Hader’s gymnastic vocal talents, and also boasts Quinta Brunson, Matt Berry, and Giancarlo Esposito among its cast. (It’s a more family-friendly—though no less amusing—take on the Cat than when Hader last played the character, in a 2014 SNL sketch.)

A father to three daughters, Hader has seen it all—from Frozen and Tangled to Off Campus—and does his part, when possible, to pass down the classic films that were formative during his own childhood. “You don’t want to push it too hard,” he tells GQ on a recent call. “For every one I show them, there’s 10 that I start and they go, ‘Ugh, Dad, this is boring.’” The rest of us, however, are eager to take note. Below, Hader shares some of his favorite quote-unquote “kids’ movies” whose cinematic merit makes them a must-see at any age.

“When I was young, my dad told me that was a movie that he watched as a kid. I didn't grow up in a time when there were a lot of Westerns, but [for] my dad and uncles, that was all that was on TV.

What I was moved by was the relationship [between] Alan Ladd as Shane and this little boy [Brandon deWilde] enamored with a gunfighter. It has this heroic sensibility to it, but underneath was this honesty of, ‘You don't want that life,’ which I thought was interesting. To me, Van Heflin, the dad, was the cooler guy. So it upended those expectations of what the movie was going to be.

It has this ending where it's poignant and kind of sad, but I remember thinking, ‘Oh, that's good that that's what happens at the end of the story.’ It's a good coming-of-age story… It's kind of sneaky. You could see it on the face of it just being this cut-and-dry, Western-hero type of movie. But when you really think about it, even as a kid, I got a sense that there's a lot of depth to it.”

“Buster Keaton is one of my favorites, and that was one I showed my kids. I have three daughters. Now they're 17, 14, and 12, but at the time, they were very little. I'm a movie nerd, so I was gonna show them some silent movies, and I was like, ‘I know. Everybody's gonna roll their eyes at me for showing the kids silent movies. How pretentious.’ But they really responded to Buster Keaton, specifically Sherlock Jr. and Three Ages, his take on the movie Intolerance by D.W. Griffith. They just loved it, and it was fun getting to watch it through their eyes.

I recently took my youngest [to see] it at the Aero Theater in LA, and it still was great. To see a movie that was made over a hundred years ago still work on all [levels], like… it works better than most movies now. It's just very simple. [Keaton] wasn't some kind of auteur guy. He was a vaudeville comedian that was curious about filmmaking; what you could do with editing, what you could do with shots. And I think he ended up making the blueprint of how you shoot physical comedy and action. It’s pretty amazing.

What's interesting about some of those silent movies is that they don't really know what they're doing. The best stuff comes out of, ‘I don't know, I had this idea, let's figure it out in a really open, curious way.’ And in doing that, you invent the language.

Charlie Chaplin, too. [I’ve shown] them City Lights and The Circus and The Gold Rush. But they were really drawn to Buster Keaton, I think, because he was kind of dry and you didn't really know what he was thinking, and so you could put all your feelings onto him.”

“That's one that my parents showed me when I was a kid. It's a long movie, but it has so many crazy [moments] and a lot of physical comedy. I just love all those comedians in the movie. Every time someone pulls up, you're like, ‘Oh my God, it's Jonathan Winters. I love Phil Silvers. Oh my God, Spencer Tracy's in this movie!’ Dick Shawn is my personal favorite in that movie, the guy who's the real mama's boy.

It's kind of the one Stanley Kramer movie I like. Stanley Kramer movies tended to be, I don't know, a little sentimental, or overtly didactic or something, [but] this movie is just having a ton of fun. I have very fond memories of watching it with my parents and my grandparents. My dad was in town recently, and when I came home, he was watching it with my kids, and they were all laughing really hard and telling me scenes from it.

They really loved it, but [just like] with the silent movies, you don't want to push it too hard. For every [classic movie] I show them, there's 10 that I start and they go, ‘Ugh, Dad, this is boring,’ and I go, ‘Alright, we can watch Nobody Wants This or Off Campus.’”

“The Secret of NIMH was one that I loved as a kid, because it was ostensibly a kid's movie, but it was incredibly dark and disturbing. It has stuff that I still find really frightening. And I loved the mythology of it. As a kid, I liked the idea that in your backyard [there] could be this whole kingdom that you don't even know about.

There's specifically a shot in it with the crow, played by Dom DeLuise, and in the distance they see this cat. There's this long shot [of] this field, and then suddenly, just really quickly, you see this cat pop up and go back down under [as] it's trying to catch a bird or something. That shot had a huge effect on me. I thought, ‘Oh man, that's so terrifying.’

And then the story of the rats and NIMH, and they're all being tested on, and they fall into this abyss, and the whole ending where they're having this sword fight… I just thought it was fantastic. Everything was high stakes. And it wasn't Disney. I liked Disney, but Disney never went that dark.

Maybe not a lot of people agree with me, [but] I’m definitely someone [that] likes when something's scary for a kid, within reason. A lot of people are like, ‘No, no, no, they can't see that.’ And it's like, ‘No, let them see it. Get scared. That's good.’ I was happy that I was scared.

I'm sure if The Secret of NIMH came out now, they would have a whole room of executives going, ‘You can't have the owl with its head upside-down, turning around and with cobwebs all over him. It's just too scary.’ But for me, it scared me to death, and then I would keep watching it, and then it became less scary, and then I started to appreciate it. I think it's good for kids to be scared.”

“That movie came out when I was a freshman in high school. I got a job on the school paper, and my first job was to write a review of all the new movies, and it was the week that that movie came out. And so I remember I went with a notebook, I was writing my thoughts down, and then I ended up writing a pretty bad [review]. I liked [the movie] a lot, but my actual writing was terrible. The editors took it and rewrote it all.

But I loved it. I love Tim Burton. I loved the music in it, specifically the song that Catherine O'Hara sings as Sally; I think it's such a moving song. And Henry Selick’s direction in it is amazing. People always think Tim Burton made that movie. He didn't. It's Henry Selick. He spent every day there with those animators making that thing work.

I love the scope of it and how grand it is, from the beginning—that ‘This Is Halloween’ song, there's something almost operatic about it. And it's its own thing. The best stuff, to me, is always when the filmmakers and the writers [are] doing a thing that they want to see, and they trust an audience will get it.

When it came out, I remember it wasn't what it is now. It was kind of this weird oddity. And then as time went on… I remember moving to LA, and it was the 10-year anniversary of it, and they had a screening someplace, and I drove past all these people dressed like the characters, they had Jack Skellington t-shirts on and all that stuff, and I went, ‘Wow, that's crazy.’ Cause I was a huge fan of it, and had the soundtrack, and I absolutely adored that movie.

[Once] I had kids, it was one of the first movies I showed them, and they watched it every Halloween and every Christmas. My youngest daughter [recently] played Sally in a school play, and she got to sing that song, and so it was full circle. I got very emotional. I was like, ‘Oh my gosh, there she is, singing that song.’ And I had become friendly with Catherine O'Hara, and she passed away, and it was really… Yeah, I was a blubbering idiot when she started singing that song.”

#6624. To Penny, or Not to Penny?

Few shoes are as essential in the modern menswear landscape as the classic leather loafer. Whether you’re dressing up or dressing down, it is the “the Swiss Army Knife of shoes,” an any-occasion solution to many footwear conundrums. But within the broader loafer genus, one particular species—the penny loafer—includes a divisive design feature: a small, coin-shaped slot in the leather strap “saddle” across the vamp of the shoe.

As the lore goes, Ivy Style pioneers and sock-hopping teens in the mid-20th century liked to store a penny in their shoes as “payphone change.” But now that payphones are a thing of the misty-eyed past and the American one-cent penny is no longer minted for circulation, is it still cool to put a penny (or any other coin, or coin-shaped object) in your penny loafers? What is the line between timeless and passé?

We invited two of GQ’s most well-heeled staffers, associate grooming and wellness editor Tyler Chin and senior style commerce writer Gerald Ortiz, to ponder the pros and cons of the penny loafer.

Mahershala Ali, an ostensible penny proponent, in London on October 19.

Tyler Chin, associate grooming and wellness editor: Sure! I’ve seen so many guys wearing loafers these days, and yet still haven’t seen any of them harking back to the shoe’s origins with a penny in them. Maybe I’m not looking hard enough or I’m not in circles where they are doing that, but it’s an inoffensive and easy way to accessorize the shoe. Plus, I had an English teacher in high school who did it, and while she wasn’t particularly cool, I did think the sartorial choice was neat.

Gerald Ortiz, senior style commerce writer: No. I feel like guys who put pennies in their loafers are either try-hards, or just learned about that particular piece of prep trivia. The only acceptable instance to wear a penny in your loafers is if you’re really about that prep/Ivy style, like Preppy Pete, or the guys who frequent Alfargo’s Market (shout out).

TC: I think the penny would be the most boring and obvious choice. If a quarter could fit in there, I think it’d be so funny if you were at a vending machine and short a few cents and settled up with the coin in your shoe. One time, the button on my pants fell off on the street and I lost it, but imagine I had a spare button in my shoe? I could reattach it right there and then! In the hypothetical I’d also need to be toting around a sewing kit, but hey, I’d love to think I could be MacGyver in that scenario.

GO: It doesn’t have to be anything at all. But if you’re going to do it, I don’t see why it couldn’t be another coin or object. It could be a Chuck E. Cheese token. Whatever you put in the slot, it would still be wack.

TC: I just got back from a trip to Tokyo, and I had a lot of leftover yen coins. Two of them—the five and 50 yen—have a hole on them, which differentiates them from your boring American coin. I’d throw one of them in my shoe and be one of those people who never stops talking about their travel abroad.

GO: I saw on Instagram someone stick a hundred dollar bill into their loafer and that was funny for a second. Then I scrolled on to the next meme.

TC: I’m pretty sure a coin that is that close to the ground will surely have a patina before the end of the day. Also, I can’t remember the last time I’ve seen a shiny new coin—who carries loose change these days anyway?!

GO: You know, it might be kind of cool to see a penny completely covered in verdigris slotted into your loafers. But maybe the coin should match the sheen of your loafers. I dunno, just don’t do it and you won’t have to think about it.

TC: Everyone I know is very familiar with my love of penny loafers. Lately I’ve been wearing them with bootcut jeans that are just a little longer than usual, so they have a nice drape over the shoe. Top-wise, I’d go with a tucked-in button up, T-shirt, or sweater. Pretty much anything that would go well with sneakers would be equally suited for wearing with loafers. Which is nearly all outfits.

GO: Penniless or not, a pair of wide khakis with a clean-to-slight break and a striped button-up is a failsafe outfit. It wouldn’t be very inventive, but it would pass muster for most conceivable situations. Vintage straight-leg jeans with good fades, a fucked-up band tee, and a sportcoat is also a great look. But the best looks are always the unexpected pairings.

TC: It’s sort of like how guys will go on and on about having functional sleeve buttons on a suit jacket. Is there any situation where you’ll actually need to unbutton those or do you just leave one or two undone to “stunt” on your unimpressed bros? If you really want to put a coin in your shoe for the sake of accessorizing your footwear, then by all means get a shoe with a functioning penny slot and use it! But if you’re doing it just so you can say “Yeah, these joints are the real deal!” and use it as an excuse to talk about the history of the shoe and why yours are superior, then I’d rather you wear lace-ups.

GO: I think it’s fine, even preferable to have a faux penny slot. It’s like how jeans still come with the watch pocket. It’s purely vestigial at this point, but would feel incomplete without it.

TC: One of these days, I’ll be telling my great-great grandchildren about the origins of the penny loafer and they’ll be confused because they would’ve never heard or seen the one-cent coin before. I’ll whip one out of my shoe and instead of being impressed they’ll chalk it up to another one of Old Man Ty’s tales from his youth, and tuck me back into the nursing home they checked me into.

GO: Lol. I guess it’ll make the penny more covetable. But it’ll be a long time before the penny becomes that desirable. That said, I love this British brand Six 95 that’s designing cool pieces using coins, so I can absolutely see more labels farming pennies for their clothes and accessories.

TC: Men will break up anything other than a toxic relationship. Loafers did nothing wrong and sometimes it’s just the person wearing them that needs to hit the curb.

GO: It makes sense. It’s like any trend, really. Even if they’re as versatile as we’ve been propping them up to be, loafers reached an inflection point where, for some guys, they’re just not the vibe anymore. But I don’t think they’ll dip so low to be thought of as cringe or as an emblem of the 2020s. They’re as classic as it gets and they’ll go through their own ebbs and flows.

TC: A quarter to increase my net worth 25-fold.

#6623. Toasted Meringue Hazelnut Blondies

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 325°. Lightly coat a 9x9" pan with nonstick vegetable oil spray. Line pan with parchment paper, leaving overhang on two sides. Toast 2 cups blanched hazelnuts on a rimmed baking sheet, tossing halfway through, until golden brown and fragrant, 15–18 minutes. Let sit until cool enough to handle, then coarsely chop; set aside.

Cook 1 cup (2 sticks) unsalted butter in a small saucepan over medium heat, stirring often, until butter foams, then browns (do not burn), 7–9 minutes. Remove from heat.

Whisk 2 large eggs, room temperature, 1 large egg yolk, room temperature, 1¼ cups (250 g) granulated sugar, ¼ cup (packed; 50 g) light brown sugar, 2 tsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract, 1½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and ¾ tsp. instant espresso powder (if using) in the bowl of a stand mixer fitted with the whisk attachment on medium-high speed until mixture is pale and doubled in volume, 6–9 minutes (mixture will form an unbroken ribbon when whisk is lifted from bowl).

Reduce speed to low and with motor running, gradually pour in brown butter in a thin, continuous stream; beat until incorporated, about 2 minutes. Remove bowl from mixer and gently fold in 1½ cups (187 g) all-purpose flour with a rubber spatula, scraping down sides and bottom of bowl, until no large, dry streaks of flour remain. Scrape batter into prepared pan; smooth surface. Top with reserved hazelnuts and press in gently to adhere (surface will be smothered with nuts).

Bake blondie until edges are golden brown and center is just set, 35–45 minutes. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let blondie cool in pan. Using parchment paper to help, lift blondie out of pan and transfer to rack.

Meanwhile, combine 3 large egg whites and 1 cup (200 g) granulated sugar in the clean bowl of stand mixer and set over a saucepan of just simmering water (make sure bottom of bowl does not touch water). Heat, whisking constantly, until sugar is dissolved and an instant-read thermometer registers 170°.

Fit bowl onto mixer fitted with clean whisk attachment. Beat egg white mixture until thick and glossy and mixture is barely lukewarm, 8–10 minutes. Add 1 tsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt; fold in with a rubber spatula just to combine. Taste meringue and season with more kosher salt if needed

Peel away parchment paper from blondie and transfer to a heatproof platter. Using an offset spatula, spread meringue evenly over blondie, covering from edge to edge. Using a kitchen torch, toast meringue until deep golden brown. Sprinkle with flaky sea salt. Using a long knife and wiping off with a damp kitchen towel between cuts, slice blondie into 9 squares.

Do Ahead: Blondie can be baked 24 hours ahead. Store, loosely covered, at room temperature.
